Enumeration and Updates for Conjunctive Linear Algebra Queries Through Expressibility
Thomas Muñoz ORCID:0000-0003-0004-5041, Cristian Riveros ORCID:0000-0003-0832-116X, Stijn Vansummeren ORCID:0000-0001-7793-9049

Abstract
. Due to the importance of linear algebra and matrix operations in data analytics, there is significant interest in using relational query optimization and processing techniques for evaluating (sparse) linear algebra programs. In particular, in recent years close connections have been established between linear algebra programs and relational algebra that allow transferring optimization techniques of the latter to the former. In this paper, we ask ourselves which linear algebra programs in MATLANG correspond to the free-connex and q-hierarchical fragments of conjunctive first-order logic. Both fragments have desirable query processing properties: free-connex conjunctive queries support constant-delay enumeration after a linear-time preprocessing phase, and q-hierarchical conjunctive queries further allow constant-time updates. By characterizing the corresponding fragments of MATLANG, we hence identify the fragments of linear algebra programs that one can evaluate with constant-delay enumeration after linear-time preprocessing and with constant-time updates. To derive our results, we improve and generalize previous correspondences between MATLANG and relational algebra evaluated over semiring-annotated relations. In addition, we identify properties on semirings that allow to generalize the complexity bounds for free-connex and q-hierarchical conjunctive queries from Boolean annotations to general semirings.



[a]
[b]
[a]

1.
Introduction

Linear algebra forms the backbone of modern data analytics, as most machine learning algorithms are coded as sequences of matrix operations
[
ABC+16
,
ELB+17
,
ASS+17
,
EBH+17
,
YHZ+17
]
. In practice, linear algebra programs operate over matrices with millions of entries. Therefore, efficient evaluation of linear algebra programs is a relevant challenge for data management systems which has attracted research attention with several proposals in the area
[
HBY13
,
KVG+19
,
WHS+20
,
JLY+20
,
LGG+18
]
.

To optimize and evaluate linear algebra programs, we must first agree on the language in which such programs are expressed. There has been a renewed interest in recent years for designing query languages for specifying linear algebra programs and for understanding their expressive power
[
SEM+20
,
BHPS20
,
BGdBW19
,
BGdB20
,
GMRV21
]
.
One such proposal is
\mathsf{MATLANG}

[
BGdBW19
]
, a formal matrix query language that consists of only the basic linear algebra operations and whose extensions (e.g.,
\mathsf{for}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
) achieve the expressive power of most linear algebra operations
[
GMRV21
]
. Although
\mathsf{MATLANG}
is a theoretical query language, it includes the core of any linear algebra program and, thus, the optimization and efficient evaluation of
\mathsf{MATLANG}
could have a crucial impact on today’s machine learning systems.

In this work, we study the efficient evaluation of
\mathsf{MATLANG}
programs over sparse matrices whose entries are taken from a general semiring. We consider
\mathsf{MATLANG}
evaluation in both the static and dynamic setting. For static evaluation, we want to identify the fragment that one can evaluate by preprocessing the input in linear time to build a data structure for enumerating the output entries with constant-delay. For dynamic evaluation, we assume that matrix entries are updated regularly and we want to maintain the output of a
\mathsf{MATLANG}
query without recomputing it. For this dynamic setting, we aim to identify the
\mathsf{MATLANG}
fragment that one can evaluate by taking linear time in the size of the update to refresh the aforementioned data structure so that it supports constant-delay enumeration of the modified output entries. These guarantees for both scenarios have become the holy grail for algorithmic query processing since, arguably, it is the best that one can achieve complexity-wise in terms of the input, the output, and the cost of an update
[
BGS20
,
BDG07
,
Bra13
,
IUV17
,
KNOZ20
,
SSV18
,
KS13
,
DG07
,
DSS14
]
.

To identify the
\mathsf{MATLANG}
fragments with these guarantees, our approach is straightforward but effective. Instead of developing evaluation algorithms from scratch, we establish a direct correspondence between linear algebra and relational algebra to take advantage of the query evaluation results for conjunctive queries. Indeed, prior work has precisely characterized which subfragments of conjunctive queries can be evaluated and updated efficiently
[
BDG07
,
BKS17
,
BGS20
,
IUV17
]
. Our main strategy, then, is to link these conjunctive query fragments to corresponding linear algebra fragments. More specifically, our contributions are as follows.

(1)

We start by understanding the deep connection between positive first-order logic (
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
) over binary relations and
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}

[
GMRV21
]
, an extension of
\mathsf{MATLANG}
. We formalize this connection by introducing schema encodings, which specify how relations simulate matrices and vice-versa, forcing a lossless relationship between both. Using this machinery, we show that
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and positive first-order logic are equally expressive over any relation, matrix, and matrix dimension (including non-rectangular matrices). Moreover, we show that conjunctive queries (CQ) coincide with
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
without matrix addition, which we call
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
. This result forms the basis for linking both settings and translating the algorithmic results from CQ to subfragments of
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
.

(2)

We propose free-connex
\mathsf{MATLANG}
(
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
) for static evaluation, which is a natural
\mathsf{MATLANG}
subfragment that we show to be equally expressive as
free-connex CQ

[
BDG07
]
, a subfragment of CQ that allows linear time preprocessing and constant-delay enumeration. To obtain our expressiveness result, we show that free-connex CQs over binary relations are equally expressive as the two-variable fragment of conjunctive
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
, a logical characterization of this class that could be of independent interest.

(3)

For the dynamic setting we introduce the language
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
, a
\mathsf{MATLANG}
fragment that we show equally expressive to
q-hierarchical CQ

[
BKS17
,
IUV17
]
, a fragment of CQ that allows constant update time and constant-delay enumeration.

(4)

Both free-connex and q-hierarchical CQ are known to characterize the class of CQs that one can evaluate efficiently on Boolean databases. We are interested, however, in evaluating
\mathsf{MATLANG}
queries on matrices featuring entries in a
general semiring
. To obtain the complexity bounds for
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
on general semirings, therefore, we show that the upper and lower bounds for free-connex and q-hierarchical CQs generalize from Boolean annotations to classes of semirings which includes most semirings used in practice, like the reals. The tight expressiveness connections established in this paper then prove that for such semirings
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
can be evaluated with the same guarantees as their CQ counterparts and that they are optimal: one cannot evaluate any other
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query outside this fragment under complexity-theoretic assumptions
[
BKS17
]
.

This article is further organized as follows. Section
2
adresses all definitions. Section
3
describes how to go from a relational setting to a linear algebra setting and viceversa. In Section
4
we show
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and positive first-order logic are equally expressive. Section
5
defines free-connex CQs and characterizes binary free-connex CQs with the conjunctive two variable fragment of positive first order logic. Afterwards,
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
is defined in Section
6
. The definition of q-hierarchical CQs is located in Section
7
and in that matter binary q-hierarchical CQs are shown to be equally expressive as hierarchical queries of the conjunctive two variable fragment of positive first order logic. Subsequently in Section
8
,
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
is defined. Later, Section
9
defines the enumeration problem and states known upper and lower bounds on the Boolean semiring for free-connex CQs. In Section
10
the upper and lower bounds are extended to general semirings for free-connex CQs which use inequality atoms. Section
11
defines the dynamic enumeration problem and known upper and lower bounds on the Boolean semiring for q-hierarchical CQs; and illustrates how to extend these bounds to general semirings and for q-hierarchical CQs which use inequality atoms. We end by presenting some conclusions and future work in Section
12
.

1.1.
New material.

Our results have previously been published in the 27th International Conference on Database Theory, ICDT 2024. This article is an extended and complete version containing full proofs of all formal statements omitted in the conference version. Moreover, several notions and proofs were revisited to improve the presentation and shorten the article. Specifically, we introduce a
prenex normal form
for
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
in Section
4
, which resulted in being especially useful for several proofs, in particular, for the proof that

\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and positive first-order logic are equally expressive.
Furthermore, these changes in the main proof required a new presentation of other results, like the lower bound statements of
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
in Section
10
and Section
11
, respectively.

1.2.
Related work.

In addition to the work that has already been cited above, the following work is relevant. Brijder et al.
[
BGdB20
]
have shown equivalence between
\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{FO}^{+}_{3}
, the 3-variable fragment of positive first order logic. By contrast, we show equivalence between
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
, and study the relationship between the free-connex and q-hierarchical fragments of
\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}
, the conjunctive fragment of positive first order logic.

Geerts et al.
[
GMRV21
]
previously established a correspondence between
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
. However, as we illustrate in Section
3.1
their correspondence is (1) restricted to square matrices, (2) asymmetric between the two settings, and (3) encodes matrix instances as databases of more than linear size, making it unsuitable to derive the complexity bounds.

Eldar et al.
[
ECK23
]
have recently also generalized complexity bounds for free-connex CQs from Boolean annotations to general semirings. Nevertheless, this generalization is with respect to
direct access
, not enumeration. In their work the focus is to compute aggregate queries, which is achieved by providing direct access to the answers of a query even if the annotated value (aggregation result) is zero. By contrast, in our setting, zero-annotated values must not be reported during the enumeration of query answers. This distinction in the treatment of zero leads to a substantial difference in the properties that a semiring must have in order to generalize the existing complexity bounds.

There are deep connections known between the treewidth and the number of variables of a conjunctive
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formula (
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}
). For example, Kolaitis and Vardi established the equivalence of boolean queries in
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{k}
, the
k
-variable fragment of
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}
, and boolean queries in
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}
of treewidth less than
k
. Because they focus on boolean queries (i.e., without free variables), this result does not imply our result that for binary queries free-connex
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}
equals
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
. Similarly, Geerts and Reutter
[
GR22
]
introduce a tensor logic
\mathsf{TL}
over binary relations and show that conjunctive expressions in this language that have treewidth
k
can be expressed in
\mathsf{TL}_{k+1}
, the
k
-variable fragment of
\mathsf{TL}
. While they do take free variables into account, we show in Appendix
E.1
that there are free-connex conjunctive queries with 2 free variables with treewidth 2 in their formalism—for which their result hence only implies expressibility in
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{3}
, not
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
as we show here.

Several proposals
[
HBY13
,
KVG+19
,
WHS+20
,
JLY+20
,
LGG+18
]
have been made regarding the efficient evaluation of linear algebra programs in the last few years. All these works focused on query optimization without formal guarantees regarding the preprocessing, updates, or enumeration in query evaluation. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work on finding subfragments of a linear algebra query language (i.e.,
\mathsf{MATLANG}
) with such efficiency guarantees.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we recall the main definitions of
\mathsf{MATLANG}
, a query language on matrices, and first order logic (FO), a query language on relations.

Semirings.

We evaluate both languages over arbitrary commutative and non-trivial semirings.
A (commutative and non-trivial)
semiring

(K,\oplus,\odot,\mymathbb{0},\mymathbb{1})
is an algebraic structure where
K
is a
non-empty set,
\oplus
and
\odot
are binary operations over
K
, and

\mymathbb{0},\mymathbb{1}\in K
with
\mymathbb{0}\not=\mymathbb{1}
.
Furthermore,
\oplus
and
\odot
are associative
operations,
\mymathbb{0}
and
\mymathbb{1}
are the identities of
\oplus
and
\odot
,
respectively,
\oplus
and
\odot
are commutative operations,
\odot

distributes over
\oplus
, and
\mymathbb{0}
annihilates
K
(i.e.

\mymathbb{0}\odot k=k\odot\mymathbb{0}=\mymathbb{0}
).
We use

\bigoplus_{L}
and
\bigodot_{L}
to denote the
\oplus
and
\odot
operation over
all elements in
L\subseteq K
, respectively. Typical examples of semirings are
the reals
(\mathbb{R},+,\times,0,1)
, the natural numbers
(\mathbb{N},+,\times,0,1)
,
and the boolean semiring
\mathbb{B}=(\{\texttt{t},\texttt{f}\},\vee,\wedge,\texttt{f},\texttt{t})
.

Henceforth, when we say “semiring” we mean “commutative and non-trivial”
semiring. We fix such an arbitrary semiring
K
throughout the document. We denote by
\mathbb{N}_{>0}
the set of non-zero natural numbers.

Matrices and size symbols.

A
K
-matrix
(or just matrix) of
dimension
m\times n
is a
m\times n
matrix with elements in
K
as its
entries.
We write
\mathbf{A}_{ij}
to denote the
(i,j)
-entry of
\mathbf{A}
. Matrices of
dimension
m\times 1
are
column vectors
and those of
dimension
1\times n
are
row vectors
. We also refer to matrices of dimension
1\times 1
as
scalars
.

We assume a collection of
size symbols
denoted with greek letters

\alpha,\beta,\dots
and assume that the natural number
1
is a valid size
symbol. A
type
is a pair
(\alpha,\beta)
of size symbols. Intuitively,
types represent sets of matrix dimensions. In particular, we obtain dimensions
from types by replacing size symbols by elements from
\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, where the size
symbol
1
is always replaced by the natural number
1
. So,
(\alpha,\beta)
with
\alpha\not=1\not=\beta

represents the set of dimensions
\{(m,n)\mid m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}\}
, while

(\alpha,\alpha)
represents the dimensions
\{(m,m)\mid m\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}\}
of
square matrices;
(\alpha,1)
represents the dimensions

\{(m,1)\mid m\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}\}
of column vectors; and
(1,1)
represents the dimension
(1,1)
of scalars.

Schemas and instances.

We assume a set
\mathcal{M}=\{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{V},\ldots\}

of
matrix symbols
, disjoint with the size symbols and denoted by bold uppercase letters. Each matrix symbol
\mathbf{A}
has a fixed associated type.
We write
\mathbf{A}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
to denote that
\mathbf{A}
has type
(\alpha,\beta)
.

A matrix
schema

\Sch
is a finite set of matrix and size symbols.
We require that the special size symbol
1
is always in
\Sch
, and that all size symbols occurring in the type of any matrix symbol
\mathbf{A}\in\Sch
are also in
\Sch
.
A matrix
instance

\mathcal{I}
over a matrix schema
\Sch
is a function that maps each size symbol
\alpha
in
\Sch
to a non-zero natural number
\alpha^{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, and maps each matrix symbol
\mathbf{A}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
in
\Sch
to a
K
-matrix
\mathbf{A}^{\mathcal{I}}
of dimension
\alpha^{\mathcal{I}}\times\beta^{\mathcal{I}}
.
We assume that for the size symbol
1
, we have
1^{\mathcal{I}}=1
, for every instance

\mathcal{I}
.

Sum-Matlang.

Let
\Sch
be a matrix schema.
Before defining the syntax of
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
, we assume a set
\mathcal{V}=\{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x},\dots\}
of
vector variables
over
\Sch
, which is disjoint with matrix and size symbols in
\Sch
.
Each such variable
\mathbf{v}
has a fixed associated type,
which must be a vector type
(\gamma,1)
for some size symbol
\gamma\in\Sch
. We also write
\mathbf{v}\colon(\gamma,1)
in that case.

The syntax of
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}

expressions
[
GMRV21
]
over
\Sch
is defined by the grammar:

e

\Coloneqq

\mathbf{A}\in\Sch

(matrix symbol)

|

\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}

(vector variable)

|

e^{T}

(transpose)

|

e_{1}\cdot e_{2}

(matrix multiplication)

|

e_{1}+e_{2}

(matrix addition)

|

e_{1}\times e_{2}

(scalar multiplication)

|

e_{1}\odot e_{2}

(pointwise multiplication)

|

\Sigma\mathbf{v}.e

(sum-iteration).

In addition, we require that expressions
e
are
well-typed
, in the sense that their
type

\texttt{type}(e)
is correctly defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\texttt{type}(\mathbf{A})\ \coloneqq&(\alpha,\beta)\ \text{ for a matrix symbol }\mathbf{A}\colon(\alpha,\beta)\\
\texttt{type}(\mathbf{v})\ \coloneqq&(\gamma,1)\ \text{ for vector variable }\mathbf{v}\colon(\gamma,1)\\
\texttt{type}(e^{T})\ \coloneqq&(\beta,\alpha)\ \text{ if }\texttt{type}(e)=(\alpha,\beta)\\
\texttt{type}(e_{1}\cdot e_{2})\ \coloneqq&(\alpha,\gamma)\ \text{ if }\texttt{type}(e_{1})=(\alpha,\beta)\text{ and }\texttt{type}(e_{2})=(\beta,\gamma)\\
\texttt{type}(e_{1}+e_{2})\ \coloneqq&(\alpha,\beta)\ \text{ if }\texttt{type}(e_{1})=\texttt{type}(e_{2})=(\alpha,\beta)\\
\texttt{type}(e_{1}\times e_{2})\ \coloneqq&(\alpha,\beta)\ \text{ if }\texttt{type}(e_{1})=(1,1)\text{ and }\texttt{type}(e_{2})=(\alpha,\beta)\\
\texttt{type}(e_{1}\odot e_{2})\ \coloneqq&(\alpha,\beta)\ \text{ if }\texttt{type}(e_{1})=\texttt{type}(e_{2})=(\alpha,\beta)\\
\texttt{type}(\Sigma\mathbf{v}.e)\ \coloneqq&(\alpha,\beta)\ \text{ if }e\colon(\alpha,\beta)\text{ and }\texttt{type}(\mathbf{v})=(\gamma,1).\end{array}

In what follows, we always consider well-typed expressions and write
e\colon(\alpha,\beta)
to denote that
e
is well typed, and its type is
(\alpha,\beta)
.

For an expression
e
, we say that a vector variable
\mathbf{v}
is
bound
if it is under a sum-iteration
\Sigma\mathbf{v}
, and
free
otherwise.
We say that an expression
e
is a
sentence
if
e
does not have free vector variables.
To evaluate expressions that are not sentences, we require the following notion of a
valuation
.
Fix a matrix instance
\mathcal{I}
over
\Sch
. A
vector valuation
over
\mathcal{I}
is a
function
\mu
that maps each vector symbol
\mathbf{v}\colon(\gamma,1)
to a column
vector of dimension
\gamma^{\mathcal{I}}\times 1
. Further, if
\mathbf{b}
is a vector of dimension

\gamma^{\mathcal{I}}\times 1
, then let
\mu[\mathbf{v}:=\mathbf{b}]
denote the
extended

vector valuation over
\mathcal{I}
that coincides with
\mu
, except that

\mathbf{v}\colon(\gamma,1)
is mapped to
\mathbf{b}
.

Let
e\colon(\alpha,\beta)
be a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression over
\Sch
.
When one evaluates
e
over a matrix instance
\mathcal{I}
and a matrix valuation
\mu
over
\mathcal{I}
, it produces a matrix
\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)
of dimension
\alpha^{\mathcal{I}}\times\beta^{\mathcal{I}}
such that each entry
i,j
satisfies:

\begin{array}[]{rlrl}\llbracket\mathbf{A}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ij}\ \coloneqq&\mathbf{A}^{\mathcal{I}}_{ij}\,\text{ for }\mathbf{A}\in\Sch\\
\llbracket\mathbf{v}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ij}\ \coloneqq&\mu(\mathbf{v})_{ij}\,\text{ for }\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}\\
\llbracket e^{T}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ij}\ \coloneqq&\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ji}\\
\llbracket e_{1}\cdot e_{2}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ij}\ \coloneqq&\lx@intercol\bigoplus_{k}\llbracket e_{1}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ik}\odot\llbracket e_{2}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{kj}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\llbracket e_{1}+e_{2}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ij}\ \coloneqq&\llbracket e_{1}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ij}\oplus\llbracket e_{2}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ij}\\
\llbracket e_{1}\times e_{2}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ij}\ \coloneqq&\lx@intercol a\odot\llbracket e_{2}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ij}\text{ with }\llbracket e_{1}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)=[a]\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\llbracket e_{1}\odot e_{2}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ij}\ \coloneqq&\llbracket e_{1}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ij}\odot\llbracket e_{2}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ij}\\
\llbracket\Sigma\mathbf{v}.\,e\,\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{ij}\ \coloneqq&\bigoplus_{k=1}^{\gamma^{\mathcal{I}}}\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu[\mathbf{v}\coloneqq\mathbf{b}^{\gamma^{\mathcal{I}}}_{k}])_{ij}\end{array}

In the last line, it is assumed that
\mathbf{v}\colon(\gamma,1)
, and
\mathbf{b}^{n}_{1},\mathbf{b}^{n}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{b}^{n}_{n}
denote the
n
-dimensional canonical vectors, namely, the vectors
[\mymathbb{1}\,\mymathbb{0}\,\ldots\,\mymathbb{0}]^{T}
,
[\mymathbb{0}\,\mymathbb{1}\,\ldots\,\mymathbb{0}]^{T}
​,
\ldots
,
[\mymathbb{0}\,\,\mymathbb{0}\,\ldots\,\mymathbb{1}]^{T}
, respectively.

Note that if
e
is a sentence, then
\llbracket e\rrbracket
is independent of the vector valuation
\mu
in the sense that
\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)=\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu^{\prime})
for all vector valuations
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
. We therefore sometimes also simply write
\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})
instead of
\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)
when
e
is a sentence.

Fragments of Sum-Matlang.

We recall here two fragments of
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
that will be important for the paper. First, we define
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
as the
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
fragment that includes all operations except matrix addition (
+
).
Second, we define
\mathsf{MATLANG}
that is a fragment of
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
.
Specifically, define the syntax of
\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions over
\Sch
by the
following grammar:

e

\Coloneqq

\mathbf{A}\in\Sch\,\mid\,e^{T}\,\mid\,e_{1}\cdot e_{2}\,\mid\,e_{1}+e_{2}\,\mid\,e_{1}\times e_{2}\,\mid\,e_{1}\odot e_{2}\,\mid\,\mathbf{1}^{\alpha}\,\mid\,\mathbf{I}^{\alpha}

for every size symbol
\alpha\in\Sch
.
Here,
\mathbf{1}^{\alpha}
and
\mathbf{I}^{\alpha}
denote the
ones-vector
and
identity-matrix
, respectively, of type

\texttt{type}(\mathbf{1}^{\alpha})=(\alpha,1)
and
\texttt{type}(\mathbf{I}^{\alpha})=(\alpha,\alpha)
. Their semantics can be defined by using
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
as:

\begin{array}[]{rlrl}\llbracket\mathbf{1}^{\alpha}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)\coloneqq&\llbracket\Sigma\mathbf{v}.\mathbf{v}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu),&\llbracket\mathbf{I}^{\alpha}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)\coloneqq&\llbracket\Sigma\mathbf{v}.\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{T}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu).\end{array}

Note that the original
\mathsf{MATLANG}
language as introduced in
[
BGdBW19
]
included an operator for
vector diagonalization
. This operator can be simulated using the
\mathbf{I}^{\alpha}
-operator, and conversely
\mathbf{I}^{\alpha}
can be simulated using vector diagonalization.
Furthermore, we have included the pointwise multiplication
\odot
in
\mathsf{MATLANG}
, also known as the
Hadamard product
. This
operation will be essential for our characterization
results. In
[
BGdBW19
,
GMRV21
]
, the syntax of
\mathsf{MATLANG}
was more generally parameterized by a family of
n
-ary
functions that could be pointwise applied. Similarly to

[
BGdB20
,
Gee19
]
we do not include such functions
here, but leave their detailed study to future
work.

Sum-Matlang queries.

A
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}

query

\mathbf{Q}
over a matrix schema
\Sch
is an expression of the form
\mathbf{H}\coloneqq e

where
e
is a well-typed
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
sentence,
\mathbf{H}
is a “fresh” matrix symbol that does not occur in
\Sch
, and
\texttt{type}(\mathbf{H})=\texttt{type}(e)
.
When evaluated on a matrix instance
\mathcal{I}
over schema
\Sch
,

\mathbf{Q}
returns a matrix instance
\mathcal{E}
over the extended schema
\Sch\cup\{\mathbf{H}\}
:
\mathcal{E}
coincides with
\mathcal{I}
for every matrix and size symbol in
\Sch
and additionally maps

\mathbf{H}^{\mathcal{E}}=\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\emptyset)
with
\emptyset
denoting the empty vector valuation.
We denote the instance resulting from evaluating
\mathbf{Q}
by
\llbracket\mathbf{Q}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})
. If
\Sch
is a matrix schema and
\mathbf{Q}
a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query over
\Sch
then we use
\Sch\!\left(\mathbf{Q}\right)
to denote the extended schema
\Sch\cup\{\mathbf{H}\}
.

The former notion of query is extended to any fragment of
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
in a natural manner, e.g., a
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query is a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\mathbf{H}\coloneqq e
where
e
is a well-typed
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression.

K
-relations.

A
K
-relation

over a domain of data values
\mathbb{D}
is a function
f\colon\mathbb{D}^{a}\to K

such that
f(\overline{d})\not=\mymathbb{0}
for finitely many
\overline{d}\in\mathbb{D}^{a}
. Here, ‘
a
’ is the
arity
of
R
.
Since we want to compare relational queries with
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
queries, we will restrict our attention in what follows to
K
-relations where
the domain
\mathbb{D}
of data values is the set
\mathbb{N}_{>0}
. In this context, we may naturally view a
K
-matrix of
dimensions
n\times m
as a
K
-relation such that the entry
(i,j)
of the matrix is
encoded by the
K
-value of the tuple
(i,j)
in the relation (see also
Section
3
).

Vocabularies and databases.

We assume an infinite set of
relation symbols
together with an infinite and disjoint set of
constant symbols
.
Every relation symbol
R
is associated with a
number, its
arity
, which we denote by
\arity(R)\in\mathbb{N}
.
A
vocabulary

\Voc
is a finite set of relation and constant symbols.
A
database
over
\Voc
is a function
db
that maps every constant symbol
c\in\Voc
to a value
c^{\textit{db}}
in
\mathbb{N}_{>0}
; and every relation symbol
R\in\Voc
to a
K
-relation
R^{\textit{db}}
of arity
\arity(R)
.

Positive first order logic.

As our relational query language, we will work
with the positive fragment of first order logic (
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
). In contrast to the
standard setting in database theory, where the only atomic formulas are
relational atoms of the form
R(\overline{x})
, we also allow the ability to compare
variables with constant symbols. To this end, the following definitions are in
order. We assume an infinite set of variables, which we usually denote by

x,y,z
. We denote tuples of variables by
\overline{x}
,
\overline{y}
, and so
on. A
relational atom
is an expression of the form
R(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
with

R
a relation symbol of arity
k
. A

comparison atom
is
of the form
x\leq c
with
x
a
variable and
c
a constant symbol. An
equality atom
is of the form
x=y
with
x,y
variables.
A
positive first order logic formula
(
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formula) over a vocabulary
\Voc
is an expression
generated by the following grammar:

\varphi

\Coloneqq

R(\overline{x})\,\mid\,x\leq c\,\mid\,x=y\,\mid\,\exists y.\,\varphi\,\mid\,\varphi\wedge\varphi\,\mid\,\varphi\vee\varphi

where
R
and
c
range over relations and constants in
\Voc
, respectively. The notions of
free and bound variables
are defined as usual in FO. We denote the set of free variables of
\varphi
by
\free(\varphi)
and the multiset of atoms occurring in
\varphi
by
\atoms(\varphi)
.

We evaluate formulas over
K
-relations as follows using the well-known semiring semantics
[
GKT07
]
.
A
valuation
over a set of variables
X
is a function
\nu\colon X\to\mathbb{N}_{>0}
that assigns a
value in
\mathbb{N}_{>0}
to each variable in
X
(recall that
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{N}_{>0}
).
We denote by
\nu\colon X
that
\nu
is a valuation on
X
and by
{\nu}|_{Y}
the
restriction of
\nu
to
X\cap Y
.
As usual, valuations are extended point-wise to tuples of variables, i.e.,

\nu(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\left(\nu(x_{1}),\dots,\nu(x_{n})\right)
.
Let
\varphi
be an
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formula over vocabulary
\Voc
. When evaluated on a database
db
over vocabulary
\Voc
, it defines a mapping
\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}
from valuations over
\free(\varphi)
to
K
inductively defined as:

\begin{array}[]{rlrl}\llceil{R(\overline{x})}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)\ \coloneqq&R^{\textit{db}}(\nu(\overline{x}))\\
\llceil{x\leq c}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)\ \coloneqq&\begin{cases}\mymathbb{1}&\text{if }\nu(x)\leq c^{\textit{db}}\\
\mymathbb{0}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\\
\llceil{x=y}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)\ \coloneqq&\begin{cases}\mymathbb{1}&\text{ if }\nu(x)=\nu(y)\\
\mymathbb{0}&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}\\
\llceil{\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)\ \coloneqq&\llceil{\varphi_{1}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}({\nu}|_{\free(\varphi_{1})})\odot\llceil{\varphi_{2}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}({\nu}|_{\free(\varphi_{2})})\\
\llceil{\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)\ \coloneqq&\llceil{\varphi_{1}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}({\nu}|_{\free(\varphi_{1})})\oplus\llceil{\varphi_{2}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}({\nu}|_{\free(\varphi_{2})})\\
\llceil{\exists y.\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)\ \coloneqq&\bigoplus_{\mu\colon\free(\varphi)\text{s.t.}{\mu}|_{\free(\varphi)\setminus\{y\}}=\nu}\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\mu).\end{array}

The
support
of the mapping
\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}
is the set of
valuations that it maps to a nonzero annotation. As the reader may notice, it
is possible that
\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}
has infinite support. This situation is
remeniscent of classical
\mathsf{FO}
(evaluated on the Boolean semiring) which may also
define infinite relations. To circumvent this problem, we restrict ourselves to

safe
formulas
[
AHV95
]
, which in classical FO
ensures that output relations are finite. To define safety, we first define the set
\rr(\varphi)\subseteq\free(\varphi)
of
range restricted
variables of a formula:

\displaystyle\rr(R(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}))

\displaystyle=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}

\displaystyle\rr(x\leq c)

\displaystyle=\{x\}

\displaystyle\rr(x=y)

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\rr(\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle=\rr(\varphi_{1})\cup\rr(\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle\rr(\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle=\rr(\varphi_{1})\cap\rr(\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle\rr(\exists y.\varphi)

\displaystyle=\rr(\varphi_{1})\setminus\{y\}.

An
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formula
\varphi
is
safe

if it satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

every occurrence of an equality atom
x=y
is a part of a subformula of

\varphi
of the form
\psi\wedge(x=y)
or
(x=y)\wedge\psi
where at
least one of
\{x,y\}
is in
\rr(\psi)
;

(2)

\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
is only used when
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}

have the same set of free variables.

Proposition 1
.

Every safe
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formula has finite support.

The proof is remeniscent of that of classical
\mathsf{FO}
; we therefore delegate it to Appendix
A
. For the rest of this paper, we restrict to safe formulas. Hence when we say “formula” we mean “safe formula”, and similarly for queries.

FO queries.

An
\mathsf{FO}^{+}

query

Q
over vocabulary
\Voc
is an expression of the form
H(\overline{x})\leftarrow\varphi
where

\varphi
is an
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formula over
\Voc
,

\overline{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
is a sequence of (not necessarily distinct) free
variables of
\varphi
, such that every free variable of
\varphi

occurs in
\overline{x}
, and
H
is a “fresh” relation
symbol not in
\Voc
with
\arity(H)=k
.
The formula
\varphi
is called the
body
of
Q
, and
H(\overline{x})
its
head
.

When evaluated over a database
db
over
\Voc
,
Q
returns a database

\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\textit{db}}
over the extended vocabulary
\Voc\cup\{H\}
.
This database
\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\textit{db}}
coincides with
db
for every relation and constant symbol in
\Voc
,
and maps the relation symbol
H
to the
K
-relation of arity
k
defined as follows.
For a sequence of domain values
\overline{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{k})
, we write
\overline{d}\models\overline{x}
if,
for all
i\not=j
with
x_{i}=x_{j}
we also have
d_{i}=d_{j}
. Clearly, if

\overline{d}\models\overline{x}
then the mapping

\{x_{1}\to d_{1},\dots,x_{k}\mapsto d_{k}\}
is well-defined. Denote this mapping
by
\overline{x}\mapsto\overline{d}
in this case. Then

\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\textit{db}}(H)\coloneqq\overline{d}\mapsto\begin{cases}\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\overline{x}\mapsto\overline{d})&\text{if }\overline{d}\models\overline{x}\\
\mymathbb{0}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

In what follows, if
Q
is a query, then we will often use the notation

Q(\overline{x})
to denote that the sequence of the variables in the head of
Q
is

\overline{x}
. If
Q
is a query over
\Voc
and
H(\overline{x})
its head, then we write
\Voc(Q)
for the extended vocabulary
\Voc\cup\{H\}
.

Formula and query equivalence

Two
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formulas
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
are said to be

equivalent
, denoted
\varphi_{1}\equiv\varphi_{2}
if

\llceil{\varphi_{1}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}=\llceil{\varphi_{2}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}
for every database. Note that
because
\llceil{\varphi_{1}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}
is a set of mappings, all with domain

\free(\varphi_{1})
, and because the same holds for
\llceil{\varphi_{2}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}
, we
neccesarily have
\free(\varphi_{1})=\free(\varphi_{2})
when

\varphi_{1}\equiv\varphi_{2}
. Similarly, two
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
queries
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are equivalent, denoted
Q_{1}\equiv Q_{2}
if
\llbracket Q_{1}\rrbracket_{\textit{db}}=\llbracket Q_{2}\rrbracket_{\textit{db}}
for every database. This implies that they have the same head but the variables occurring in the heads need not be the same.

Note that because we have fixed
\mathcal{K}
arbitrarily, formula and query equivalence cannot use the concrete interpretation of the operators in
\mathcal{K}
operators, and must hence hold for every choice of
\mathcal{K}
.

Fragments of
\mathsf{FO}^{+}

We denote by
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}
the fragment of
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formulas in which disjunction is disallowed. A query
Q=H(\overline{x})\leftarrow\varphi
is an
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}
query if
\varphi
is in
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}
. If additionally
\varphi
is in prenex normal form, i.e.,

Q:H(\overline{x})\leftarrow\exists\overline{y}.a_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge a_{n}

where
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
are relational, comparison, or equality atoms,
then
Q
is a
conjunctive query
(CQ). Note that, while the classes of conjunctive queries and
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}
queries are equally expressive, for our purposes conjunctive queries are hence formally a syntactic fragment of
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}
queries.

An
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formula is binary if every relational atom occurring in it has arity at most two and it has at most two free variables. Similarly, an
\mathsf{FO}^{+}

query is
binary
if every relational atom occurring in it (body and head)
has arity at most two. Because in
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
both the input and output are matrices,
our correspondences between
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
will focus on binary queries.

Discussion.

We have added comparison atoms to
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
in order to establish its correspondence with
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
. To illustrate why we will need comparison atoms, consider the
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression
\mathbf{I}^{\alpha}
of type
(\alpha,\alpha)
that computes
the identity matrix. This can be expressed by means of the following CQ
Q:I(x,x)\leftarrow x\leq\alpha
. We hence use
comparison atoms to align the dimension of the matrices with the domain size of
relations.

To make the correspondence hold, we note that in
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
there is a special size symbol,
1
,
which is always interpreted as the constant
1\in\mathbb{N}
.
This size symbol is used in particular to represent column and row vectors,
which have type
(\alpha,1)
and
(1,\alpha)
respectively.
We endow CQs with the same property in the sense that we will assume in what follows that
1
is a valid constant symbol and that
1^{\textit{db}}=1
for every database
db
.

3.
From matrices to relations and back

In this section, we lay the grounds to revisit and generalize the connection between
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
. Towards this goal, we introduce next all the formal machinery necessary to provide translations between the two query languages that work for any matrix schema, are symmetric, and ensure that matrices are encoded as databases of linear size. These translations are specified in Section
4
. We start by discussing why we need to revisit this formal machinery to then continue presenting the formal details.

3.1.
The need to revisit the characterization of Sum-Matlang

In
[
GMRV21
]
, the authors already propose a way of translating between
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
. Next, we summarize how the translations that we propose in this paper improve over those of
[
GMRV21
]
, justifying why we need to revisit this characterization.

In
[
GMRV21
]
, the translation from
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
to
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
goes as follows. It is assumed that on the
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
side there is a unary relation
R_{\alpha}
for each size symbol
\alpha
and that, in the database
db
simulating the
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
instance
\mathcal{I}
we have
R^{\textit{db}}_{\alpha}(i)=1
for all
1\leq i\leq\alpha^{\mathcal{I}}
. Note that, consequently, the size of
db
is larger than that of
\mathcal{I}
: it take unit space to store
\alpha^{\mathcal{I}}
in
\mathcal{I}
while it takes
\alpha^{\mathcal{I}}
space in
db
. Therefore,
\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel
(the size of
db
) is certainly not linear in the size of
\mathcal{I}
. Since we are interested in evaluating
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions with linear time preprocessing by reducing to the relational case, this encoding of matrix instances as databases is hence unsuitable. We resolve this in our work by adding inequality atoms to
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
, and adopting a representation of matrix instances by databases that is linear in size.

Conversely, in
[
GMRV21
]
, the translation from
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
to
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
only works when the input relations to the
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query represent square matrices of type
(\alpha,\alpha)
, or vectors with type
(\alpha,1)
or
(1,\alpha)
, for a fixed single size symbol
\alpha
such that
\alpha^{\textit{db}}=n
where
\{d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\}
is the
active domain
of
db
. This assumption implies that if one translates a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query into
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
, optimizes the
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query, and then translates back, one obtains a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query whose input schema is different than that of the original query. This phenomemon is illustrated by means of the scenario below. By contrast, we establish a more general correspondence, parametrized by schema encodings, that preserves the original input schema in such a round-trip translation.

Consider for example the schema
\Sch=\{\mathbf{A}\}
with
\mathbf{A}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
. Let the instance
\mathcal{I}
over
\Sch
be such that
\alpha^{\mathcal{I}}=3
,
\beta^{\mathcal{I}}=2
, and:

\mathbf{A}^{\mathcal{I}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}.

Let
\mathbf{Q}
be a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query over
\Sch
to be evaluated on
\mathcal{I}
. In
[
GMRV21
]
the corresponding relational schema is
\{A,R_{1}^{\alpha},R_{2}^{\beta}\}
. The instance
db
is such that
A^{\textit{db}}(1,1)=1
,
A^{\textit{db}}(3,2)=1
and
0
for any other tuple. Also,
(R_{1}^{\alpha})^{\textit{db}}(i)=1
for all
i\leq\alpha^{\mathcal{I}}=3
and
(R_{2}^{\beta})^{\textit{db}}(i)=1
for all
i\leq\beta^{\mathcal{I}}=2
. Then, if we translate this relational setting back to a matrix schema and instance, the result is
\Sch^{\prime}=\{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{R}_{1},\mathbf{R}_{2}\}
with
\mathbf{A}\colon(\alpha,\alpha),\mathbf{R}_{1}\colon(\alpha,1)
and
\mathbf{R}_{2}\colon(\alpha,1)
. The instance
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
over
\Sch^{\prime}
is such that
\alpha^{\mathcal{I}}=3
(because of the active domain), and:

\mathbf{A}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\ =\ \begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{bmatrix}.

Additionally, we have
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
over
\Sch^{\prime}
and that can be evaluated on instance
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Note that
\llbracket\mathbf{Q}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{I}}=\llbracket\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
does not hold in general, even for the expression
\mathbf{A}
since
\mathbf{A}^{\mathcal{I}}\neq\mathbf{A}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

Given the previous discussion, in the following we determine precisely in what sense relations can encode matrices, or matrices can represent relations, and how this correspondence transfers to queries. Then, in Section
4
we show how to generalize the expressibility results in
[
GMRV21
]
for any matrix sizes and every encoding between schemas.

3.2.
How we relate objects.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a matrix of dimension
m\times n
. There exist multiple natural ways to encode
\mathbf{A}
as a relation, depending on the dimension
m\times n
.

•

We can always encode
\mathbf{A}
, whatever the values of
m
and
n
(i.e.,
\mathbf{A}
could be a matrix, a column or row vector, or a scalar), as the
binary
K
-relation
R
such that (1)
\mathbf{A}_{i,j}=R(i,j)
for every

i\leq m
,
j\leq n
and (2)
R(i,j)=\mymathbb{0}
if
i>m
or
j>n
.

•

If
\mathbf{A}
is a column vector (
n=1
) then we can also encode it as the
unary
K
-relation
R
such that
\mathbf{A}_{i,1}=R(i)
for every
i\leq m
and
R(i)=\mymathbb{0}
if
i>m
.

•

Similarly, if
\mathbf{A}
is a row vector (
m=1
) then we can encode it as the
unary
K
-relation
R
with
\mathbf{A}_{1,j}=R(j)
for every
j\leq n
and

R(i)=\mymathbb{0}
if
j>n
.

•

If
\mathbf{A}
is a scalar (
m=n=1
), we can encode it as a nullary
K
-relation
R
with
\mathbf{A}_{1,1}=R()
.

Note that if
\mathbf{A}
is scalar then we could hence encode it by means of a binary relation, a unary relation, or a nullary relation; and if it is a vector we can encode it by a binary or unary relation. In what follows, we write
\mathbf{A}\simeq R
to denote that
R
encodes
\mathbf{A}
following any of the alternatives above.

Conversely, given a (nullary, unary, or binary)
K
-relation
R
we may
interpret this as a matrix of appropriate dimension. Specifically, we say that
relation
R
is
consistent
with dimension
m\times n
if there exists a
matrix
\mathbf{A}
of dimension
m\times n
such that
\mathbf{A}\simeq R
. This is
equivalent to requiring that relation is
\mymathbb{0}
on entries outside of

m\times n
. Note that, given
R
that is consistent with

m\times n
there is exactly one matrix
\mathbf{A}\colon m\times n
such that

\mathbf{A}\simeq R
.

3.3.
How we relate schemas.

A
matrix-to-relational schema encoding
from a matrix schema
\Sch
to a relational vocabulary
\Voc
is a function
\Rel\colon\Sch\to\Voc
that maps every
matrix symbol
\mathbf{A}
in
\Sch
to a unary or binary relation symbol
\Rel(\mathbf{A})
in
\Voc
, and every size symbol
\alpha
in
\Sch
to a constant symbol
\Rel(\alpha)
in
\Voc
. Here,

\Rel(\mathbf{A})
can be unary only if
\mathbf{A}
is of vector type, and nullary only if
\mathbf{A}
is of scalar type. Intuitively,
\Rel

specifies which relation symbols will be used to store the encodings of which
matrix symbols. In addition, we require that
\Rel(1)=1
and that
\Rel
is a bijection between
\Sch
and
\sigma
.
This makes sure that we can always invert
\Rel
. In what follows, we will only specify that
\Rel
is a matrix-to-relational schema
encoding
on
\Sch
, leaving the vocabulary
\Voc
unspecified. In that
case, we write
\Rel(\Sch)
for the relational vocabulary
\Voc
.

Conversely, we define a
relational-to-matrix schema encoding
from
\Voc

into
\Sch
as a function
\Mat\colon\Voc\to\Sch
that maps every relation
symbol
R
to a matrix symbol
\Mat(R)
and every constant symbol
c
to a size
symbol
\Mat(c)
. We require that all unary relations are mapped to matrix
symbols of vector type, either row or column, and that all nullary relations are mapped to matrix symbols of scalar type. Furthermore,
\Mat
must map
1\mapsto 1
and be bijective. Similarly, we denote by

\Mat(\Voc)
the matrix schema
\Sch
mapped by
\Mat
.

Note that the bijection assumption over
\Mat
imposes some requirements over
\Voc
to encode it as matrices. For example,
\Mat
requires the existence of at least one constant symbol in
\Voc
for encoding matrices dimensions, since every matrix symbol has at least one size symbol in its type, and that size symbol is by definition in
\Sch
. The bijection between constant and size symbols is necessary in order to have lossless encoding between both settings.

Given that
\Rel
and
\Mat
are bijections between
\Sch
and
\Voc
, their inverses
\Rel^{-1}
and
\Mat^{-1}
are well defined. Furthermore, by definition we have that
\Rel^{-1}
and
\Mat^{-1}
are relational-to-matrix and matrix-to-relational schema encodings, respectively.

3.4.
How we relate instances.

We start by specifying how to encode matrix instances as database instances. Fix a matrix-to-relational schema encoding
\Rel
between
\Sch
and
\Rel(\Sch)
.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be a matrix instance over
\Sch
and
db
a database over
\Rel(\Sch)
. We say that
db
is a relational encoding of
\mathcal{I}
w.r.t.
\Rel
, denoted by
\mathcal{I}\simeq_{\Rel}\textit{db}
, if

•

\mathbf{A}^{\mathcal{I}}\simeq\Rel(\mathbf{A})^{\textit{db}}
for every matrix symbol
\mathbf{A}
in
\Sch
, and

•

\Rel(\alpha)^{\textit{db}}=\alpha^{\mathcal{I}}
for every size symbol
\alpha
in
\Sch
.

Note that, given
\mathcal{I}
and
\Rel
, the relational encoding
db
is uniquely defined. As
such, we also denote this database by
\Rel(\mathcal{I})
.

We now focus on interpreting database instances as matrix instances, which is more subtle.
Fix a relational-to-matrix schema encoding
\Mat
from
\Voc
to
\Mat(\Voc)
. We need to first leverage the consistency requirement from relations to databases. Formally, we say that a database
db
over
\Voc
is
consistent with

\Mat
if for every relation symbol
R
in
\Voc
,
R^{\textit{db}}
is consistent with
dimension
c^{\textit{db}}\times d^{\textit{db}}
where
\Mat(R)\colon(\Mat(c),\Mat(d))
.
In other words, a consistent database specifies the value of each dimension, and the relations are themselves consistent with them.

Let
db
be a database over
\Voc
, consistent with
\Mat
and let
\mathcal{I}
be a matrix instance of
\Mat(\Voc)
.
We say that
\mathcal{I}
is a matrix encoding of
db
w.r.t.
\Mat
, denoted
\textit{db}\simeq_{\Mat}\mathcal{I}
, if

•

\Mat(R)^{\mathcal{I}}\simeq R^{\textit{db}}
for every relation symbol
R\in\Voc
; and

•

c^{\textit{db}}=\Mat(c)^{\mathcal{I}}
for every constant symbol
c\in\Voc
.

Given
\Mat
and a consistent database
db
, the matrix encoding
\mathcal{I}

is uniquely defined. As such, we also denote this instance by
\Mat(\textit{db})
.

From the previous definitions, one notes an asymmetry between both directions. Although an encoding always holds from matrices to relations, we require that the relations are consistent with the sizes (i.e., constants) from relations to matrices. Nevertheless, this asymmetry does not impose a problem when we want to go back and forth, as the next result shows.

Proposition 2
.

Let
\Rel
and
\Mat
be matrix-to-relational and relational-to-matrix schema encodings from
\Sch
to
\Voc
and from
\Voc
to
\Sch
, respectively, such that
\Mat=\Rel^{-1}
. Then

•

\Rel^{-1}(\Rel(\Sch))=\Sch
and
\Mat^{-1}(\Mat(\Voc))=\Voc
;

•

\Rel(\mathcal{I})
is consistent with
\Rel^{-1}
, for every instance
\mathcal{I}
over
\Sch
;

•

\Rel^{-1}(\Rel(\mathcal{I}))=\mathcal{I}
, for every instance
\mathcal{I}
over
\Sch
; and

•

\Mat^{-1}(\Mat(\textit{db}))=\textit{db}
, for every
db
consistent with
\Mat
.

The previous proposition is a direct consequence of the definitions; however, it shows that the consistency requirement and schema encodings provide a lossless encoding between the relational and matrix settings. This fact is crucial to formalize the expressiveness equivalence between
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
, and their subfragments in the following sections.

4.
Sum-matlang and positive FO

The previous section has established the formal basis for comparing
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
. In this section, we give our first expressibility results, proving that
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
are equally expressive in some precise sense.
Furthermore, we show that its fragments
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and binary conjunctive queries (CQs) are also equally expressive. These results will be our starting point to find fragments of
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
that captures fragments of
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
with good algorithmic properties, like free-connex and q-hierarchical CQs.

4.1.
From Sum-Matlang to positive FO

We first aim to simulate every
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query with an
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query w.r.t. some matrix-to-relational schema encoding.
In what follows, fix a matrix schema
\Sch
. Let
\mathbf{Q}
be a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query over
\Sch
, and
\Rel
be a matrix-to-relational schema encoding on
\Sch\!\left(\mathbf{Q}\right)
. We say that
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query
Q

simulates

\mathbf{Q}
w.r.t.
\Rel
if
\Rel(\llbracket\mathbf{Q}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I}))=\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}
for every matrix instance
\mathcal{I}
over
\Sch
. Note that the definition implies that the output matrix symbol of
\mathbf{Q}
must be mapped to the output relation symbol of
Q
by
\Rel
, since
\Rel
is a bijection and the condition must hold for every matrix instance. Indeed, it is equivalent to
\llbracket\mathbf{Q}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})=\Rel^{-1}(\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})})
, namely, that one can evaluate
\mathbf{Q}
by first evaluating
\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}
and then mapping the results back.

Our main goal will be to prove that one can simulate every
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query in the relational setting by some
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query.
For the sake of presentation, we will take a detour by first showing that we can simulate every
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query by some CQ to then leverage this result to
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
. The first step towards the proof is to note that
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions have a prenex normal form as follows. We say that a
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression
e\colon(\alpha,\beta)
is in
prenex normal form
if it has the
form:

e\ \coloneqq\ \Sigma\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}.\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbf{s}\times\big(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{T}\big)

such that
\mathbf{x}\colon(\alpha,1)
,
\mathbf{y}\colon(\beta,1)
, and
\mathbf{s}
is an expression satisfying the following grammar:

\mathbf{s}\coloneqq\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{w}\mid\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{w}\mid\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{u}\mid\mathbf{s}\times\mathbf{s}

where
\mathbf{A}\in\Sch
and
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}
range over
\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}\}
and
\mathbf{u}
ranges over the free vector variables of
e
.

Lemma 3
.

Every
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression can be expressed in prenex normal form.

The proof of Lemma
3
is by direct induction on the expression syntax; the interested reader may find it in Appendix
B
.
By using the prenex normal form of
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
, we can proof the simulation of
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
queries by CQs.

Proposition 4
.

For every
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\mathbf{Q}
over
\Sch
and every matrix-to-relational schema encoding
\Rel
on
\Sch\!\left(\mathbf{Q}\right)
, there exists a CQ
Q
that simulates
\mathbf{Q}
w.r.t.
\Rel
.

Proof 4.1
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{H}\coloneqq e
be a
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query such that

e\colon(\alpha,\beta)
. By definition of queries,
e
is a sentence. Then by
Lemma
3
we may assume that

e=\Sigma\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}.\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbf{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbf{s}_{n}\times\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{T}
where
\mathbf{s}_{i}\coloneqq\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{w}\mid\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{w}
with

\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}\}
.

Let
x,y,v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}
be
\mathsf{FO}
variables. In what follows, the variables
v,w\in\{x,y,v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
are selected respectively for
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}\}
:
x
is used when
\mathbf{x}
appears,
y
is used when
\mathbf{y}
appears, and so on. Additionally, let us denote
\Rel(\mathbf{A})
simply by means of
A
(in non-bold math font), for every matrix symbol
\mathbf{A}
, and let us denote the constant symbol
\Rel(\alpha)
simply by
\alpha
, for every size symbol
\alpha
(recall that
\Rel(1)=1
by definition).

We now build a CQ
Q
that simulates
\mathbf{Q}
w.r.t.
\Rel
. For each
1\leq i\leq n
:

•

If
\mathbf{s}_{i}\coloneqq\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{w}
define:

–

a_{i}\coloneqq A(v,w)
if
A
is binary.

–

a_{i}\coloneqq A(v)\wedge w\leq 1
if
A
is unary and
\mathbf{A}\colon(\alpha,1)
.

–

a_{i}\coloneqq v\leq 1\wedge A(w)
if
A
is unary and
\mathbf{A}\colon(1,\alpha)
.

–

a_{i}\coloneqq v\leq 1\wedge w\leq 1\wedge A()
if
A
is nullary.

•

If
\mathbf{s}_{i}\coloneqq\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{w}
with
\mathbf{v}\colon(\gamma,1)
and
\mathbf{w}\colon(\gamma,1)
define:

–

a_{i}\coloneqq v\leq\gamma
if
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{w}
.

–

a_{i}\coloneqq v\leq\gamma\wedge w\leq\gamma\wedge v=w
if
\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{w}
.

Finally, define
\varphi\coloneqq\exists v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}.a_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge a_{n}\wedge x\leq\alpha\wedge y\leq\beta
. Note that this formula is in prenex normal form. Then the CQ
Q\colon H(x,y)\leftarrow\varphi
simulates
\mathbf{Q}
if
\Rel(\mathbf{H})
is binary and
\mathbf{H}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
; the CQ
Q\colon H(x)\leftarrow\exists y.\varphi
simulates
\mathbf{Q}
if
\Rel(\mathbf{H})
is unary and
\mathbf{H}\colon(\alpha,1)
; the CQ
Q\colon H(y)\leftarrow\exists x.\varphi
simulates
\mathbf{Q}
if
\Rel(\mathbf{H})
is unary and
\mathbf{H}\colon(1,\beta)
; and the CQ
Q\colon H()\leftarrow\exists x,y.\varphi
simulates
\mathbf{Q}
if
\Rel(\mathbf{H})
is nullary.

Finally, we can provide a formal proof for the simulation from
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
to
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
.

Proposition 5
.

For every
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\mathbf{Q}
over
\Sch
and every matrix-to-relational schema encoding
\Rel
on
\Sch\!\left(\mathbf{Q}\right)
, there exists an
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query
Q
that simulates
\mathbf{Q}
w.r.t.
\Rel
.

Proof 4.2
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{H}\coloneqq e
be a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query. By definition of query,
e
is
a sentence with
\texttt{type}(e)=(\alpha,\beta)
for some
\alpha
,
\beta
.
Note that every
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
sentence can be expressed as a sum of
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
sentences. Indeed,
\left(e_{1}+e_{2}\right)^{T}=e_{1}^{T}+e_{2}^{T}
;
e_{1}\odot\left(e_{1}+e_{2}\right)=e_{1}\odot e_{2}+e_{1}\odot e_{3}
;
\left(e_{1}+e_{2}\right)\odot e_{3}=e_{1}\odot e_{3}+e_{2}\odot e_{3}
;
e_{1}\cdot\left(e_{2}+e_{3}\right)=e_{1}\cdot e_{2}+e_{1}\cdot e_{3}
;
\left(e_{1}+e_{2}\right)\cdot e_{3}=e_{1}\cdot e_{3}+e_{2}\cdot e_{3}
; and
\Sigma\mathbf{v}.\left(e_{1}+e_{2}\right)=\Sigma\mathbf{v}.e_{1}+\Sigma\mathbf{v}.e_{2}
.
Therefore, we can assume that
e
is of the form
e_{1}+\cdots+e_{l}

such that each
e_{i}
is a
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
sentence in prenex normal form.

By Proposition
4
, for each
i=1,\ldots,l
there exists a CQ
Q_{i}\colon H(\bar{x}_{i})\leftarrow\psi_{i}
that simulates the query
\mathbf{H}\coloneqq e_{i}
w.r.t.
\Rel
.
Further, since
e=e_{1}+\cdots+e_{l}
is well-typed, then
e_{i}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
for every
i
. This implies that every formula
\psi_{i}
has the same number of free variables and we can assume that
\bar{x}_{1}=\ldots=\bar{x}_{l}=\bar{x}
without loss of generality. Therefore, the
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query
Q\colon H(\bar{x})\leftarrow\psi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\psi_{l}
simulates
\mathbf{Q}
where
\psi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\psi_{l}
is a safe formula.

4.2.
From positive FO to Sum-Matlang

We now aim to simulate every
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query with a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query. Contrary to the previous direction, the expressiveness result here is more subtle and requires more discussion and additional notions.

Fix a vocabulary
\Voc
. Let
Q
be a
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query over
\Voc
and let
\Mat
be relational-to-matrix schema encoding on
\Voc(Q)
. We say that a matrix query
\mathbf{Q}

simulates

Q
w.r.t.
\Mat
if
\Mat(\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\textit{db}})=\llbracket\mathbf{Q}\rrbracket(\Mat(\textit{db}))
for every database
db
consistent with
\Mat
. We note again that this definition implies that the input vocabulary and output relation symbol of
Q
coincides with the input schema and output matrix symbol of
\mathbf{Q}
, respectively. Further, it is equivalent that
\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\textit{db}}=\Mat^{-1}(\llbracket\mathbf{Q}\rrbracket(\Mat(\textit{db})))
.

Before stating how to connect
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
with
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
, we need to overcome the following problem: a
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query can use the same variable within different relational atoms, which can be mapped to matrix symbols of different types. For an illustrative example of this problem, consider the query

Q\colon H(x,y)\leftarrow\exists z.\left(R(x,y)\wedge S(y,z)\right)

and a relational-to-matrix schema encoding such that
\Mat
maps
R
and
S
to symbols of type
(\alpha,\beta)
,
H
to a symbol of type
(\beta,\beta)
, and
c
and
d
to
\alpha
and
\beta
, respectively. For a consistent database
db
w.r.t.
\Mat
, we could have that
R
and
S
are consistent with
c^{\textit{db}}\times d^{\textit{db}}
, but
H
is not consistent with
d^{\textit{db}}\times d^{\textit{db}}
if
d^{\textit{db}}<c^{\textit{db}}
. Moreover,
\Mat
bounds variable
y
with different sizes
c^{\textit{db}}
and
d^{\textit{db}}
. It is then problematic to simulate
Q
under
\Mat
in
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
because
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions need to be well-typed.

Given the previous discussion, a
well-typedness
definition of a
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formula is necessary. Let
\Mat
be a relational-to-matrix schema encoding on
\Voc
. Given a
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formula
\varphi
over
\Voc
and a function
\tau
from
\free(\varphi)
to size symbols in
\Mat(\sigma)
, define the rule
\Mat\vdash\varphi\colon\tau
inductively as shown in Figure
1
, where
\tau_{1}\sim\tau_{2}
if and only if
\tau_{1}(x)=\tau_{2}(x)
for every
x\in dom(\tau_{1})\cap dom(\tau_{2})
. We say that
\varphi
over
\Voc
is
well-typed
w.r.t.
\Mat
if there exists such a function
\tau
such that
\Mat\vdash\varphi\colon\tau
. Note that if
\varphi
is well-typed, then there is a unique
\tau
such that
\Mat\vdash\varphi\colon\tau
.

\dfrac{\texttt{type}(\Mat(R))=(\alpha,\beta)}{\Mat\vdash R(x_{1},x_{2})\colon\{x_{1}\mapsto\alpha,x_{2}\mapsto\beta\}}\quad\quad\quad\dfrac{\texttt{type}(\Mat(R))=(\alpha,1)\text{ or }(1,\alpha)}{\Mat\vdash R(x)\colon\{x\mapsto\alpha\}}

\dfrac{\texttt{type}(\Mat(R))=(1,1)}{\Mat\vdash R()\colon\{\}}\quad\quad\quad\dfrac{}{\Mat\vdash x\leq c\colon\{x\mapsto\Mat(c)\}}\quad\quad\quad\dfrac{\Mat\vdash\varphi\colon\tau}{\Mat\vdash\exists\overline{y}.\varphi\colon{\tau}|_{\free(\varphi)\setminus\overline{y}}}

\dfrac{\Mat\vdash\varphi_{1}\colon\tau_{1},\Mat\vdash\varphi_{2}\colon\tau_{2}\text{ and }\tau_{1}\sim\tau_{2}}{\Mat\vdash\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}\colon\tau_{1}\cup\tau_{2}}\quad\quad\quad\dfrac{\Mat\vdash\varphi_{1}\colon\tau_{1},\Mat\vdash\varphi_{2}\colon\tau_{2}\text{ and }\tau_{1}\sim\tau_{2}}{\Mat\vdash\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}\colon\tau_{1}\cup\tau_{2}}

\dfrac{\alpha\text{ a size symbol }}{\Mat\vdash x=y\colon\{x\mapsto\alpha,y\mapsto\alpha\}}

Figure 1
.
Well-typedness of
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
under relational-to-matrix mapping
\Mat
.

Now, let
Q\colon H(\overline{x})\leftarrow\varphi
be a binary
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query over
\Voc
and
\Mat
be a matrix encoding specification on
\sigma(Q)
. We say that
Q
is
well-typed
w.r.t.
\Mat
if
\varphi
is well-typed w.r.t.
\Mat
and for
\tau
such that
\Mat\vdash\varphi\colon\tau
, we have:

•

if
\overline{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})
, then
\texttt{type}(\Mat(H))=(\tau(x_{1}),\tau(x_{2}))
; or

•

if
\overline{x}=(x)
, then
\texttt{type}(\Mat(H))
is either
(\tau(x),1)
or
(1,\tau(x))
.

We write
\Mat\vdash Q\colon\tau
to indicate that
Q
is well-typed w.r.t.
\Mat
, and
\tau
is the unique function testifying to well-typedness of
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formula of
Q
. We note that that we can show that the query obtained by Proposition
5
is always well-typed.

The next proposition connects well-typedness with consistency.

Proposition 6
.

For binary
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query
Q\colon H(\overline{x})\leftarrow\varphi
over a vocabulary
\Voc
, if
\Mat\vdash Q\colon\tau
then for any
db
consistent with
\Mat
we have:

•

If
\overline{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})
then
\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\textit{db}}(H)
is consistent with dimension
\tau(x_{1})^{\textit{db}}\times\tau(x_{2})^{\textit{db}}
.

•

If
\overline{x}=(x)
then
\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\textit{db}}(H)
is consistent with both dimension
\tau(x)^{\textit{db}}\times 1
and
1\times\tau(x)^{\textit{db}}
.

Proof 4.3
.

We show a more detailed statement. Namely, that for any
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formula
\varphi
with
\Mat\vdash\varphi\colon\tau
, any database
db
consistent with
\Mat
and any variable
x\in\free(\varphi)
if
\nu
is a valuation with
\nu(x)>\tau(x)^{\textit{db}}
then
\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)=\mymathbb{0}
. The proposition follows from this more general statement since
Q
is of the form
H(\overline{x})\leftarrow\varphi
with
\overline{x}
a tuple consisting of the free variables of
\varphi
(possibly with repetition) and since
\texttt{type}(\Mat(H))=\tau(\overline{x})
as
Q
is well-typed under
\Mat
.

The proof of the more detailed statement is by induction on
\varphi
. We illustrate the reasoning when
\varphi
is an atom, or a disjunction. The other cases are similar.

If
\varphi
is a relational atom
R(x_{1},x_{2})
, then by definition of the typing relation, we know that
(\tau(x_{1}),\tau(x_{2}))=(\alpha,\beta)
where
\texttt{type}(\Mat(R))=(\alpha,\beta)
. Hence, for valuation
\nu
with
\nu(x_{1})>\tau(x_{1})^{\textit{db}}
or
\nu(x_{2})>\tau(x_{2})^{\textit{db}}
we have
\llceil{R(x_{1},x_{2})}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)=\mymathbb{0}
because
R^{\textit{db}}
is consistent with dimensions
\alpha^{\textit{db}}\times\beta^{\textit{db}}=\tau(x_{1})^{\textit{db}}\times\tau(x_{2})^{\textit{db}}
. If
\varphi
is a comparison atom
x\leq\alpha
, then
\tau(x)=\alpha
by definition of the typing relation. Hence, for valuation
\nu
with
\nu(x)>\tau(x)^{\textit{db}}=\alpha^{\textit{db}}
we trivially obtain
\llceil{x\leq\alpha}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)=\mymathbb{0}
.

When
\varphi
is a disjunction
\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
, let a valuation
\nu\colon\free(\varphi)
such that
\nu(x)>\tau(x)^{\textit{db}}
for some
x\in\free(\varphi)
. Because we consider only safe formula’s, both
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
have the same set of free variables, which equals the free variables of
\varphi
. Then, from
x\in\free(\varphi_{1})
and
x\in\free(\varphi_{2})
, and so by induction hypothesis both
\llceil{\varphi_{1}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)=\mymathbb{0}
and
\llceil{\varphi_{2}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)=\mymathbb{0}
hold. Hence
\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)=\mymathbb{0}
.

Similar to the proof from
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
to
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
, we first illustrate how to simulate CQs with
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
queries.

Proposition 7
.

For every binary CQ
Q
over a vocabulary
\Voc
and every relational-to-matrix schema encoding
\Mat
on
\Voc(Q)
such that
Q
is well typed w.r.t.
\Mat
there exists a
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\mathbf{Q}
that simulates
Q
w.r.t.
\Mat
.

Proof 4.4
.

Let
Q\colon H(\overline{x})\leftarrow\exists v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}.\varphi
with
\free(\varphi)=\{x,y\}
and
\overline{x}\subseteq\{x,y\}
be a CQ, i.e.,
\varphi=a_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge a_{n}
where each
a_{i}
is a relational, comparison, or equality atom using variables from the set
\{x,y,v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
. Assume
\Mat\vdash Q\colon\tau
.

For simplicity, let all input relations be binary (we will deal with the other cases later). Let
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}\}
be vector variables of type
(\tau(x),1),(\tau(y),1),(\tau(v_{1}),1),\ldots,\allowbreak(\tau(v_{k}),1)
, respectively. In what follows, we choose
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}\}
respectively according to
v,w\in\{x,y,v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
. For each
i=1,\ldots,n
define:

•

\mathbf{s}_{i}\coloneqq\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{w}
if
a_{i}=A(v,w)
. Note that
\Mat(A)\colon(\tau(v),\tau(w))
,
\mathbf{v}\colon(\tau(v),1)
and
\mathbf{w}\colon(\tau(w),1)
.

•

\mathbf{s}_{i}\coloneqq\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{v}
if
a_{i}=v\leq\alpha
. Note that
\mathbf{v}\colon(\tau(v),1)
.

•

\mathbf{s}_{i}\coloneqq\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{w}
if
a_{i}:=v=w
. Note that
\mathbf{v}\colon(\tau(v),1)
and
\mathbf{w}\colon(\tau(w),1)
, with
\tau(v)=\tau(w)
because
Q
is well-typed.

Finally, define

•

e=\Sigma\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}.\hskip 4.30554pt\left(\mathbf{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbf{s}_{n}\right)\times\left(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{T}\right)
if the head of
Q
is
H(x,y)
.

•

e=\Sigma\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}.\hskip 4.30554pt\left(\mathbf{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbf{s}_{n}\right)\times\left(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{T}\right)
if the head of
Q
is
H(x,x)
.

•

e=\Sigma\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}.\hskip 4.30554pt\left(\mathbf{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbf{s}_{n}\right)\times\mathbf{x}
if the head of
Q
is
H(x)
and
\mathbf{H}\colon(\alpha,1)
. Otherwise, if
\mathbf{H}\colon(1,\alpha)
, define
e=\Sigma\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}.\hskip 4.30554pt\left(\mathbf{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbf{s}_{n}\right)\times\mathbf{x}^{T}
.

Then
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{H}\coloneqq e
simulates
Q
w.r.t.
\Mat
for any choice of
\overline{x}\subseteq\{x,y\}
.

If some input is not binary, define:

•

\mathbf{s}_{i}\coloneqq\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{w}
if
a_{i}=A(v)
such that
\Mat(A)\colon(\tau(v),1)
and
\mathbf{v}\colon(\tau(v),1)
for some
\mathbf{w}\colon(1,1)
.

•

\mathbf{s}_{i}\coloneqq\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{w}
if
a_{i}=A()
such that
\Mat(A)\colon(1,1)
, for some
\mathbf{w}\colon(1,1)
.

Finally, if there is no
\mathbf{v}_{i}\colon(1,1)
in
e
above we add a dummy
\mathbf{w}\colon(1,1)
vector variable as
e=\Sigma\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k},\mathbf{w}.\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbf{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbf{s}_{n}\times\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{T}
.

We have now all the formal machinery to state how to simulate every binary
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query over relations with a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query over matrices.

Proposition 8
.

For every binary
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query
Q
over a vocabulary
\Voc
and every relational-to-matrix schema encoding
\Mat
on
\Voc(Q)
such that
Q
is well typed w.r.t.
\Mat
there exists a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\mathbf{Q}
that simulates
Q
w.r.t.
\Mat
.

Proof 4.5
.

First, note that every
\varphi\in\mathsf{FO}^{+}
can be written in disjunctive normal form, i.e.,
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\varphi_{l}
where each
\varphi_{i}
is in prenex normal form and the big disjunction is safe. On one hand,
\exists x.\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}\equiv\exists x.\psi_{1}\vee\exists x.\psi_{2}
and
\left(\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}\right)\wedge\psi_{3}\equiv\left(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}\right)\vee\left(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{3}\right)
. On the other hand, if
\psi=R(\overline{x})\mid x\leq c\mid x=y\mid\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}\mid\exists x.\psi
then it can be written in prenex normal form, because
\psi_{1}\wedge\exists x.\psi_{2}\equiv\exists x.\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}
since without loss of generality we can assume that
x
does not appear in
\varphi_{1}
.

Now, let
Q\colon H(\overline{x})\leftarrow\varphi
be a binary
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query such that
\varphi
is in disjunctive normal form, i.e.,
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\varphi_{l}
. By Proposition
7
there exist
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
queries
\mathbf{Q}_{i}=\mathbf{H}\coloneqq e_{i}
that simulate the CQs
Q\colon H(\overline{x})\leftarrow\varphi_{i}
w.r.t.
\Mat
, respectively.

It is straightforward to see that
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{H}\coloneqq e_{1}+\cdots+e_{l}
simulates
Q
w.r.t.
\Mat
.

4.3.
Equivalence between Sum-Matlang and positive FO

Taking into account the correspondence between
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
established by Propositions
5
and
8
, in what follows we say that matrix query language
\mathcal{L}_{M}\subseteq\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and relational language
\mathcal{L}_{R}\subseteq\mathsf{FO}^{+}
are
equivalent
or
equally expressive
if (1) for every matrix query
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{L}_{M}
over a matrix schema
\Sch
and every matrix-to-relational schema encoding
\Rel
on
\Sch\!\left(\mathbf{Q}\right)
there exists a query
Q\in\mathcal{L}_{R}
that simulates
\mathbf{Q}
w.r.t.
\Rel
and is well-typed w.r.t.
\Rel^{-1}
; and (2) for every binary query
Q\in\mathcal{L}_{R}
over a vocabulary
\sigma
and every relational-to-matrix schema encoding
\Mat
such that
Q
is well-typed w.r.t.
\Mat
there exists
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{L}_{M}
that simulates
Q
w.r.t
\Mat
.

From Propositions
5
and
8
, and Propositions
4
and
7
one obtains the following characterization of positive FO and CQs, respectively.

Corollary 9
.

(1)

\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and binary
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
queries are equally expressive.

(2)

\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and binary conjunctive queries are equally expressive.

While the result between
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and CQs is a consequence of the connection between
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
, it provides the basis to explore the fragments of
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
that correspond to fragments of CQ, like free-connex or q-hierarchical CQ. We determine these fragments in the next sections.

5.
Free-connex conjunctive queries and
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}

We wish to understand the fragment of
\mathsf{MATLANG}
that allows efficient
enumeration-based evaluation, i.e., enumerating the query result with constant
delay after a preprocessing phase that is linear in the input instance. Towards
that goal, we will first specialize in this section and the next the
correspondence between
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and CQs given by
Corollary
9
to
free-connex

CQs
[
BDG07
,
Bra13
]
. Next, we recall the definition of free-connex CQs and state the main result of this section.

5.1.
Free-connex CQs.

First, recall that a query
Q
is a CQ if it is in prenex normal form:

Q:H(\overline{x})\leftarrow\exists\overline{y}.a_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge a_{n}

(*)

where
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
are relational, comparison, or equality atoms.
From now on, unless explicitly stated otherwise, we will assume without loss of generality that a CQ does not have equality atoms.
Indeed, we can always remove equality atoms from CQ by taking the equivalence classes formed by all equalities in the body and replacing each variable in the body and head of the query with a representative from its class.

A CQ
Q
is
acyclic
(also sometimes referred to as

\alpha
-acyclic
[
Fag83
]
) if it has a
join
tree
. A join tree for
Q
is an undirected tree
T
whose node-set is the set
of all atoms (i.e., relational or comparison atoms) occurring in
Q
, that satisfies the

connectedness
property. This property requires that for every variable

x
occurring (free or bound) in
Q
, all the nodes containing
x
form a
connected subtree of
T
. Note that we consider comparison atoms as unary.
Then, a CQ
Q
like (
*
‣
5.1
) is

free-connex

if it is acyclic and the query
Q^{\prime}
obtained by adding the head atom
H(\overline{x})

to the body of
Q
is also acyclic.
The requirement that the query
Q^{\prime}
obtained by adding the head atom
H(\overline{x})
to
the body of
Q
is also acyclic forbids queries like

H(x,y)\leftarrow\exists z.A(x,z)\wedge B(z,y)
. Indeed: when we adjoin the head
to the body we get
\exists z.A(x,z)\wedge B(z,y)\wedge H(x,y)
, which is
cyclic. We will usually refer to free-connex CQs simply as fc-CQs in what
follows.

Free-connex CQs are a subset of acyclic CQs
that allow efficient enumeration-based query evaluation: in the Boolean semiring
and under data complexity they allow to enumerate the query result

\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\textit{db}}(H)
of free-connex CQ
Q\colon H(\overline{x})\leftarrow\varphi
with

constant delay
after a preprocessing phase that is linear in
db
. In
fact, under complexity-theoretic assumptions, the class of CQs that admits
constant delay enumeration after linear time preprocessing is precisely the
class of free-connex CQs
[
BDG07
]
.

In this section, we prove the following correspondence between fc-CQs and
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
,
the two-variable fragment of
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}
. Formally, a formula
\varphi
in

\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}
is said to be in
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
if it does not use equality and the
set of all variables used in
\varphi
(free or bound) is of cardinality at most
two. So,
\exists y\exists z.A(x,y)\wedge B(y,z)
is not in
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
, but the
equivalent formula
\exists y.\left(A(x,y)\wedge\exists x.B(y,x)\right)

is. An
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
query is a binary query whose body is an
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}

formula. Recall that a query is binary if every relational atom occurring in its
body and head have arity at most two.

Theorem 10
.

Binary free-connex CQs and
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
queries are equally expressive.

We find this a remarkable characterization of the fc-CQs on binary relations
that, to the best of our knowledge, it is new. Moreover, we will use this result
in Section
6
to identify the fragment of
\mathsf{MATLANG}
queries that expressively correspond to fc-CQs. Having that fragment in hand, we
later turn to its actual query evaluation on arbitrary (not necessarily Boolean)
semirings in Section
10
.

The rest of this section is devoted to proving
Theorem
10
. We do so in three
steps. First, in Section
5.2
we define
query plans
,
which allow to give an alternate but equivalent definition of fc-CQs that we find
more convenient to use. Then, in Sections
5.3
and
5.4
we prove the two directions of Theorem
10
.

5.2.
Query plans.

To simplify notation, in what follows we denote the set of all FO variables that occur in an object
X
by
\var(X)
. In particular, if
X
is itself a set of variables, then
\var(X)=X
.

A
query plan
(QP for short) is a pair
(T,N)
with
T
a

generalized join tree
and
N
a
sibling-closed connex subset of

T
.
A generalized join tree (GJT) is a node-labeled directed tree

T=(V,E)
such that

•

T
is binary: every node has at most two children.

•

Every leaf is labeled by an atom (relational or inequality).

•

Every interior node
n
is labeled by a set of variables and has
at least one child
c
such that
\var(n)\subseteq\var(c)
. Such
child
c
is called the
guard
of
n
.

•

Connectedness property: for every variable
x
the sub-graph
consisting of the nodes
n
where
x\in\var(n)
is connected.

A
connex subset of
T
is a set
N\subseteq V
that includes the
root of
T
such that the subgraph of
T
induced by
N
is a tree.
N
is

sibling-closed
if for every node
n\in N
with a sibling
m
in
T
,
also
m
is in
N
. The
frontier
of a connex set
N
is the subset

F\subseteq N
consisting of those nodes in
N
that are the leaves in the
subtree of
T
induced by
N
.

{exa}

To illustrate, Figure
2
shows two query plans. There and in future illustrations we use the convention that we depict a node with id
n
and label
l
as
n;l
. The elements of the query plan’s connex set are indicated by the gray area. So, in
T_{1}
, we have
N_{1}=\{a,b,e\}
and in
T_{2}
we have
N_{2}=\{a,b,e,f\}
. Since all nodes in a connex set must be connected, an unallowed choice of
N_{1}
for the tree
T_{1}
would be
\{a,c\}
. Furthermore, also
\{a,b\}
is not allowed, since the set must be sibling-closed.

a;\{y\}
b;\{y,z\}
c;S(y,z)
d;T(z,w)
e;R(x,y)
a;\{y,w\}
b;\{y,w\}
c;R(x,y)
d;S(y,w,z)
e;\{w\}
f;T(u,w)
(T_{1},N_{1})
(T_{2},N_{2})

Figure 2
.

Two query plans
(T_{1},N_{1})
and
(T_{2},N_{2})
where the sets
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are indicated by the gray area.

For ease of presentation, we abbreviate query plans of the form
(T,\{r\})
simply by
(T,r)
when the connex set consists only of the single root node
r
. Furthermore, if
T
is a GJT and
n
is a node of
T
, then we write
{T}|_{n}
for the subtree of
T
rooted at
n
.

Let
(T,N)
be a query plan and assume that
\{\!\!\{a_{1},\ldots a_{n}\}\!\!\}
is the
multiset of atoms occurring as labels in the leaves of
T
. Then the

formula associated to
(T,N)
is the conjunctive
formula

\varphi[T,N]\ :=\ \exists y_{1}\ldots\exists y_{k}.\,a_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge a_{n}

where
\var(T)\setminus\var(N)=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\}
. In particular,

\var(N)=\free(\varphi[T,N])
. A query plan
(T,N)
is a
query plan
for
a CQ
Q
with body
\varphi
if
\varphi[T,N]\equiv\varphi
.

{exa}

Referring to Figure
2
, we have

\displaystyle\varphi[T_{1},N_{1}]

\displaystyle=\exists w.\,S(y,z)\wedge T(z,w)\wedge R(x,y),

\displaystyle\varphi[T_{2},N_{2}]

\displaystyle=\exists x\exists z.\,R(x,y)\wedge S(y,w,z)\wedge T(u,w).

The following proposition is a particular case of the results shown in
[
IUV17
,
IUV+20
]
.

Proposition 11
.

A CQ
Q
is free-connex if, and only if, it has a query plan.

5.3.
From binary free-connex CQ to the two variable fragment of FO

We next show that it is possible to translate every binary fc-CQ
Q
into an
equivalent
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
query. Our construction is by induction on a suitable query
plan for
Q
. To enable this induction, we first observe the following properties of query plans.

Two query plans
(T,N)
and
(T^{\prime},N^{\prime})
are said to be
equivalent
if (1) the multiset of atoms
\atoms(T)
appearing in
T
equals the multiset of atoms
\atoms(T^{\prime})
appearing in
T^{\prime}
, and (2)
\var(N)=\var(N^{\prime})
.
1
1

1

Note that in condition (1) we view
\atoms(T)
and
\atoms(T^{\prime})
as
multisets
: so
T
and
