T^{\prime}
must have the same set of atoms, and each atom must appear the same number of times in both.

Clearly if two query plans are equivalent then their associated formulas are
also equivalent.

Lemma 12
.

For every QP there exists an equivalent QP such that for any node
n
with two children
c_{1},c_{2}
it holds that
\var(c_{1})\subseteq\var(n)
and
\var(c_{2})\subseteq\var(n)
. In particular, either
\var(c_{1})=\var(n)
or
\var(c_{2})=\var(n)
holds.

Proof 5.1
.

Let
n
be any node of
T
that has two children
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
.
Let
(T,N)
be the original query plan.
We modify
(T,N)
into an equivalent plan
(T^{\prime},N^{\prime})
that satisfies the lemma by adding, for every node
n
with two children
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, two new intermediate nodes
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
as follows labeled with
\var(n_{1})=\var(n)\cap\var(c_{1})
and
\var(n_{2})=\var(n)\cap\var(c_{2})
.

n
c_{1}
c_{2}
n
n_{1}
c_{1}
n_{2}
c_{2}

We obtain the set
N^{\prime}
by adding also
n_{i}
to
N
if
c_{i}\in N
,
i=1,2
.
It is readily verified that
T^{\prime}
is a GJT;
N^{\prime}
is a sibling-closed connex
subset of
T^{\prime}
; that
T
and
T^{\prime}
have the same multiset of atoms; and that

\var(N)=\var(N^{\prime})
. Hence
(T,N)\equiv(T^{\prime},N^{\prime})
. Moreover, in
T^{\prime}

every node
n
with two children
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
is such that

\var(n_{1})\subseteq\var(n)
and
\var(n_{2})\subseteq\var(n)
. Finally, because
T
is a GJT, it is the case that either
\var(n)\subseteq\var(c_{1})
or
\var(n)\subseteq\var(c_{2})
. As such, either
\var(n)=\var(n_{1})
or
\var(n)=\var(n_{2})
.

Next, we show how the formulas and subformulas encoded by a query plan are related.

Lemma 13
.

Let
(T,M)
and
(T,N)
be two QPs over the same GJT such that

N\subseteq M
. Then
\varphi[T,N]\equiv\exists\overline{z}.\varphi[T,M]
with

\overline{z}=var(M)\setminus\var(N)
.

Proof 5.2
.

Let
\{\!\!\{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\}\!\!\}
be the multiset of atoms in
T
. Let
\overline{x}
be the set of all variables occurring in
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}
that are not mentioned in
\var(N)
. Let
\overline{y}
be the set of all variables occurring in
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}
that are not mentioned in
\var(M)
. Because
N\subseteq M
also
\var(N)\subseteq\var(M)
and hence
\overline{x}\supseteq\overline{y}
. Moreover,
\overline{x}=\overline{y}\cup\overline{z}
. Then, by definition,

\varphi[T,N]=\exists\overline{x}.(a_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge a_{k})\equiv\exists\overline{z}.\exists\overline{y}.(a_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge a_{k})\equiv\exists\overline{z}.\varphi[T,M]

Lemma 14
.

Let
(T,N)
be a query plan and let
F=\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{l}\}
be the frontier of
N
, i.e, the bottom nodes of
N
. Then
\varphi[T,N]\equiv\varphi[{T}|_{r_{1}},r_{1}]\wedge\dots\wedge\varphi[{T}|_{r_{l}},r_{l}]
.

Proof 5.3
.

Let, for every frontier node
r_{i}
,
A_{i}
be the multiset of atoms appearing in
{T}|_{r_{i}}
. Then, by definition

\varphi[T,N]=\exists\overline{u}.\ \bigwedge_{a_{1}\in A_{1}}a_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\bigwedge_{a_{l}\in A_{l}}a_{l}

where
\overline{u}
is the sequence of all variables in
T
except the variables in
N
.

We can write
\overline{u}
as union of
l
sets
\overline{v_{1}}\cup\dots\cup\overline{v_{l}}
where
\overline{v_{i}}
consists of all variables in
T_{i}
except
\var(N)
.
Because
T
is a GJT, it satisfies the connectedness property, and hence we know that any variable that is shared by atoms in
T_{i}
and atoms in
T_{j}
, for
1\leq i<j\leq l
must be in
\var(r_{i})\cap\var(r_{j})\subseteq\var(N)
.
It follows that the sets
\overline{v_{i}}
are disjoint: assume for the purpose of obtaining a contradiction that variable
x
occur in both
\overline{v_{i}}
and
\overline{v_{j}}
. Then it occurs in
T_{i}
and
T_{j}
, and hence, by the running intersection property of
T
in
\var(r_{i})\cap\var(r_{j})
.
Therefore,
x\in\var(N)
, which gives the desired contradiction. Then we can rewrite
\varphi[T,N]
as follows

\displaystyle\varphi[T,N]

\displaystyle=\exists\overline{u}.\ \bigwedge_{a_{1}\in A_{1}}a_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\bigwedge_{a_{l}\in A_{l}}a_{l}

\displaystyle=\exists\overline{v_{1}}\exists\overline{v_{2}}\dots\exists\overline{v_{l}}.\ \bigwedge_{a_{1}\in A_{1}}a_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\bigwedge_{a_{l}\in A_{l}}a_{l}

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\exists\overline{v_{1}}.\ \bigwedge_{a_{1}\in A_{1}}a_{1}\right)\wedge\dots\wedge\left(\exists\overline{v_{l}}.\ \bigwedge_{a_{l}\in A_{l}}a_{l}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\varphi[T_{1},r_{1}]\wedge\dots\wedge\varphi[T_{l},r_{l}].

Here, the last equivalence holds because
\overline{v_{i}}
is exactly the set of variables in
T_{i}
except
\var(r_{i})
.
This can be seen as follows. By definition,
\overline{v_{i}}=\var(T_{i})\setminus\var(N)
.
Because
\var(N)\supseteq\var(r_{i})
, it follows that
\overline{v_{i}}\subseteq\var(T_{i})\setminus\var(r_{i})
.
Now assume, for the purpose of obtaining a contradiction that this inclusion is strict, i.e., that there is some
x\in\var(T_{i})\cap\var(N)
that is not in
\var(r_{i})
.
Then, because it is in
\var(N)
there is some
r_{j}\not=r_{i}
with
x\in\var(r_{j})
.
Because also
x\in\var(T_{i})
, it appears in an atom of
T_{i}
and is shared with an atom of
T_{j}
.
Because
T
has the running intersection property, we know that hence
x\in\var(r_{i})\cap\var(r_{j})
, which is the desired contradiction since then
x\in\var(r_{i})
.

Corollary 15
.

Let
(T,r)
be a query plan where
r
has one child
c
. Then
\varphi[T,r]\equiv\exists\overline{y}.\varphi[{T}|_{c},c]
with
\overline{y}=\var(c)\setminus\var(r)
.

Proof 5.4
.

Consider the set
M=\{r,c\}
, which is connex in
T
. By
Lemma
13
we have

\varphi[T,r]\equiv\exists\overline{y}.\varphi[T,M]
. Moreover, since the frontier of

M
is
\{c\}
we obtain by
Lemma
14

that
\varphi[T,M]\equiv\varphi[{T}|_{c},c]
. Hence

\varphi[T,r]\equiv\exists\overline{y}.\varphi[{T}|_{c},c]
.

Corollary 16
.

Let
(T,r)
be a query plan where
r
has two children
r_{1},r_{2}
such that one of the following holds:

•

\var(r_{1})=\var(r)
and
\var(r_{2})\subseteq\var(r)
; or

•

\var(r_{2})=\var(r)
and
\var(r_{1})\subseteq\var(r)
.

Then
\varphi[T,r]\equiv\varphi[{T}|_{r_{1}},r_{1}]\wedge\varphi[{T}|_{r_{2}},r_{2}]
.

Proof 5.5
.

Let
N=\{r,r_{1},r_{2}\}
. This set is a connex subset of
T
. Hence,
(T,N)
is
also a query plan. Observe that, because
\var(N)=\var(r)
by the
assumptions on
\var(r_{1})
and
\var(r_{2})
, we have

\varphi[T,r]=\varphi[T,N]
. Further observe that the frontier of
N
is

\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
. Then, by Lemma
14

\varphi[T,r]=\varphi[T,N]\equiv\varphi[T_{1},r_{1}]\wedge\varphi[T_{2},r_{2}]
as desired.

Finally, we note a property of query plans over binary relations.

Lemma 17
.

Let
(T,N)
be a query plan such that
\varphi[T,N]
is binary then
|\var(N)|\leq 2
and any node of
T
contains at most two variables.

Proof 5.6
.

By definition
\var(N)=\free(\varphi[T,N])
, then necessarily

|\var(N)|\leq 2
because
\varphi[T,N]
is binary. Now, suppose that
there exists some node
g
of
T
that has more than two variables. This
implies that the child which is the guard of
g
also has more than two
variables, and so on. Thus there is an leaf atom that uses more than two
variables, which is not possible since
\varphi[T,N]
is binary.

At this point we are ready to prove that we can translate any binary query plan of the form
(T,r)
into an equivalent
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula.

Proposition 18
.

For every QP
(T,r)
such that
\varphi[T,r]
is binary, there exists an
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula
\psi
equivalent to
\varphi[T,r]
.

Proof 5.7
.

By Lemma
12
we may assume without loss of generality that for every node
n
in
T
with children
c_{1},c_{2}
either
\var(c_{1})=\var(n)
and
\var(c_{2})\subseteq\var(n)
; or
\var(c_{2})=\var(n)
and
\var(c_{1})\subseteq\var(n)
. Moreover, by Lemma
17
we know that all nodes
n
in
T
have
|\var(n)|\leq 2
.
We build the
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula
\psi
equivalent to
\varphi[T,r]
by induction on the height of
T
.

The base case is where the height of
T
is zero. Then
T
consists of just its root,
r
, which must be labeled by atom
a
and
\varphi[T,r]=a
. Then it suffices to take
\psi\coloneqq a
. Clearly,
\psi\equiv\varphi[T,r]
and, because
a
is of arity at most two by assumption,
\psi\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
.

Now, for the inductive step. Assume
T
has height
k>0
. By inductive hypothesis we have that the statement holds for height
k-1
.
We distinguish the cases where
r
has only one child or it has two children.

•

First, if
r
has only one child
c
. By induction hypothesis there exists a formula
\psi_{c}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
such that
\psi_{c}\equiv\varphi[{T}|_{c},c]
. Note that this implies that
\free(\psi_{c})=\free(\varphi[{T}|_{c},c])
. Take
\psi\coloneqq\exists\var(c)\setminus\var(r).\psi_{c}
. By Corollary
15
we have

\psi=\exists\var(c)\setminus\var(r).\psi_{c}\equiv\exists\var(c)\setminus\var(r).\varphi[{T}|_{c},c]\equiv\varphi[T,r].

Clearly,
\psi\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
, since it is a projection of
\psi_{c}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
.

•

Second, assume that
r
has two children,
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
. We may
assume w.l.o.g. that
\var(r_{1})=\var(r)
and

\var(r_{2})\subseteq\var(r)
. By the inductive
hypothesis there exist
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas

\psi_{1}\equiv\varphi[{T}|_{r_{1}},r_{1}]
and
\psi_{2}\equiv\varphi[{T}|_{r_{2}},r_{2}]
. We
next construct an
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula equivalent to
\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}
. This suffices since by
Corollary
16

\varphi[{T}|_{r_{1}},r_{1}]\wedge\varphi[{T}|_{r_{2}},r_{2}]\equiv\varphi[T,r]
and hence

\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}\equiv\varphi[T,r]
.

Note that
\free(\psi_{1})=\var(r_{1})=\var(r)
and
\free(\psi_{2})=\var(r_{2})\subseteq\var(r)
. Moreover,
\varphi[T,r]
is binary. Thus, there exists a set of two variables, say
\{x,y\}
, such that
\var(r)\subseteq\{x,y\}
. Consequently,
\free(\psi_{1})\subseteq\{x,y\}
,
\free(\psi_{2})\subseteq\{x,y\}
. Because
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
we know that when
\psi_{1}
(or
\psi_{2}
) has exactly two free variables,
x
and
y
are the only variables used in
\psi_{1}
(resp.
\psi_{2}
). When
\psi_{1}
(resp.
\psi_{2}
) has fewer than two free variables it is possible that also some other variable
z\not\in\{x,y\}
occurs in
\psi_{1}
. However, because
\psi_{1}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
it is then always possible to rename bound variables to use only
x,y
. The same holds for
\psi_{2}
. We conclude that there exist
formulas
\psi_{1}^{\prime},\psi_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
such that
\psi_{1}\equiv\psi_{1}^{\prime}
,
\psi_{2}\equiv\psi_{2}^{\prime}
and
\psi_{1}^{\prime},\psi_{2}^{\prime}
only use the variables
x
and
y
. Thus
\varphi[{T}|_{r_{1}},r_{1}]\wedge\varphi[{T}|_{r_{2}},r_{2}]\equiv\psi_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\psi_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
.

Finally, we are able to show that every formula encoded by a binary query plan has an equivalent
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula.

Proposition 19
.

For every QP
(T,N)
such that
\varphi[T,N]
is binary, there exists an
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula
\psi
equivalent to
\varphi[T,N]
.

Proof 5.8
.

By Lemma
12
we may assume w.l.o.g. that for every node
n
in
T
with two children
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
we have
\var(c_{1})=\var(n)
and
\var(c_{2})\subseteq\var(n)
; or
\var(c_{1})=\var(n)
and
\var(c_{2})\subseteq\var(n)
.
Assume that the frontier of
N
is
F=\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{l}\}
. By Proposition
18
,
there are
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{l}
equivalent to

\varphi[{T}|_{r_{1}},r_{1}],\ldots,\varphi[{T}|_{r_{l}},r_{l}]
, respectively. By
Lemma
14

we have

\varphi[T,N]\equiv\varphi[{T}|_{r_{1}},r_{1}]\wedge\dots\wedge\varphi[{T}|_{r_{l}},r_{l}]\equiv\psi_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\psi_{l}
. To prove the proposition, it
hence suffices to show that the conjunction
\psi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\psi_{l}

is expressible in
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
.
For this we reason as follows.

By assumption
\varphi[T,N]
is binary, thus there exists a set of two
variables, say
\{x,y\}
, such that
\var(N)\subseteq\{x,y\}
. Hence,

\var(r_{i})\subseteq\var(N)\subseteq\{x,y\}
. Then, because
\psi_{i}
is
equivalent to
\varphi[T_{i},r_{i}]
, also

\free(\psi_{i})=\var(r_{i})\subseteq\{x,y\}
for
i=1,\ldots,l
. Similar to
our argumentation in the proof of Proposition
18

we can then exploit the fact that every
\psi_{i}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
to obtain
formulas
\psi_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\psi_{l}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
such that
\psi_{i}^{\prime}\equiv\psi_{i}

and
\psi_{i}^{\prime}
only uses the variables
x
and
y
, for
i=1,\ldots,l
. The
formula’s
\psi^{\prime}_{i}
are obtained from
\psi_{i}
by renaming bound variables, which is only necessary if
\psi_{i}
has fewer than two free variables but also mentions some bound variable
z\not\in\{x,y\}
. Thus

\varphi[T,N]\equiv\varphi[{T}|_{r_{1}},r_{1}]\wedge\dots\wedge\varphi[{T}|_{r_{l}},r_{l}]\equiv\psi_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\ldots\wedge\psi_{l}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}

5.4.
From the two variable fragment of FO to binary free-connex CQ

We end this section by proving the other direction, namely, that every formula in
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
is equivalent to some binary free-connex CQ. Similar to the previous subsection, we use binary query plans.

Proposition 20
.

For every
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula
\psi
there exists a binary QP
(T,N)
such that
\varphi[T,N]
is equivalent to
\psi
.

Proof 5.9
.

Fix two distinct variables
x
and
y
. Because
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas use at most
two variables, we may assume without loss of generality that the only variables occurring in
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}

formulas are
x
and
y
.
We obtain the proposition by proving a more detailed statement: for every

\psi\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
there exists a binary QP
(T,N)
such that
\varphi[T,N]\equiv\psi
and either:

•

N=\{r\}
with
r
the root node
r
; or

•

N
consists of three nodes,
N=\{r,c_{1},c_{2}\}
with
r
the root of

T
and
c_{1},c_{2}
its children, which are labeled by

\var(r)=\emptyset,\var(c_{1})=\{x\}
, and
\var(c_{2})=\{y\}
,
respectively. This case only happens when
\psi
is a cross product

\psi=\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}
.

Note that in the second case it follows from Lemma
14
that
\psi=\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}\equiv\varphi[T,N]\equiv\varphi[{T}|_{c_{1}},c_{1}]\wedge\varphi[{T}|_{c_{2}},c_{2}]
since the frontier of
\{r,c_{1},c_{2}\}
is
\{c_{1},c_{2}\}
.

We prove the stronger statement by induction on
\psi
.

•

When
\psi
is an atom then the result trivially follows by creating GJT

T
with a single node
r
, labeled by the atom, and fixing
N=\{r\}
.

•

When
\psi=\exists z.\psi^{\prime}
with
z\in\{x,y\}
we may assume
w.l.o.g. that
z\in\free(\psi^{\prime})
: if not then
\psi\equiv\psi^{\prime}
and the
result follows directly from the induction hypothesis on
\psi^{\prime}
. So, assume

z\in\free(\psi^{\prime})
. By induction hypothesis we have a binary QP
(T^{\prime},N^{\prime})

equivalent to
\psi^{\prime}
. We next consider two cases.

–

N^{\prime}
is a singleton,
N^{\prime}=\{r^{\prime}\}
with
r^{\prime}
the root of
T^{\prime}
.
We create the claimed binary QP
(T,N)
as follows: let
T
be obtained by
adjoining a new root
r
to
T^{\prime}
; label
r
by
\var(r^{\prime})\setminus\{z\}
;
and make
r^{\prime}
the only child of
r
. Take
N=\{r\}
. Then
(T,N)
remains
binary. Equivalence of
\varphi[T,N]
to
\psi
follows by induction
hypothesis and Lemma
15
.

–

Otherwise
N^{\prime}=\{r^{\prime},c_{1},c_{2}\}
with
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
the children of

r^{\prime}
in
T^{\prime}
;
\var(r^{\prime})=\emptyset
;
\var(c_{1})=\{x\}
;

\var(c_{2})=\{y\}
; and

\psi^{\prime}\equiv\varphi[{T^{\prime}}|_{c_{1}},c_{1}]\wedge\varphi[{T^{\prime}}|_{c_{2}},c_{2}]
. Since
z\in\{x,y\}
we have either

\var(c_{1})=\{z\}
or
\var(c_{2})=\{z\}
(but not both). Assume
w.l.o.g. that
\var(c_{1})=\{z\}
; the other case is similar. We create the
claimed QP
(T,N)
as follows: let
T
be obtained by removing
r^{\prime}
from

T^{\prime}
and replacing it with a new root,
r
, which has children
c_{1}
and

c_{2}
and which is labeled by
\var(c_{2})
. Since
\var(c_{1})
and
\var(c_{2})

were disjoint, this new tree satisfies the guardedness and connectedness
properties, and remains binary. Take
N=\{r\}
. Then

\var(N)=\var(c_{2})=\var(N^{\prime})\setminus\var(c_{1})=\free(\psi^{\prime})\setminus\{z\}=\free(\psi),

as desired. To see why

\varphi[T,N]\equiv\psi
first define
M=\{r,c_{1},c_{2}\}
. By
lemma
13
we obtain that

\varphi[T,N]\equiv\exists z.\varphi[T,M]
. Furthermore, since the
frontier of
M
is
\{c_{1},c_{2}\}
we know from
Lemma
14

that

\varphi[T,M]\equiv\varphi[{T}|_{c_{1}},c_{1}]\wedge\varphi[{T}|_{c_{2}},c_{2}]=\varphi[{T^{\prime}}|_{c_{1}},c_{1}]\wedge\varphi[{T^{\prime}}|_{c_{2}},c_{2}]\equiv\psi^{\prime}
. Hence

\varphi[T,N]\equiv\exists z.\psi^{\prime}=\psi.

•

Otherwise
\psi
is a conjunction,
\psi=\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}
. By induction
hypothesis, we have binary QPs
(T_{1},N_{1})
and
(T_{2},N_{2})
equivalent to
\psi_{1}

and
\psi_{2}
, respectively. We may assume w.l.o.g. that
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
have
no nodes in common, as we can always rename nodes otherwise. We may further
assume w.l.o.g. that

\var(T_{1})\cap\var(T_{2})\subseteq\var(N_{1})\cap\var(N_{2})
, i.e., that all
variables that are mentioned in both
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are also in

\var(N_{1})\cap\var(N_{2})
. Indeed, for every variable

z\in(\var(T_{1})\cap\var(T_{2}))\setminus(\var(N_{1})\cap\var(N_{2}))
we can
simply rename all occurrences of
z
in
T_{1}
to a new unique variable
z^{\prime}

that does not occur anywhere in
T_{2}
. Assume that we apply this reasoning to
all such variables
Z
and let
T^{\prime}_{1}
be the result of applying this renaming
on
T_{1}
. Note that
N_{1}
is still a connex subset of
T^{\prime}_{1}
. Moreover,

\varphi[T_{1},N_{1}]\equiv\varphi[T^{\prime}_{1},N_{1}]
: essentially we have only renamed
bound variables in the formula
\varphi[T_{1},N_{1}]
to obtain

\varphi[T^{\prime}_{1},N_{1}]
. So we can continue our reasoning with
(T^{\prime}_{1},N_{1})

instead of
(T_{1},N_{1})
and the former has no variables in common with
T_{2}
,
except those in
\var(N_{1})\cap\var(N_{2})
.

By our inductive hypothesis,
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are either singletons, or contain
exactly three nodes. We consider three cases.

–

Both
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are singletons, say
N_{1}=\{r_{1}\}
and

N_{2}=\{r_{2}\}
with
r_{1},r_{2}
the roots of
T_{1},T_{2}
respectively. In
particular, because
\varphi[T_{i},N_{i}]
is equivalent to
\psi_{i}
for

i=1,2
we necessarily have
\var(r_{i})=\free(\psi_{i})
. Construct the
claimed binary QP
(T,N)
by taking the (disjoint) union of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}

and adjoining a new root node
r
with children
r_{1},r_{2}
such that

\var(r)=\var(r_{1})\cap\var(r_{2})
. Since the only variables shared
between
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are in

\var(N_{1})\cap\var(N_{2})=\var(r_{1})\cap\var(r_{2})
it follows that
T

satisfies the connectedness property, and is hence a GJT. Moreover, let

N=\{r,r_{1},r_{2}\}
. Clearly

\var(N)=\var(r_{1})\cup\var(r_{2})=\free(\psi_{1})\cup\free(\psi_{2})=\free(\psi)\subseteq\{x,y\}.

Correctness follows by
Lemma
14
,
since
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are the frontier nodes of
N
:

\varphi[T,N]\equiv\varphi[{T}|_{r_{1}},r_{1}]\wedge\varphi[{T}|_{r_{2}},r_{2}]=\varphi[T_{1},r_{1}]\wedge\varphi[T_{2},r_{2}]\equiv\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}.

–

Both
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
contain exactly three nodes, say

N_{1}=\{r_{1},c^{1}_{1},c^{1}_{2}\}
and
N_{2}=\{r_{2},c^{2}_{1},c^{2}_{2}\}
with
r_{i}

the root node of
T_{i}
and
c^{i}_{j}
the two children of
r_{i}
in
T_{i}
, for

i,j\in\{1,2\}
. Furthermore,
\var(r^{i})=\emptyset
;

\var(c^{i}_{1})=\{x\}
and
\var(c^{i}_{2})=\{y\}
for
i=1,2
and
\psi_{1}
and

\psi_{2}
must themselves be conjunctions,

\psi_{1}=\psi^{1}_{1}\wedge\psi^{1}_{2}
and
\psi_{2}=\psi^{2}_{1}\wedge\psi^{2}_{2}
. By induction hypothesis,

\psi_{i}\equiv\varphi[T_{i},N_{i}]\equiv\varphi[{T_{i}}|_{c^{i}_{1}},c^{i}_{1}]\wedge\varphi[{T_{i}}|_{c^{i}_{2}},c^{i}_{2}]

(1)

We construct the claimed QP
(T,N)
by taking
T
as follows, and
N=\{r,d_{1},d_{2}\}
.

r;\emptyset
d_{1};\{x\}
c^{1}_{1}
T^{1}_{1}
c^{2}_{1}
T^{2}_{1}
d_{2};\{y\}
c^{1}_{2}
T^{1}_{2}
c^{2}_{2}
T^{2}_{2}

Here
r
,
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
are new nodes with
\var(r)=\emptyset
;

\var(d_{1})=\{x\}
, and
\var(d_{2})=\{y\}
, and

T^{i}_{j}={T_{i}}|_{c^{i}_{j}}
for
i=1,2
. It is straightforward to
verify that
T
satisfies the guardedness and connectedness properties. It
is hence a GJT. To see why
\varphi[T,N]\equiv\psi
, first define

M=N\cup\{c^{1}_{1},c^{1}_{2},c^{2}_{1},c^{2}_{2}\}
. Note that

\var(M)=\var(N)
. Hence, by
Lemma
13
, we have

\varphi[T,N]\equiv\varphi[T,M]
. Then, because the frontier of
M
is

\{c^{1}_{1},c^{1}_{2},c^{2}_{1},c^{2}_{2}\}
we have by
Lemma
14

that
\varphi[T,M]\equiv\bigwedge_{i,j\in\{1,2\}}\varphi[{T}|_{c^{i}_{j}},c^{i}_{j}]\equiv\bigwedge_{i,j\in\{1,2\}}\varphi[{T_{i}}|_{c^{i}_{j}},c^{i}_{j}]\equiv\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}
,
where the last equivalence follows from (
1
).

–

One of
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
has exactly three nodes; the other has one node. Assume w.lo.g. that
N_{1}=\{r_{1}\}
and
N_{2}=\{r_{2},c_{1},c_{2}\}
. We then construct the claimed binary QP
(T,N)
as follows. If
\var(r_{1})=\{x,y\}
then we take
T
as follows, where
r,r^{\prime}
are new nodes with
\var(r)=\var(r^{\prime})=\var(r_{1})=\{x,y\}
and
N=\{r\}
. Moreover,
T^{2}_{j}={T_{2}}|_{c_{j}}
for
j=1,2
.
r
r^{\prime}
r_{1}
T_{1}
c_{1}
T^{2}_{1}
c_{2}
T^{2}_{2}

When
\var(r_{1})\not=\{x,y\}
then either
\var(r_{1})\subseteq\var(c_{1})
or
\var(r_{1})\subseteq\var(c_{2})
(or both). If
\var(r_{1})\subseteq\var(c_{1})
then we take
T
as above but set
\var(r)=\emptyset
;
\var(r^{\prime})=\var(c_{1})=\{x\}
; and
N=\{r,r_{1},c_{2}\}
. The case where
\var(r_{1})\subseteq\var(c^{2}_{2})
but
\var(r_{1})\not\subseteq\var(c_{1})
is similar. In all cases, equivalence with
\psi
follows from the same reasoning used earlier.

6.
The free-connex fragment of matlang queries

Define
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
to be the class of all
\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions generated by the grammar:

e\ \ \Coloneqq\ \ \mathbf{A}\ \mid\ \mathbf{1}^{\alpha}\ \mid\ \mathbf{I}^{\alpha}\ \mid\ e^{T}\ \mid\ e_{1}\times e_{2}\ \mid\ e_{1}\odot e_{2}\ \mid\ e_{1}\cdot v_{2}\ \mid\ v_{1}\cdot e_{2}

where
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions with type
(\alpha,1)
or
(1,\alpha)
. In other words, matrix multiplication
e_{1}\cdot e_{2}
is only allowed when at least one of
e_{1}
or
e_{2}
has a row or column vector type.

In this section, we prove the following correspondence.

Theorem 21
.

\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
are equally expressive.

Combined with Theorem
10
we obtain the correspondence with binary free-connex CQ:

Corollary 22
.

\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and binary free-connex CQs are equally expressive.

We prove both directions of Theorem
21
separately.

6.1.
From
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
to
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}

We first simulate
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
queries by means of
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
queries. In order to achieve this, free variables of formulas need to describe row and column indexes of expressions. Moreover, it is crucial to know which free variable will correspond to the row index and which free variable will correspond to the column index. For this reason we assume the existence of an arbitary
canonical order

\prec
over
\mathsf{FO}
variables, where
x\prec y
denotes that
x
strictly precedes
y
in this order.

Fix a vocabulary
\Voc
. We first define the notion of when a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression simulates a
formula
over
\Voc
instead of a query.
Let
\psi\in\mathsf{FO}^{+}
over
\Voc
. Let
\Mat
be a relational-to-matrix schema encoding on
\Voc
such that
\Mat\vdash\psi\colon\tau
. We say that
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
sentence
e
simulates
\psi
w.r.t.
\Mat
if the
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\Mat(\textit{ans})\coloneqq e
simulates the query
\textit{ans}(\overline{x})\leftarrow\psi
w.r.t.
\Mat
, where

(1)

\overline{x}
is a tuple containing exactly the free variables of
\psi
, without repetition and canonically ordered: i.e., if
\overline{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})
then
x_{1}\prec x_{2}
.

(2)

ans
is an arbitrary relation symbol, not occurring in
\varphi
, of the same arity as
\free(\varphi)
such that
\texttt{type}(\Mat(\textit{ans}))=\tau(\overline{x})
.

So, in the second bullet if
\varphi
has two free variables then
\texttt{type}(\Mat(\textit{ans}))=(\tau(x_{1}),\tau(x_{2}))
; if
\varphi
has one free variable then
\texttt{type}(\Mat(\textit{ans}))=(\tau(x),1)
; and if
\varphi
has zero free variables then
\texttt{type}(\Mat(\textit{ans}))=(1,1)
. In particular, due to well-typedness, when
\varphi
has two free variables
\Mat(\textit{ans})
is consistent with dimensions
\tau(x_{1})^{\textit{db}}\times\tau(x_{2})^{\textit{db}}
; with
\tau(x)^{\textit{db}}\times 1
when
\varphi
has one free variable; and with
1\times 1
when
\varphi
has no free variable, for every database
db
consistent with
\Mat
.
Further, we note that, while relation symbol
ans
is fixed arbitrarily, it is clear that if
e
simulates
\varphi
w.r.t. one particular choice of
ans
, then it simulates it w.r.t. all valid choices.

It is important to stress that (1) in the query
\textit{ans}(\overline{x})\leftarrow\varphi
no variable is ever repeated in the head; and (2) if
\varphi
hence has a single free variable, and hence computes a unary relation, then the simulating
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression will always simulate it by means of a
column
vector. We fix this merely to simplify the proof, and could also have fixed it to be a row vector instead.

Proposition 23
.

Let
\psi\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
over
\Voc
.
Let
\Mat
be a relational-to-matrix schema encoding on
\Voc
such that

\Mat\vdash\psi\colon\tau
. Then there exists an expression
e\in\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}

that simulates
\psi
w.r.t.
\Mat
.

Proof 6.1
.

Fix two distinct variables
x_{1}\prec x_{2}
. We may assume without loss of generality that the
only variables occurring in
\psi
are
x_{1},x_{2}
. We prove the statement by induction on
\psi
. For every relation symbol
R
, we denote
\Mat(R)
simply by
\mathbf{R}
. In particular,
\mathbf{ans}=\Mat(\textit{ans})
.

First, when
\psi=a
and
a
is a relational atom we have multiple cases.

•

Atom
a
is a binary atom, and its variables occur in canonical order, i.e.
a=R(x_{1},x_{2})
. Then take
e:=\mathbf{R}
.

•

Atom
a
is a binary atom, but its variables occur in reverse canonical order, i.e.,
a=R(x_{2},x_{1})
. Then take
e:=\mathbf{R}^{T}
.

•

Atom
a
is a binary atom that mentions the same variable twice,

a=R(y,y)
for some
y\in\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
. Note that
\mathbf{ans}=\Mat(\textit{ans})
will be of column vector type
(\tau(y),1)
since
\overline{x}=(y)
. Also note that, because of well-typedness,
\mathbf{R}
must be of type
(\tau(y),\tau(y))
, i.e.,
\mathbf{R}
is a square matrix. Then construct
e
as follows. First consider the expression
e^{\prime}:=\mathbf{I}^{\tau(y)}\odot\mathbf{R}.
This selects from
\mathbf{R}
all the entries on the diagonal, and sets to
\mymathbb{0}
all other entries. Then take
e:=e^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{1}^{\tau(y)}
, wich converts the diagonal entries into a column vector.

•

Atom
a
is a unary atom of the form
a=R(y)
with
y\in\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
. We distinguish two subcases.

–

\mathbf{R}
has column vector type. In that case, take
e:=\mathbf{R}
.

–

\mathbf{R}
has row vector type. Because
\mathbf{ans}
always has
column vector type by definition, we convert the input row vector into a
column vector by taking
e:=\mathbf{R}^{T}
.

•

Atom
a
is a nullary atom,
a=R()
. Then
\mathbf{R}
necessarily has type
1\times 1
, as does
\mathbf{ans}
. So, it suffices to take
e:=\mathbf{R}
.

Second, when
\psi
is comparison of the form
x\leq\alpha
then the simulating expression
e
is
\mathbf{1}^{\alpha}
. This expression has type
(\tau(x),1)
, which conforms to the desired type of the definition since
\tau(x)=\alpha
.

Third, when
\psi=\exists y.\psi_{1}
we may assume w.l.o.g. that

y\in\free(\psi_{1})
since otherwise
\psi\equiv\psi_{1}
and the result
follows directly from the induction hypothesis. So, assume

y\in\free(\psi_{1})\subseteq\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
. There are two cases.

•

If
\free(\psi_{1})=\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
then
e_{1}\colon(\tau(x_{1}),\tau(x_{2}))
simulates
\psi_{1}
. If
y=x_{2}
then we take
e=e_{1}\cdot\mathbf{1}^{\tau(y)}
to simulate
\psi
w.r.t.
\Mat
. If
y=x_{1}
then we take
e=e_{1}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{1}^{\tau(y)}
to simulate
\psi
w.r.t.
\Mat
.
Note that in both cases the simulating expression has type

(\tau(x),1)
with
x
the unique variable in

\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\setminus\{y\}
, as desired.

•

If
\free(\psi_{1})=\{y\}
, then
e_{1}\colon(\tau(y),1)
simulates
\psi_{1}
and thus
e_{1}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{1}^{\tau(y)}\colon(1,1)
simulates
\psi
w.r.t.
\Mat
.

The fourth and final case is when
\psi=\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}
.
By inductive hypothesis there exists
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions
e_{1},e_{2}
that simulate
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
w.r.t.
\Mat
, respectively. If
\free(\psi_{1})=\free(\psi_{2})
then the simulating expression is simply

e_{1}\odot e_{2}
. It will be of type
(\tau(x_{1}),\tau(x_{2}))
,

(\tau(x_{1}),1)
,
(\tau(x_{2}),1)
or
(1,1)
depending if the free variables
are
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
,
\{x_{1}\}
,
\{x_{2}\}
or
\emptyset
, respectively. When
\free(\psi_{1})\not=\free(\psi_{2})
, we make the following case distinction.

(1)

If
\free(\psi_{1})=\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
then
e_{1}\colon(\tau(x_{1}),\tau(x_{2}))
.

(a)

If
\free(\psi_{2})=\{x_{1}\}
then
e_{2}\colon(\tau(x_{1}),1)
and we take
e:=e_{1}\odot\left(e_{2}\cdot\left(\mathbf{1}^{\tau(x_{2})}\right)^{T}\right).

(b)

If
\free(\psi_{2})=\{x_{2}\}
then
e_{2}\colon(\tau(x_{2}),1)
and we take
e:=e_{1}\odot\left(\mathbf{1}^{\tau(x_{1})}\cdot e_{2}^{T}\right).

(c)

If
\free(\psi_{2})=\emptyset
then
e_{2}\colon(1,1)
and the simulating expression is
e_{2}\times e_{1}
.

(2)

If
\free(\psi_{1})=\{x_{1}\}
then
e_{1}\colon(\tau(x_{1}),1)
.

(a)

If
\free(\psi_{2})=\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
then we reason analogous to case (1)(b).

(b)

If
\free(\psi_{2})=\{x_{2}\}
then
e_{2}\colon(\tau(x_{2}),1)
and define
e:=\left(e_{1}\cdot\left(\mathbf{1}^{\tau(x_{2})}\right)^{T}\right)\odot\left(\mathbf{1}^{\tau(x_{1})}\cdot e_{2}^{T}\right).

(c)

If
\free(\psi_{2})=\emptyset
then
e_{2}\colon(1,1)
and the simulating expression is
e_{2}\times e_{1}
.

(3)

If
\free(\psi_{1})=\{x_{2}\}
we reason analogous to case (2).

(4)

If
\free(\psi_{1})=\emptyset
then
\free(\psi_{2})\not=\emptyset
and we reason analogous to the cases above where
\free(\psi_{2})=\emptyset
but
\free(\psi_{1})\not=\emptyset
.

Finally, we extend Proposition
23
from formulas to queries.

Corollary 24
.

Let
Q
be an
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
query over
\Voc
and let
\Mat
be a relational-to-matrix schema encoding on
\Voc(Q)
such that

\Mat\vdash Q\colon\tau
. Then there exists
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\mathbf{Q}

that simulates
Q
w.r.t.
\Mat
.

Proof 6.2
.

Let
Q\colon H\leftarrow\psi
be a
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
query over
\Voc
and let
\Mat
be a relational-to-matrix schema encoding on
\Voc(Q)
such that
\Mat\vdash\psi\colon\tau
. By Proposition
23
there exists
e\in\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}

that simulates
\psi
w.r.t.
\Mat
. Let
\Mat(H)=\mathbf{H}
. We derive a
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\mathbf{Q}\colon\mathbf{H}:=\overline{e}
that simulates
Q
as follows.

•

When
\overline{x}=(x,y)
, if
x\prec y
then define
\overline{e}:=e
, otherwise define
\overline{e}:=e^{T}
.

•

When
\overline{x}=(x,x)
then
Q
outputs the encoding of a square matrix, where the value of diagonal entry
(x,x)
is computed by
\psi(x)
. To simulate this query, we observe that
e\colon(\tau(x),1)
is a column vector that simulates
\psi
. We first compute the square matrix of type
(\tau(x),\tau(x))
with the vector
e
in all its columns as
e\cdot\left(\mathbf{1}^{\tau(x)}\right)^{T}.
Then set all non-diagonal entries of the former expression to zero, by taking
\overline{e}\coloneqq\mathbf{I}^{\tau(x)}\odot\left(e\cdot\left(\mathbf{1}^{\tau(x)}\right)^{T}\right).

•

When
\overline{x}=(x)
we have
e\colon(\tau(x),1)
. If
\mathbf{H}\colon(\alpha,1)
define
\overline{e}:=e
otherwise if
\mathbf{H}\colon(1,\alpha)
define
\overline{e}:=e^{T}.

•

When
\overline{x}=()
we have
e\colon(1,1)
. Then define
\overline{e}:=e
.

6.2.
From
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
to
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}

To establish the converse direction of Theorem
21
we first define when an
\mathsf{FO}^{+}

formula
simulates a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
sentence instead of a query.
Let
e\colon(\alpha,\beta)
be a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
sentence over
\Sch
let
\Rel
be a matrix-to-relational schema encoding on
\Sch
. We say that
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formula
\psi
simulates
e
w.r.t.
\Rel
if
\psi
has exactly two free variables
x\prec y
and for every matrix instance
\mathcal{I}
and all
i,j
we

\llceil{\psi}\rrfloor_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}(x\mapsto i,y\mapsto j)=\begin{cases}\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})_{i,j}&\text{ if }1\leq i\leq\mathcal{I}(\alpha),1\leq j\leq\mathcal{I}(\beta)\\
\mymathbb{0}&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}.

Let
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
denote the two-variable fragment of
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}
where equality atoms are allowed (subject to being safe), as long as only two variables are used in the entire formula. For proving the direction from
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
to
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
, we first do the proof by using equality, to later show how to remove it.

Proposition 25
.

Let
e
be a
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression over
\Sch
and let
\Rel
be a matrix-to-relational schema encoding on
\Sch
. There exists
\psi_{e}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
that simulates
e
w.r.t.
\Rel
.

Proof 6.3
.

Let
e\in\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and fix two distinct variables
x\prec y
. We build the formula
\psi_{e}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
that simulates
e
w.r.t.
\Rel
by induction on
e
. In particular, we build
\psi_{e}
such that
\free(\psi_{e})=\{x,y\}
.
For a matrix symbol
\mathbf{A}
, let us denote
\Rel(A)
simply as
A
.

(1)

If
e=\mathbf{A}
then take:

•

\psi_{e}\coloneqq A(x,y)
when
\texttt{type}(\mathbf{A})=(\alpha,\beta)
;
\texttt{type}(\mathbf{A})=(1,1)
;
A
is binary and
\texttt{type}(\mathbf{A})=(\alpha,1)
or
\texttt{type}(\mathbf{A})=(1,\beta)
.

•

\psi_{e}\coloneqq A(x)\wedge y\leq 1
if
A
is unary and
\texttt{type}(\mathbf{A})=(\alpha,1)
.

•

\psi_{e}\coloneqq x\leq 1\wedge A(y)
if
A
is unary and
\texttt{type}(\mathbf{A})=(1,\beta)
.

(2)

If
e=\mathbf{1}^{\alpha}\colon(\alpha,1)
then take
\psi_{e}\coloneqq x\leq\alpha\wedge y\leq 1
.

(3)

If
e=\mathbf{I}^{\alpha}\colon(\alpha,\alpha)
then take
\psi_{e}\coloneqq x\leq\alpha\wedge y\leq\alpha\wedge x=y
. This will be the only case where we include an equality atom.

(4)

If
e=(e^{\prime})^{T}\colon(\beta,\alpha)
then take
\psi_{e}\coloneqq\psi_{e^{\prime}}[x\leftrightarrow y]
where
\psi_{e^{\prime}}[x\leftrightarrow y]
denotes the formula obtained from
\psi_{e^{\prime}}
by swapping
x
and
y
. (I.e., simultaneously replacing all occurrences of
x
—free and bound—with
y
and all occurrences of
y
with
x
.)

(5)

If
e=e_{1}\times e_{2}
with
e_{1}\colon(1,1)
and
e_{2}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
we reason as follows. First observe that because
\psi_{e_{1}}
simulates
e_{1}
and because
e_{1}
always outputs scalar matrices,
\llceil{\psi_{e_{1}}}\rrfloor_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}(x\mapsto i,y\mapsto j)=\mymathbb{0}
whenever
i\not=1
or
j\not=1
. We conclude that therefore,
\exists x,y.\psi_{e_{1}}
will always return
\llbracket e_{1}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})(1,1)
when evaluated on
\Rel(\mathcal{I})
. Hence, we take
\psi_{e}\coloneqq\left(\exists x,y.\psi_{e_{1}}\right)\wedge\psi_{e_{2}}
.

(6)

If
e=e_{1}\odot e_{2}
then take
\psi_{e}\coloneqq\psi_{e_{1}}\wedge\psi_{e_{2}}
.

(7)

If
e=e_{1}\cdot v_{2}
with
e_{1}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
and with
v_{2}\colon(\beta,1)
evaluating to a column vector, we reason as follows. First observe that because
\psi_{v_{2}}
simulates
v_{2}
and because
v_{2}
always outputs column vectors,
\llceil{\psi_{v_{2}}}\rrfloor_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}(x\mapsto i,y\mapsto j)=\mymathbb{0}
whenever
j\not=1
. Let
\varphi_{v_{2}}=\psi_{v_{2}}[x\leftrightarrow y]
be the
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
formula obtained from
\psi_{v_{2}}
by simultaneously replacing all occurrences (free and bound) of
x
by
y
, and all occurrences of
y
by
x
. Then
\llceil{\varphi_{v_{2}}}\rrfloor_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}(y\mapsto i,x\mapsto j)=\mymathbb{0}
whenever
j\not=1
.
We conclude that therefore

\llbracket v_{2}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})_{i,1}=\llceil{\exists x.\varphi_{v_{2}}}\rrfloor_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}(y\mapsto i)\text{ for all }\mathcal{I}\text{ and }i.

(2)

Next observe that
e
also always outputs a column vector, and that

\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})_{i,1}=\llbracket e_{1}\cdot v_{2}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})_{i,1}=\bigoplus_{k}\,\llbracket e_{1}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})_{i,k}\odot\llbracket v_{2}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I},\mu)_{k,1}

Take
\psi^{\prime}=\exists y.\left(\psi_{e_{1}}(x,y)\wedge\varphi^{\prime}_{v_{2}}(y)\right)
where
\varphi^{\prime}_{v_{2}}=\exists x.\varphi_{v_{2}}
. Then
\psi^{\prime}
is in
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
. Because
\psi_{e_{1}}
simulates
e_{1}
and because of (
2
) it follows that
\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})_{i,1}=\llceil{\psi^{\prime}}\rrfloor_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}(x\mapsto i)
for all
\mathcal{I}
and
i
. Hence, we take
\psi_{e}:=\psi^{\prime}\wedge y\leq 1
to simulate
e
.

(8)

The case
e=v_{1}\cdot e_{2}
is similar to the previous case.

To establish our desired result at the level of queries we require the following
two lemmas that will help to remove the equalities from
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
. The proofs can be found in Appendix
C
.

Lemma 26
.

Let
\varphi
be a
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula (hence, without equality) using only the
distinct variables
x,y
such that

\free(\varphi)\cap\{x,y\}\not=\emptyset
. Let
\psi
be the
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}

formula
\exists x.(\varphi\wedge x=y)
. Note that
\free(\psi)=\{y\}
. There exists an
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula
\psi^{\prime}
equivalent to
\psi
.

Lemma 27
.

For every
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
formula
\psi
there exists an equivalent
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
formula
\psi^{\prime}
where equality atoms only occur at the top level: for every subformula
\exists z.\phi^{\prime}
of
\psi^{\prime}
it holds that
\phi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
does not contain equality atoms.

Finally, we can prove the direction from
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
to
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
.

Corollary 28
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}
be an
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query over
\Sch
and let
\Rel
be a matrix-to-relational schema encoding on
\Sch
. There exists an
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
query
Q
that simulates
\mathbf{Q}
w.r.t.
\Rel
.

Proof 6.4
.

Let
\mathbf{H}\coloneqq e
be an
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query and let
H=\Rel(\mathbf{H})
. By
Proposition
25
there exists

\psi\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
that simulates
e
w.r.t.
\Rel
. By construction,
\psi

has two distinct free variables. Assume that
x,y
are these two distinct
variables, and
x\prec y
.
There are three cases to consider.

(1)
H
is a binary relation. By
Lemma
27
there exists

\psi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
that is equivalent to
\psi
and where equality atoms only
occur at the top level. Because
\psi
has
\{x,y\}
as free
variables, so does
\psi^{\prime}
. If no equality atom occurs in
\psi^{\prime}
then we take
Q
to be the query
H(x,y)\leftarrow\psi^{\prime}
. If some equality atom does occur in
\psi^{\prime}
then we may observe w.l.o.g. that the equality is
x=y
since the equalities
x=x
and
y=y
are trivial and can always be removed. As such,
\psi^{\prime}\equiv\psi^{\prime\prime}\wedge x=y
with
\psi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
and at least one of
x,y
appearing free in
\psi^{\prime\prime}
. Assume w.l.o.g. that
x
occurs free in
\psi^{\prime\prime}
. Then the query
H(x,x)\leftarrow\exists y.\psi^{\prime}
simulates
\mathbf{Q}
w.r.t.
\Rel
. Although this is formally a query in
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
, we can we can next apply
Lemma
26
to
\psi^{\prime\prime}
to obtain an equivalent formula in
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
for the body
\exists y.\psi^{\prime}\equiv\exists y.(\psi^{\prime\prime}\wedge x=y)
, yielding the desired result.

(2)
H
is a unary relation. Assume that
\mathbf{H}
is of column vector type

(\alpha,1)
; the reasoning when
\mathbf{H}
has row vector type is similar. Take

\varphi=\exists y.\psi
. Because
\psi
simulates
e
and because
e

always outputs column vectors,

\llceil{\psi}\rrfloor_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}(x\mapsto i,y\mapsto j)=\mymathbb{0}
whenever

j\not=1
, for every
\mathcal{I}
. We conclude that therefore

\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})(i,1)=\llceil{\exists y.\psi}\rrfloor_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}(x\mapsto i)
for all
i

and
\mathcal{I}
. As such, the query
Q^{\prime}\colon H(x)\leftarrow\varphi
simulates

\mathbf{Q}
. While
Q^{\prime}
is a query in
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
, we can next apply
Lemma
27
to
\varphi
to obtain an
equivalent formula
\varphi^{\prime}\equiv\varphi
in which equality only occurs at
the top level. Note that, since
\varphi
has only
\{x\}
as free variables,
so does
\varphi^{\prime}
. Hence, any equality that occurs at the top level in

\varphi^{\prime}
must be of the form
x=x
, which is trivial and can therefore be
removed. After removing such trivial equalities, take

Q\colon H(x)\leftarrow\varphi^{\prime}
as the simulating query.

(3)
H
is a nullary relation. The reason is entirely similar to the previous case.

7.
Q-hierarchical conjunctive queries

We next turn our attention to specializing the
correspondence between
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and CQs given by
Corollary
9
to
q-hierarchical

CQs
[
BDG07
,
Bra13
]
.
Q-hierarchical CQs are relevant since, over the Boolean semiring, they capture the free-connex CQs that, in addition to supporting constant delay enumeration after linear time preprocessing, have the property that every single-tuple update (insertion or deletion) to the input database can be processed in constant time, after which the enumeration of the updated query result can again proceed with constant delay
[
BKS17
]
.
Formally, q-hierarchical CQs are defined as follows.
Let
Q\colon H(\overline{x})\leftarrow\exists\overline{y}.a_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge a_{n}
be a CQ. For every variable
x
, define
\atoms(x)
to be the set
\{a_{i}\mid x\in\var(a_{i})\}
of
relational atoms that mention
x
. Note that, contrary to acyclic queries, here we make the distinction between relational atoms and inequalities. Then
Q
is
q-hierarchical
if for any two variables
x,y
the following hold:

(1)

\atoms(x)\subseteq\atoms(y)
or
\atoms(x)\supseteq\atoms(y)
or
\atoms(x)\cap\atoms(y)=\emptyset
, and

(2)

if
x\in\overline{x}
and
\atoms(x)\subsetneq\atoms(y)
then
y\in\overline{x}
.

For example,
H(x)\leftarrow\exists y.A(x,y)\wedge U(x)
is
q-hierarchical. By contrast, the variant

H(x)\leftarrow\exists y.A(x,y)\wedge U(y)
is not q-hierarchical, as it
violates the second condition. Furthermore,

H(x,y)\leftarrow A(x,y)\wedge U(x)\wedge V(y)
violates the first condition,
and is also not q-hierarchical. Note that all these examples are
free-connex. We refer to q-hierarchical CQs simply as qh-CQs.

In this section, we first prove a correspondence between qh-CQs and a fragment of
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
. Using this, we characterize the fragment of
\mathsf{MATLANG}
that corresponds to qh-CQs in Section
8
.
Concretely, we prove the following result in this section.
We say that a
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula
\varphi
is
simple
if every subformula of the form
\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
in
\varphi
satisfies
\free(\varphi_{1})=\free(\varphi_{2})
. We denote by
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
the class of all simple
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas.
Let
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k}\in\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
with
k\geq 1
such that
\free(\varphi_{i})\subseteq\{x,y\}
for two distinct variables
\{x,y\}
. We say that
\varphi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{k}
is a
hierarchical conjunction
if
\{\{x,y\},\{x\},\{y\}\}\not\subseteq\{\free(\varphi_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
.
For example,
R(x,y)\wedge S(y)\wedge U()
is a hierarchical conjunction, but
R(x,y)\wedge S(y)\wedge T(x)
is not.
We denote by
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
all hierarchical conjunctions of
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas.
Observe that, by definition,
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}\subseteq\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
. As usual, an
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
query is a query with body
\psi
such that
\psi\in\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
.

Theorem 29
.

Q-hierarchical binary CQs and
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
queries are equally expressive.

To prove Theorem
29
we first define
guarded query plans
in Section
7.1
. Similar to how query plans allowed us to prove equivalence between fc-CQs and
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
in Section
5
, we use guarded query plans to prove equivalence between qh-CQs and
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
. We show that
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
is included in qh-CQs in Section
7.2
, and the converse in Section
7.3
.

7.1.
Guarded query plans.

A generalized join tree (GJT)
T
(see Section
5.2
) is
guarded

if for every node
n
and every child
c
of
n
it holds that
\var(n)\subseteq\var(c)
, i.e., in a guarded GJT every child is a guard of its parent. A query plan (QP) is guarded if its GJT is guarded.
2
2

2

Guarded GJTs and QPs are called
simple
in
[
IUV+20
]
.
The following proposition is a particular case of the results shown in
[
IUV17
,
IUV+20
]
.

Proposition 30
.

A CQ
Q
is q-hierarchical if and only if it has a guarded query plan.

For later use we observe the following property. Its proof is completely analogous to the proof of Lemma
12
.

Lemma 31
.

For every guarded QP there exists an equivalent guarded QP such that for any node
n
with two children
c_{1},c_{2}

it holds that
var(n)=var(c_{1})=var(c_{2})
.

7.2.
From q-hierarchical CQs to
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
queries

Lemma 32
.

For every guarded QP
(T,r)
such that
\varphi[T,r]
is binary, there exists a
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula
\varphi
equivalent to
\varphi[T,r]
.

Proof 7.1
.

By Lemma
31
we may assume w.l.o.g. that

T
is such that for every node
n
with two children
c_{1},c_{2}
it holds

\var(c_{1})=\var(n)
and
\var(c_{2})=\var(n)
. In the proof of Proposition
18
we showed by induction on
T
that we can construct an
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula
\varphi
equivalent to
\varphi[T,r]
. It is readily verified that when
T
is guarded this construction yields a simple formula.

Proposition 33
.

For every guarded QP
(T,N)
such that
\varphi[T,N]
is binary, there exists an
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula
\psi
equivalent to
\varphi[T,N]
.

Proof 7.2
.

By Lemma
31
we may assume w.l.o.g. that
T

is such that for every node
n
with two children
c_{1},c_{2}
we have

\var(c_{1})=\var(c_{2})=\var(n)
.
Let
F=\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{l}\}
be
the frontier of
N
. Let
T_{1},\dots,T_{l}
be the subtrees of
T
rooted at

r_{1},\dots,r_{l}
, respectively. By
Lemma
32
, there are

\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{l}
equivalent to

\varphi[T_{1},r_{1}],\ldots,\varphi[T_{l},r_{l}]
, respectively. By
Lemma
14
,

\varphi[T,N]\equiv\varphi[T_{1},r_{1}]\wedge\dots\wedge\varphi[T_{l},r_{l}]\equiv\psi_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\psi_{l}
. Because
\varphi[T,N]
is binary, there exists a set
\{x,y\}
of two distinct variables such that
\var(N)\subseteq\{x,y\}
. It follows that
\free(\psi_{i})=\var(r_{i})\subseteq\var(N)\subseteq\{x,y\}
for every
i
. It remains to show that
\psi_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\psi_{l}
is a hierarchical conjunction. To that end, assume, for the purpose of obtaining a contradiction, that
\{\{x,y\},\{x\},\{y\}\}\subseteq\{\free(\psi_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
. Because
\free(\psi_{i})=\var(r_{i})
for every
i
there must exist nodes
r_{x,y}
,
r_{x}
and
r_{y}
in
F
that are labeled by
\{x,y\}
,
\{x\}
and
\{y\}
respectively. Now note that, because
T
is guarded and satisfies Lemma
31
, all ancestors of
r_{x}
(resp.
r_{y}
) can only contain
x
(resp.
y
) in their label, but not
y
(resp
x
). At the same time, because of the connectedness property, there is an ancestor
a
of both
r_{x,y}
and
r_{x}
that must contain
x
, implying that
var(a)=\{x\}
. Similarly, there is an ancestor
b
of both
r_{x,y}
and
r_{y}
that must contain
y
, implying that
\var(b)=\{y\}
. Now note that
a
and
b
are both ancestors of
r_{x,y}
, so either
a
is an ancestor of
b
, or the converse holds. Assume
a
is an ancestor of
b
. But then
a
is also an ancestor of
r_{y}
and
a
has
x
in its label, which yields the desired contradiction. The case where
b
is an ancestor of
a
is similar.

7.3.
From
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
queries to q-hierarchical CQs

Lemma 34
.

For every
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula
\varphi
there exists a guarded QP
(T,r)
such that
\varphi[T,r]
is binary and equivalent to
\varphi
.

Proof 7.3
.

The proof is analogous to the proof of Proposition
20
. Fix two distinct variables
x
and
y
and formula
\varphi\in\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
. Because
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas use at most two variables we may assume w.l.o.g. that the only variables occurring in
\varphi
are
x
and
y
. We prove by induction on
\varphi
that there exists a guarded query plan
(T,r)
such that
\varphi[T,r]\equiv\varphi
.

When
\varphi
is an atom (relational or inequality) then the result trivially follow by creating a GJT
T
with single node
r
, labeled by the atom, and fixing
N=\{r\}
.

When
\varphi=\exists z.\varphi^{\prime}
with
z\in\{x,y\}
we may assume w.l.o.g. that
z\in\free(\varphi^{\prime})
: if not then
\varphi\equiv\varphi^{\prime}
and the result follows directly from the induction hypothesis on

\varphi^{\prime}
. So, assume
z\in\free(\varphi)
. By induction hypothesis we have a guarded and binary QP

(T^{\prime},r^{\prime})
equivalent to
\varphi^{\prime}
with
r^{\prime}
the root of
T^{\prime}
. Let

T
be obtained by adjoining a new root
r
to
T^{\prime}
; label
r
by
\var(r^{\prime})\setminus\{z\}
; and make
r^{\prime}
the only child of
r
. Take
N=\{r\}
. Then

(T,N)
remains binary and guarded. Equivalence of
\varphi[T,N]
to

\psi
follows by induction hypothesis and
Lemma
15
.

Otherwise
\varphi
is a conjunction,

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
. By induction hypothesis, we have
binary and guarded QPs
(T_{1},r_{1})
and
(T_{2},r_{2})
equivalent to
\psi_{1}

and
\psi_{2}
, respectively with
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
the roots of

T_{1},T_{2}
. Using the same reasoning as in the proof of
Proposition
20
we may assume w.l.o.g. that
T_{1}

and
T_{2}
have no nodes in common, and that moreover

\var(T_{1})\cap\var(T_{2})\subseteq\var(N_{1})\cap\var(N_{2})
, i.e., that
all variables that are mentioned in both
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are also in

\var(N_{1})\cap\var(N_{2})
. Because
(T_{i},r_{i})
is equivalent to
\varphi_{i}
we have
\var(r_{i})=\free(\varphi_{i})
, for
i=1,2
. Then, because
\varphi
is simple, we necessarily have
\var(r_{1})=\free(\varphi_{1})=\free(\varphi_{2})=\var(r_{2})
. As such,
\var(r_{1})=\var(r_{2})
.
We construct the claimed QP
(T,r)
by taking the disjoint union of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
and adjoining a new root
r
with children
r_{1},r_{2}
such that
\var(r)=\var(r_{1})=\var(r_{2})
. It is readily verified that
T
satisfies the connectedness property and is hence a GJT. Moreover,
T
is guarded. Correctness follows by Corollary
16
.

Proposition 35
.

For every
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula
\psi
there exists a guarded QP
(T,N)
such that
\varphi[T,N]
is binary and equivalent to
\psi
.

Proof 7.4
.

Let
x,y
be distinct FO variables, let
\psi=\varphi_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\varphi_{k}\in\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
with

\varphi_{i}\in\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
and
\free(\varphi_{i})\subseteq\{x,y\}
for

i=1,\ldots,k
. Consider the set
F=\{\free(\varphi_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
. Note that
F\not=\emptyset
since it includes the root. Furthermore, because
\psi
is a hierarchical conjunction,
\{\{x,y\},\{x\},\{y\}\}\not\subseteq F
. There are hence
2^{2^{2}}
-3 = 13 possibilities for
F
. We construct
(T,N)
by case analysis on
F
. We only illustrate one case; the other cases are similar.

Assume
F=\{\{x,y\},\{x\},\emptyset\}
. Define, for each
S\in F
the formula
\varphi_{S}
to be the conjunction of all
\varphi_{i}
with
\free(\varphi_{i})=S
. Then

\psi\equiv\varphi_{x,y}\wedge\varphi_{x}\wedge\varphi_{\emptyset}
. Observe that each
\varphi_{S}
is simple. Hence by
Proposition
34
there exist
guarded query plans
(T_{x,y},r_{x,y}),(T_{x},r_{x})
and

(T_{\emptyset},r_{\emptyset})
equivalent to

\varphi_{x,y},\varphi_{x},\varphi_{\emptyset}
, respectively. In
particular,
\var(r_{x,y})=\{x,y\}
,
\var(r_{x})=\{x\}
and

\var(r_{\emptyset})=\emptyset
. We may assume w.l.o.g. that the GJTs

T_{x,y},T_{x}
and
T_{\emptyset}
do not have nodes in common. We construct the
claimed guarded QP
(T,N)
by constructing
T
as follows and setting
N=\{r,r^{\prime},r_{x,y},r_{x},r_{\emptyset}\}
.

r;\emptyset
r^{\prime};\{x\}
r_{x,y}
T_{x,y}
r_{x}
T_{x}
r_{\emptyset}
T_{\emptyset}

Here,
r
and
r^{\prime}
are new nodes with
\var(r)=\emptyset
;
\var(r^{\prime})=\{x\}
. It is readily verified that
T
is guarded. Correctness follows from Lemma
14
since
\{r_{x,y},r_{x},r_{\emptyset}\}
is the frontier of
N
.

8.
The q-hierarchical fragment of matlang queries

We next define
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
, a fragment of
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
that we will show to be equally expressive as
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
and qh-CQs. Like
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
,
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
is a two-layered language where expressions in the top layer can only be built from the lower layer. This lower layer, called
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
, is a fragment of
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
defined by

e\ \ \Coloneqq\ \ \mathbf{A}\ \mid\ \mathbf{1}^{\alpha}\ \mid\ \mathbf{I}^{\alpha}\ \mid\ e^{T}\ \mid\ e_{1}\times e_{2}\ \mid\ e_{1}\odot e_{2}\ \mid\ e\cdot\mathbf{1}^{\alpha}.

Note in particular that while
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
allows arbitrary matrix-vector multiplication, this is restricted to multiplication with the ones vector in
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
. Intuitively,
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
can already define q-hierarchical CQs like
H(x)\leftarrow\exists y.A(x,y)\wedge U(x)
, but it cannot define cross-products like
H(x,y)\leftarrow A(x)\wedge B(y)
, which are also q-hierarchical. For this reason, we enhance
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
with the higher layer. Specifically, let us call expressions of the form
e\cdot(\mathbf{1}^{\alpha})^{T}
and
\mathbf{1}^{\alpha}\cdot e

expansions
of
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression
e
. Note that these are well-typed only if
e
has column resp. row vector type. These expressions construct a matrix from a column (resp. row) vector by duplicating the column (resp. row)
\alpha
times.
We then define
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
to consist of all expressions
g
of the form

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\ \ e\ \mid e\cdot(\mathbf{1}^{\alpha})^{T}\mid\mathbf{1}^{\alpha}\cdot e

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\ \ f\ \mid f\odot f

where
e
ranges over
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions, and
f
over
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions and expansions thereof.

In this section we prove:

Theorem 36
.

\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
queries and
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
queries are equally expressive.

Combined with Theorem
29
we hence obtain.

Corollary 37
.

\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and q-hierarchical binary CQs are equally expressive.

The rest of this section is devoted to proving Theorem
36
. We show that qh-CQs are at least as expressive as
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
in Section
8.1
and the converse in Section
8.2
.

8.1.
From
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas to
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions

We will first simulate
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}

formulas
by means of a
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression according to the definition in Section
6.1
.

Proposition 38
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
be a formula over
\Voc
. Let
\Mat
be a relational-to-matrix schema encoding on
\Voc
such that
\Mat\vdash\varphi\colon\tau
. Then there exists a
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression
e
that simulates
\varphi
w.r.t.
\Mat
.

Proof 8.1
.

We assume
\var(\varphi)\subseteq\{x,y\}
with
x\prec y
. The proof is analogous to the proof of Proposition
23
, with the difference that when
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
the simulating expression is always
e_{1}\odot e_{2}
because
\free(\varphi_{1})=\free(\varphi_{2})
since
\varphi\in\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
. This has the consequence that the simulating expression is always a
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression.

Proposition 39
.

Let
\psi\in\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
be a formula over
\Voc
. Let
\Mat
be a relational-to-matrix schema encoding on
\Voc
such that
\Mat\vdash\psi\colon\tau
. Then there exists a
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression
e
that simulates
\psi
w.r.t.
\Mat
.

Proof 8.2
.

We assume
\var(\psi)\subseteq\{x,y\}
with
x\prec y
. By definition,
\psi=\varphi_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\varphi_{k}
with
\varphi_{i}\in\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
for every
i
. Consider the set
F=\{\free(\varphi_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
. Note that
F\not=\emptyset
. Furthermore, because
\psi
is a hierarchical conjunction,
\{\{x,y\},\{x\},\{y\}\}\not\subseteq F
. There are hence
2^{2^{2}}
-3 = 13 possibilities for
F
. We construct
e
by case analysis on
F
. We only illustrate one case; the other cases are similar.

Assume
F=\{\{x,y\},\{x\},\emptyset\}
. Define, for each
S\in F
the formula
\varphi_{S}
to be the conjunction of all
\varphi_{i}
with
\free(\varphi_{i})=S
. Then
\psi\equiv\varphi_{x,y}\wedge\varphi_{x}\wedge\varphi_{\emptyset}
. Observe that each
\varphi_{S}
is simple. Hence by Proposition
39
there exist
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions
e_{x,y},e_{x}
, and
e_{\emptyset}
that simulate
\varphi_{x,y},\varphi_{x}
, and
\varphi_{\emptyset}
, respectively. Then the
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression simulating
\varphi
is

e\coloneqq\left(e_{\varphi_{\emptyset}}\times e_{\varphi_{\{x,y\}}}\right)\odot\left(e_{\varphi_{\{x\}}}\cdot\left(\mathbf{1}^{\tau(y)}\right)^{T}\right).

To also establish the correspondence at the level of queries we first observe the following straightforward fact. For a
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression
e\colon(\alpha,1)
define
\textsf{diag}(e)
to return a matrix of type
(\alpha,\alpha)
where the entries of
e
are stored in the diagonal and where all non-diagonal entries are
\mymathbb{0}
.

Lemma 40
.

\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
is closed under transpose and diagonalisation: for any expression
g\in\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
also
g^{T}
is expressible in
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and, moreover, if
g\colon(\alpha,1)
has column vector type, then
\textsf{diag}(g)
is expressible in
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
.

Proof 8.3
.

Closure under transpose follows from the fact that
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
is closed under transpose by definition, and from the following equivalences:

\displaystyle(f_{1}\odot f_{2})^{T}

\displaystyle=f_{1}^{T}\odot f_{2}^{T}

\displaystyle\left(e\cdot(\mathbf{1}^{\alpha})^{T}\right)^{T}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}^{\alpha}\cdot e^{T}

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{1}^{\alpha}\cdot e\right)^{T}

\displaystyle=e^{T}\cdot(\mathbf{1}^{\alpha})^{T}

Closure under diagonalisation is straightforward. Assume that
g\colon(\alpha,1)
. Note that expansions never have column vector type. As such,
g
is a
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression, or a Hadamard product of
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions. Note that in the latter case, this is itself also a
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression since
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
is closed under Hadamard products. Then
\textsf{diag}(g)
can be define in
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
by first computing the column expansion of
g
and subsequently setting all non-diagonal entries of this expansion to zero by taking

\textsf{diag}(g)=\mathbf{I}^{\tau(x)}\odot\left(g\cdot\left(\mathbf{1}^{\tau(x)}\right)^{T}\right).

Corollary 41
.

Let
Q
be an
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
query over
\Voc
and let
\Mat
be a relational-to-matrix schema encoding on
\Voc(Q)
such that
\Mat\vdash Q\colon\tau
. Then there exists
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\mathbf{Q}
that simulates
Q
w.r.t.
\Mat
.

Proof 8.4
.

Assume
Q:H(\overline{x})\leftarrow\psi
. By Proposition
39
there exists a
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression
e
that simulates
\psi
w.r.t.
\Mat
.
Let
\Mat(H)=\mathbf{H}
. We build the
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\mathbf{Q}\coloneqq\mathbf{H}=\overline{e}
as follows, using Lemma
40
:

•

When
\overline{x}=(x,y)
, if
x\prec y
then define
\overline{e}\coloneqq e
, otherwise define
\overline{e}\coloneqq e^{T}
.

•

When
\overline{x}=(x,x)
we note that
\free(\psi)=\{x\}
and therefore
e\colon(\tau(x),1)
outputs the encoding of a square matrix, where the value of diagonal entry
(x,x)
is computed by
\psi(x)
. All non-diagonal entries of this matrix are
\mymathbb{0}
. Hence we take
\overline{e}:=\textsf{diag}(e)
.

•

When
\overline{x}=(x)
we have
e\colon(\tau(x),1)
. If
\mathbf{H}\colon(\tau(x),1)
define
\overline{e}\coloneqq e
otherwise if
\mathbf{H}\colon(1,\tau(x))
define
\overline{e}\coloneqq e^{T}
.

•

When
\overline{x}=()
we have
e\colon(1,1)
and define
\overline{e}\coloneqq e
.

8.2.
From
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions to
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas

To establish the converse direction of Theorem
36
we first show that how to simulate
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions by means of
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas that may also use equality, according to the definition in Section
6.2
. Define
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
to consist of all
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas as well as all formulas of the form
\varphi\wedge x=y
or
x=y\wedge\varphi
with
\varphi\in\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
and
\free(\varphi)\cap\{x,y\}\not=\emptyset
. Note that in the latter case we assume that
x,y
are the only two distinct variables that can be used in
\varphi
, so that the result again uses only two variables.

Define the
signature

\sig(\varphi)
of formula
\varphi\in\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
to be the set
\{\free(\varphi_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
where
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n}
are the non-equality conjuncts of
\varphi
. For example, the signature of
\varphi=x\leq c\wedge y\leq d\wedge x=y
is
\{\{x\},\{y\}\}
.

The following lemma shows that projections on
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
formula are expressible as
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas (hence, not using equality), provided that the original formula’s signature is of a certain shape.

Lemma 42
.

Let
\varphi
be a
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
formula such that
\sig(\varphi)
is a subset of either
\{\emptyset,\{x\},\{y\}\}
or
\{\emptyset,\{x,y\}\}
. There exists a
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula equivalent to
\exists x.\varphi
(resp.
\exists y.\varphi
) whose signature is a subset of
\{\emptyset,\{x\},\{y\}\}
.

Proof 8.5
.

We first make the following claim: if
\psi\in\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
and
\free(\psi)\cap\{x,y\}\not=\emptyset
, then there exists a
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula equivalent to
\exists x.(\varphi\wedge x=y)
(resp.
\exists x.(\varphi\wedge x=y)
). The proof of this claim is identical to the proof of Lemma
26
, observing that the construction defined there, when applied to simple formulas, yields a simple formula. Because the resulting formula has at most 1 free variable, its signature (viewed as a conjunction with only one conjunct) is trivially a subset of
\{\emptyset,\{x\},\{y\}\}
.

Next, assume that
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\varphi_{k}\wedge x=y
where the
\varphi_{i}
are
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas. (The reasoning when
\varphi
does not contain the equality atom
x=y
is similar.) We make a case analysis on
\sig(\varphi)
.

•

\sig(\varphi)
is a subset of
\{\emptyset,\{x\},\{y\}\}
. Since
\varphi
has at least one conjunct,
\sig(\varphi)
is non-empty. Moreover, the case where
\sig(\varphi)=\{\emptyset\}
cannot occur, since otherwise
\varphi
would not be safe. There are hence
2^{3}-2=6
possibilities to consider for the signature. We only illustrate the reasoning when
\sig(\varphi)=\{\emptyset,\{x\},\{y\}\}
, the other cases are similar. Assume that
\sig(\varphi)=\{\emptyset,\{x\},\{y\}\}
. For each
S\in\{\emptyset,\{x\},\{y\}\}
let
\psi_{S}
be the conjunction of all the formulas
\varphi_{i}
with
\free(\varphi_{i})=S
. Note that each
\psi_{S}
is in
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
, as it is a conjunction of simple formulas with the same set of free variables, and that
\varphi\equiv\psi_{\emptyset}\wedge\psi_{x}\wedge\psi_{y}\wedge x=y
. Then
\exists x.\varphi\equiv\psi_{\emptyset}\wedge\exists x.(\psi_{x}\wedge x=y)\wedge\psi_{y}
. By our earlier claim,
\exists x.(\psi_{x}\wedge x=y)
is expressible as a
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula
\psi^{\prime}_{x}
. Because
\exists x.(\psi_{x}\wedge x=y)
has free variables
\{y\}
, so does
\psi^{\prime}_{x}
. Hence,

\exists x.\varphi\equiv\psi_{\emptyset}\wedge\psi^{\prime}_{x}\wedge\psi_{y}
, where the right-hand side is a hierarchical conjunction of
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas and is hence in
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
. Also, observe that the signature of the right-hand side is a subset of
\{\emptyset,\{y\}\}
. This establishes the desired result.

•

\sig(\varphi)
is a subset of
\{\emptyset,\{x,y\}\}
. The reasoning is entirely similar.

First we show the reduction from
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions to
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
formulas. Here, the signature of the resulting formula is important to later remove the equalities.

Proposition 43
.

Let
e
be a
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression over
\Sch
and let
\Rel
be a matrix-to-relational schema encoding on
\Sch
. Let
x,y
be two variables such that
x\prec y
. There exists
\varphi_{e}\in\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
simulating
e
such that
\sig(\varphi_{e})
is a subset of either
\{\emptyset,\{x\},\{y\}\}
or
\{\emptyset,\{x,y\}\}
.

Proof 8.6
.

The proof is by induction and analogous to the proof of Proposition
25
. We only illustrate two interesting cases.

•

If
e=\mathbf{I}^{\alpha}\colon(\alpha,\alpha)
then take
\psi_{e}\coloneqq x\leq\alpha\wedge y\leq\alpha\wedge x=y
. Note that
x\leq\alpha\wedge y\leq\alpha
is a hierarchical conjunction of simple
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas. Therefore,
\psi_{e}\in\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
, as desired.

•

If
e=e_{1}\times e_{2}
with
e_{1}\colon(1,1)
and
e_{2}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
we reason as follows. Let
\psi_{e_{1}}
and
\psi_{e_{2}}
be the
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
formulas simulating
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
obtained by induction.

First observe that because
\psi_{e_{1}}
simulates
e_{1}
and because
e_{1}
always outputs scalar matrices,
\llceil{\psi_{e_{1}}}\rrfloor_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}(x\mapsto i,y\mapsto j)=\mymathbb{0}
whenever
i\not=1
or
j\not=1
. We conclude that therefore,
\exists x,y.\psi_{e_{1}}
will always return
\llbracket e_{1}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})(1,1)
when evaluated on
\Rel(\mathcal{I})
. Hence, we take
\psi_{e}\coloneqq\left(\exists x,y.\psi_{e_{1}}\right)\wedge\psi_{e_{2}}
. Note that
\left(\exists x,y.\psi_{e_{1}}\right)
is expressible in
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
by applying Lemma
42
twice, i.e., it is expressible as a hierarchical conjunction of simple
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas. Each of these formulas must have the empty set of free variables. Therefore, their conjunction with
\psi_{e_{1}}
is also a hierarchical conjunction, and hence in
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
. The signature of the resulting conjunction is the union of
\{\emptyset\}
with the signature of
\psi_{e_{1}}
, and therefore also a subset of either
\{\emptyset,\{x\},\{y\}\}
or
\{\emptyset,\{x,y\}\}
.

Next, we show how to translate any
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression to a
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
formula.

Proposition 44
.

Let
g
be a
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expression over
\Sch
and let
\Rel
be a matrix-to-relational schema encoding on
\Sch
. There exists
\psi\in\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
that simulates
e
w.r.t.
\Rel
.

Proof 8.7
.

Let
x,y
be two variables such that
x\prec y
. We may assume w.l.o.g. that the formulas
\varphi
returned by Proposition
43
have
\free(\varphi)=\{x,y\}
. We reason by case analysis. Let
e_{1},e_{2}
range over
\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions.

(1)

g\in\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
. Then the claim follows from Proposition
43
.

(2)

g=e_{1}\odot\left(e_{2}\cdot\left(\mathbf{1}^{\beta}\right)^{T}\right)
with
e_{1}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
and
e_{2}\colon(\alpha,1)
. By Proposition
43
, there exists
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
simulating
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, respectively. Observe that for all matrix instances
\mathcal{I}
and all valid indices
i,j
we have
\llbracket g\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})_{i,j}=\llbracket e_{1}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})_{i,j}\odot\llbracket e_{2}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})_{i,1}
. Because
\varphi_{2}
simulates
e_{2}
and because
e_{2}
always outputs a column vector,
\llceil{\varphi_{2}}\rrfloor_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}(x\mapsto i,y\mapsto j)=\mymathbb{0}
whenever
j\not=1
, for all
\mathcal{I}
. We conclude that therefore
\llbracket e_{2}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})_{i,1}=\llceil{\exists y.\varphi_{2}}\rrfloor_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}(x\mapsto i)
. Hence it suffices take
\psi=\varphi_{1}(x,y)\wedge\exists y.\varphi_{2}(x,y)
. By Lemma
42

\exists y.\varphi_{2}(x,y)
is expressible in
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
, i.e., it is expressible as a hiearchical conjunction of simple
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas, which necessarily all have free variables that are a subset of
\{x\}
. Extending this conjunction with
\varphi_{1}
, whose signature is a subset of
\{\emptyset,\{x\},\{y\}\}
or
\{\emptyset,\{x,y\}\}
by Proposition
43
, remains hierarchical. We conclude that
\psi
is expressible in
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
.

(3)

e=e_{1}\odot\left(\mathbf{1}^{\alpha}\cdot e_{2}\right)
with
e_{1}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
and
e_{2}\colon(1,\beta)
. The reasoning is similar as in the previous case, but taking
\varphi=\varphi_{1}(x,y)\wedge\exists x.\varphi_{2}(x,y)
.

(4)

e=\left(e_{1}\cdot\left(\mathbf{1}^{\beta}\right)^{T}\right)\odot\left(\mathbf{1}^{\alpha}\cdot e_{2}\right)
with
e_{1}\colon(\alpha,1)
and
e_{2}\colon(1,\beta)
. The reasoning is again similar, taking
\varphi=\left(\exists y.\varphi_{1}(x,y)\right)\wedge\left(\exists x.\varphi_{2}(x,y)\right)
.

(5)

The only other possibilities are commutative variants of cases already considered.

The next lemmas will aid to remove the equalities of
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
formulas in the final result. The reader can find their proofs in Appendix
D
.

Lemma 45
.

Let
\varphi
be a
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula (hence, without equality) using only the distinct variables
x,y
such that
\free(\varphi)=\{x,y\}
. Let
\psi
be the
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
formula
\exists x.(\varphi\wedge x=y)
. Note that
\free(\psi)=\{y\}
. There exists an
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula
\psi^{\prime}
equivalent to
\psi
.

Lemma 46
.

If
\varphi\in\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
has
\free(\varphi)=\{x,y\}
and
z\in\{x,y\}
then there exists
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
with
\free(\varphi^{\prime})=\{z\}
such that for all
\nu\colon\{z\}
and all
db
,

\displaystyle\llceil{\varphi^{\prime}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)=\llceil{\varphi\wedge x=y}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(x\mapsto\nu(z),y\mapsto\nu(z)).

We are ready to show how to translate any
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query to a
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
query.

Corollary 47
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}
be an
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query over
\Sch
and let
\Rel
be a matrix-to-relational schema encoding on
\Sch
. There exist
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
query
Q
that simulates
\mathbf{Q}
w.r.t.
\Mat
.

Proof 8.8
.

Let
\mathbf{H}\coloneqq e
be a
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query and let
H=\Rel(\mathbf{H})
. We consider three cases.

(1)

H
is a binary relation. By Proposition
44
there exists
\psi\in\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
that simulates
e
w.r.t.
\Rel
. By construction,
\psi
has two distinct free variables. Assume that
x,y
are these two distinct variables, and
x\prec y
. If
\psi\in\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
then we take
Q
to be the query
H(x,y)\leftarrow\psi
. Otherwise,
\psi=\psi^{\prime}\wedge x=y
with
\psi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
and at least one of
x,y
appearing free in
\psi^{\prime}
. If
\free(\psi^{\prime})=\{x\}
then we take
H(x,x)\leftarrow\psi^{\prime}
as simulating query. If
\free(\psi^{\prime})=\{y\}
then we take
H(y,y)\leftarrow\psi^{\prime}
as simulating query. Otherwise
\free(\psi^{\prime})=\{x,y\}
, and we take
H(x,x)\leftarrow\psi^{\prime\prime}
as simulating query, where
\psi^{\prime\prime}
is the result of applying Lemma
46
to
\psi^{\prime}
and
z=x
.

(2)

H
is a unary relation. Assume that
\mathbf{H}
is of column vector type
(\alpha,1)
; the reasoning when
\mathbf{H}
has row vector type is similar. Because of well-typedness, also
e\colon(\alpha,1)
. Now observe that expansions return matrices that are not column vectors (or, if they do, the result can be written without using an expansion). As such,
e\in\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
. By Proposition
43
there exists
\psi\in\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
that simulates
e
w.r.t.
\Rel
such that
\sig(\psi)
is a subset of either
\{\emptyset,\{x\},\{y\}\}
or of
\{\emptyset,\{x,y\}\}
. By construction,
\psi
has two distinct free variables. Assume that
x,y
are these two distinct variables, and
x\prec y
. Take
\varphi=\exists y.\psi
. Because
\psi
simulates
e
and because
e
always outputs column vectors,
\llceil{\psi}\rrfloor_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}(x\mapsto i,y\mapsto j)=\mymathbb{0}
whenever
j\not=1
, for every
\mathcal{I}
. We conclude that therefore
\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})(i,1)=\llceil{\exists y.\psi}\rrfloor_{\Rel(\mathcal{I})}(x\mapsto i)
for all
i
and
\mathcal{I}
. As such, the query
Q^{\prime}\colon H(x)\leftarrow\varphi
simulates
\mathbf{Q}
. While
Q^{\prime}
is a query in
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2,=}
, we can next apply Lemma
42
to
\varphi
to obtain an equivalent formula
\varphi^{\prime}\equiv\varphi
in
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
.

(3)

H
is a nullary relation. The reason is entirely similar to the previous case.

9.
Preliminaries for enumeration algorithms and query evaluation

In the previous sections we have presented a precise connection between
fragments of
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
and fragments of
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
. In the sequel, we move to
translate the algorithmic properties of the free-connex and q-hierarchical
conjunctive fragments of
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
to properties for the corresponding fragments
of
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
. Towards this goal, this section introduces all the extra
background and definitions regarding enumeration algorithms. The properties themselves are obtained in
Sections
10
and
11
.

Enumeration problems

An
enumeration problem

P
is a collection of pairs
(I,O)
where
I
is an
input
and
O
is a finite set of answers for
I
. There is a functional dependency from
I
to
O
: whenever
(I,O)
and
(I^{\prime},O^{\prime})
are in
P
and
I=I^{\prime}
then also
O=O^{\prime}
. For this reason, we also denote
O
simply by
P(I)
. An
enumeration algorithm
for an enumeration problem
P
is an algorithm
A
that works in two phases: an
enumeration phase
and a
preprocessing phase
. During preprocessing,
A
is given input
I
and may compute certain data structures, but does not produce any output. During the enumeration phase,
A
may use the data structures constructed during preprocessing to print the elements of
P(I)
one by one, without repetitions. The
preprocessing time
is the running time of the preprocessing phase. The
delay
is an upper bound on the time between printing any two answers in the enumeration phase; the time from the beginning of the enumeration phase until printing the first answer; and the time after printing the last answer and the time the algorithm terminates.

Query evaluation by enumeration

In our setting, we consider query evaluation as an enumeration problem. Specifically, define the
answer
of a query
Q
on database
db
to be the set of all tuples outputed by
Q
on
db
, together with their (non-zero) annotation,

\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})\ \coloneqq\ \{(\overline{d},\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\textit{db}}(H)(\overline{d}))\mid\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\textit{db}}(H)(\overline{d})\neq\mymathbb{0}\}.

Here,
H
denotes the relation symbol in the head of
Q
.

We then associate to each
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
query
Q
over vocabulary
\Voc
and semiring
\mathcal{K}
the following enumeration problem

\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
:

Problem:

\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})

Input:

A
\mathcal{K}
-database
db
over
\Voc

Output:

Enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
.

Formally this is the collection of all pairs
(I,O)
, where

I=\textit{db}
with
db
a
\mathcal{K}
-database over
\Voc
; and

O=\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
. Hence,
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
is the evaluation problem of
Q
on
\mathcal{K}
-databases.

Similarly, we associate to each
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{H}:=e
over schema
\Sch
and semiring
\mathcal{K}
the enumeration problem

\texttt{Eval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
:

Problem:

\texttt{Eval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})

Input:

A
\mathcal{K}
-instance
\mathcal{I}
over
\Sch

Output:

Enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(\mathbf{Q},\mathcal{I})
.

This is, the collection of all pairs
(I,O)
where

•

I=\mathcal{I}
with
\mathcal{I}
a
\mathcal{K}
-matrix instance over
\Sch
, sparsely represented by listing for each matrix symbol
\mathbf{A}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
the set of entries
\{(i,j,k)\mid i\leq\mathcal{I}(\alpha),j\leq\mathcal{I}(\beta),\mathbf{A}^{\mathcal{I}}_{i,j}=k\not=\mymathbb{0}\}
; and

•

O=\texttt{Answer}(\mathbf{Q},\mathcal{I})
where, assuming
\mathbf{H}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
,

\texttt{Answer}(\mathbf{Q},\mathcal{I})\coloneqq\{(i,j,\llbracket\mathbf{Q}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})(\mathbf{H})_{i,j})\mid\llbracket\mathbf{Q}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})(\mathbf{H})_{i,j}\neq\mymathbb{0},i\leq\mathcal{I}(\alpha),j\leq\mathcal{I}(\beta)\}.

Hence,
\texttt{Eval}(\mathbf{Q},\Voc,\mathcal{K})
is the
sparse
evaluation problem of
\mathbf{Q}

on matrix instances over
\Sch
, where only the non-zero output entries must be
computed.

Complexity classes

For two functions
f
and
g
from the natural
numbers to the positive reals we define
\texttt{Enum}(f,g)
to be the class of
