enumeration problems for which there exists an enumeration algorithm
A
such
that for every input
I
it holds that the preprocessing time is

\bigo\left(f(\parallel\!I\!\parallel)\right)
and the delay is

\bigo\left(g(\parallel\!I\!\parallel)\right)
, where
\parallel\!I\!\parallel
denotes the size of
I
. For
example,
\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
hence refers to the class of query
evaluation enumeration problems that have linear time preprocessing and constant
delay (in data complexity). Note that, since the inputs in an evaluation problem

\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
consists only of the database and not the query, we hence
measure complexity in
data complexity
as is standard in the
literature
[
BDG07
,
IUV+20
,
BGS20
,
BKS17
]
.

Throughout,
\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel
denotes the size of the input
\mathcal{K}
-database
db
,
measured as the length of a reasonable encoding of
db
, i.e.,

\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel:=\sum_{R\in\Voc}\parallel\!R^{\textit{db}}\!\parallel+\sum_{c\in\Voc}1
where

\parallel\!R^{\textit{db}}\!\parallel:=(\arity(R)+1)\cdot|R^{\textit{db}}|
with
|R^{\textit{db}}|
the
number of tuples
\overline{d}
with
R^{\textit{db}}(\overline{d})\not=\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
. Intuitively, we represent each tuple as well as its
\mathcal{K}
-value
and assume that encoding a domain value or
\mathcal{K}
-value takes unit space. In
addition, each constant symbol is assigned a domain value, which also takes unit
space per constant symbol.

Similarly, if
\mathcal{I}
is a matrix instance over matrix schema
\Sch
then
\parallel\!\mathcal{I}\!\parallel
denotes the size of a reasonable sparse encoding of
I
, i.e.,
\parallel\!\mathcal{I}\!\parallel:=\sum_{\mathbf{A}\in\Sch}\parallel\!\mathbf{A}^{\mathcal{I}}\!\parallel+\sum_{\alpha\in\Voc}1
where
\parallel\!\mathbf{A}^{\mathcal{I}}\!\parallel
denotes the number of entries
(i,j)
with
(\mathbf{A}^{\mathcal{I}})_{i,j}\not=\mymathbb{0}
, for
\mathbf{A}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
and
1\leq i\leq\alpha^{\mathcal{I}}
and
1\leq j\leq\beta^{\mathcal{I}}
.

Model of computation

As has become standard in the
literature
[
BDG07
,
IUV+20
,
BGS20
,
BKS17
]
, we consider algorithms on Random
Access Machines (RAM) with uniform cost measure
[
AHU74
]
. Whenever we work with
\mathcal{K}
-databases for an arbitrary semiring

\mathcal{K}
, we assume that each semiring operation can be executed in constant
time.

Complexity Hypotheses

We will use the following algorithmic problems and associated complexity hypotheses for obtaining
conditional lower bounds.

(1)

The
Sparse Boolean Matrix Multiplication problem
: given
A
and
B
as a list of nonzero entries, compute the nonzero entries of the matrix product
AB
. The
Sparse Boolean Matrix Multiplication conjecture
states that this problem cannot be solved in time
\bigo(M)
, where
M
is the size (number of non-zero entries) of the input plus output.

(2)

The
Triangle Detection problem
: given a graph represented using adjacency lists, decide whether it contains a triangle. The
Triangle Detection conjecture
states that this problem cannot be solved in time
\bigo(M)
, where
M
is the size of the input plus output.

(3)

(k,k+1)
-Hyperclique
: Given a hypergraph where every hyperedge consists of exactly
k\geq 3
vertices, decide if it contains a hyperclique of size
k+1
. A hyperclique is a set of vertices such that each pair of vertices in the set is contained in a hyperedge. The
(k,k+1)
-Hyperclique conjecture
states that this problem cannot be solved in time
\bigo(M)
, where
M
is the size of the input plus output.

Note that the three hypotheses are standard in computer science and has been used to prove conditional lower bounds for the evaluation of CQ. See
[
BGS20
]
for a discussion.

Known bounds for free-connex CQs

In what follows, let
\text{CQ}^{\not\leq}
denote the class of CQs where no inequality atoms occur in the body. The body hence mentions only relational atoms.
Also, let
\mathbb{B}=(\{\texttt{t},\texttt{f}\},\vee,\wedge,\texttt{f},\texttt{t})
be
the boolean semiring.

Bagan, Durand and Grandjean
[
BDG07
]
(see also
[
BGS20
]
) proved that, under certain
complexity-theoretic assumptions, the class of free-connex conjunctive queries
characterizes the class of conjunctive queries that, in data complexity, can be
enumerated with linear time preprocessing
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
and with constant
delay on boolean input databases. We next recall their results.

{thmC}
[
[
BDG07
]
]

Let
Q
be a
\text{CQ}^{\not\leq}
over vocabulary
\Voc
.

•

If
Q
is free-connex then
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
.

•

If
Q
is a query without self joins and

\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
, then
Q
is free-connex
unless either the Sparse Boolean Matrix Multiplication, the Triangle
Detection, or the
(k,k+1)
-Hyperclique conjecture is false.

10.
Efficient evaluation of free-connex queries

In this section, we focus on the evaluation problem for
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
, namely, the free-connex queries in
\mathsf{sum}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
.
Our aim is to transfer the known algorithmic properties of evaluating free-connex CQs to properties of evaluating
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
.

To apply the known algorithm for fc-CQs to
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
, we must first
solve two problems: (1) the efficient evaluation algorithm for fc-CQs is
only established for evaluation over Boolean semiring, not arbitrary semirings
\mathcal{K}
,
and (2) the algorithm is for CQ without inequalities (comparison atoms). We
will therefore generalize the known evaluation algorithms to
other semirings (e.g.,
\mathbb{R}
), and to queries that have also inequalities (Section
10.1
). Similarly, we do the same for the lower bounds known for fc-CQs (Section
10.2
).

10.1.
Upper bounds for free-connex queries

A semiring
(K,\oplus,\odot,\mymathbb{0},\mymathbb{1})
is
zero-divisor free
if, for all
a,b\in K
,

a\odot b=\mymathbb{0}
implies
a=\mymathbb{0}
or
b=\mymathbb{0}
. A zero-divisor
free semiring is called a
semi-integral
domain

[
Gol13
]
. Note that semirings used in practice,
like
\mathbb{B}
,
\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathbb{R}
, are semi-integral domains. We will establish:

Theorem 48
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a semi-integral domain. For every free-connex query
Q
over
\Voc
,

\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
can be evaluated with linear-time preprocessing and constant-delay.
In particular,
\texttt{Eval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
can also be evaluated with linear-time preprocessing
and constant-delay for every
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}

\mathbf{Q}
over
\Sch
.

The semi-integral condition is necessary to ensure that zero outputs can only be produced by some zero input entries. For instance, consider a semiring
(K,\oplus,\odot,\mymathbb{0},\mymathbb{1})
such that there exist
a,b\in K
where
a\neq\mymathbb{0}
,
b\neq\mymathbb{0}
and
a\odot b=\mymathbb{0}
.
Consider
Q:H(x,y)\leftarrow R(x)\wedge S(y)
and a database
db
over the previous semiring such that
R(1)=a;R(2)=a;S(1)=b
and
S(2)=b
.
Then, the output of
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
with input
db
is empty, although the body can be instantiated in four different ways, all of them producing
\mymathbb{0}
values.

To illustrate the utility of Theorem
48
, consider the
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\mathbf{A}\odot(\mathbf{U}\cdot\mathbf{V}^{T})
where
\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}
are column vectors. When this query is evaluated in a bottom-up fashion, the subexpression
(\mathbf{U}\cdot\mathbf{V}^{T})
will generate partial results of size
\parallel\!\mathbf{U}\!\parallel\parallel\!\mathbf{V}\!\parallel
, causing the entire evaluation to be of complexity
\Omega(\parallel\!\mathbf{A}\!\parallel+\parallel\!\mathbf{U}\!\parallel\parallel\!\mathbf{V}\!\parallel)
. By contrast, Theorem
48
tells us that we may evaluate the query in time
\bigo(\parallel\!\mathbf{A}\!\parallel+\parallel\!\mathbf{U}\!\parallel+\parallel\!\mathbf{V}\!\parallel)
.

We derive the enumeration algorithm for
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
by extending the algorithm of
[
BDG07
]
to any semi-integral domain
\mathcal{K}
and taking care of the inequalities in
Q
. Then, we derive the enumeration algorithm for
\texttt{Eval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
by reducing to
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
, using Corollary
22
.

We now prove Theorem
48
in two steps. We first transfer the upper bound of
\text{CQs}^{\not\leq}
over
\mathbb{B}
-databases to
\text{CQs}^{\not\leq}
over
\mathcal{K}
-databases. We then generalize the latter to CQs over
\mathcal{K}
-databases.

Remark

In what follows, we will describe enumeration algorithms for
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
. Formally, given an input database
db
, these algorithms hence have to enumerate the set
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
of output tuples with their non-zero semiring annotation. In the proofs however, we will often find it convenient to instead enumerate the set
\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db})
of valuations,

\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db})\coloneqq\{(\nu,\llceil{Q}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu))\mid\nu\colon\overline{x},\llceil{Q}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)\neq\mymathbb{0}\}.

Note that an algorithm that enumerates
\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db})
can also be used to enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
by turning each valuation into an output tuple, and viceversa an algorithm that enumerates
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
can also be used to enumerate
\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db})
: by definition every output tuple
\overline{d}\models\overline{x}
such that
\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\textit{db}}(H)(\overline{d})\neq\mymathbb{0}
induces a valuation
\nu\colon\overline{x}
such that
\llceil{Q}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)\neq\mymathbb{0}
. Note in particular that if either one of them can be enumerated with constant delay, then so can the other. We use this insight repeatedly in the proofs.

From
\mathbb{B}
-databases to
\mathcal{K}
-databases over a semi-integral domain

We start our proof of Theorem
48
by extending the algorithmic results of free connex CQ from the boolean semiring to any semi-integral domain.

Proposition 49
.

If
Q
is a free-connex
\text{CQ}^{\not\leq}
over
\Voc
and
\mathcal{K}
a semi-integral domain then
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
.

Proof 10.1
.

Because
Q
is free-connex, it has a query plan
(T,N)
by Proposition
11
. We describe different evaluation algorithms depending on the shape of
Q
and
(T,N)
. Let
db
be a
\mathcal{K}
-database over
\Voc
and assume the semi-integral domain
\mathcal{K}=(K,\oplus,\odot,\mymathbb{0},\mymathbb{1})
.

(1)

When
Q
is full, i.e, no variable is quantified, we reduce to the Boolean case as follows. Assume that
Q=H(\overline{z})\leftarrow R_{1}(\overline{z}_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge R_{n}(\overline{z}_{n})
. In
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
time construct
\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})
, the
\mathbb{B}
-database obtained from
db
defined as:

R^{\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})}\colon\overline{d}\mapsto\begin{cases}\texttt{t}\text{ if }R^{\textit{db}}(\overline{d})\neq\mymathbb{0}\\
\texttt{f}\text{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

for every
R\in\Voc
. Furthermore, preprocess
db
by creating lookup tables such that for every relation
R
and tuple
\overline{a}
of the correct arity we can retrieve the annotation
R^{\textit{db}}(\overline{a})
in time
\bigo(|\overline{a}|)
time. Note that since
\Voc
is fixed,
|\overline{a}|
is constant. This retrieval is hence
\bigo(1)
in data complexity. It is well-known that such lookup tables can be created in
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
time in the RAM model.

Finally, invoke the algorithm for
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})
on
\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})
. By Theorem
9
, this algorithm has
\bigo(\parallel\!\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})\!\parallel)=\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
preprocessing time, after which we may enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db}))
with
\bigo(1)
delay. We use the latter to enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
with
\bigo(1)
delay: whenever an element
(\overline{a},\texttt{t})
of
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db}))
is enumerated retrieve
k_{1}:=R_{1}({\overline{a}}|_{\overline{z}_{1}}),\dots,k_{n}:=R_{n}({\overline{a}}|_{\overline{z}_{n}})
by means of the previously constructed lookup tables. Here,
{\overline{a}}|_{\overline{z}_{n}}
denotes the projection of
\overline{a}
according to the variables in
\overline{z}_{i}
. Then output
(\overline{a},k_{1}\odot\dots\odot k_{n})
. Because the query is fixed, so is the number of lookups we need to do, and this hence takes
\bigo(1)
additional delay in data complexity. In summary the delay between printing one output and the next is hence
\bigo(1)
while the total processing time—comprising the construction of
\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})
, the lookup tables and the preprocessing phase of
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\texttt{Bool})
— is
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
.

We claim that the set of pairs
(\overline{a},k_{1}\odot\dots\odot k_{n})
hence printed is exactly
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
:

•

If we print
(\overline{a},k_{1}\odot\dots\odot k_{n})
then
(\overline{a},\texttt{t})
was in
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db}))
, which can only happen if
R_{i}^{\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})}({\overline{a}}|_{\overline{z}_{i}})=\texttt{t}
for every
1\leq i\leq n
. By definition of
\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})
this means that
k_{i}=R_{i}^{\textit{db}}({\overline{a}}|_{\overline{z}_{i}})\not=\mymathbb{0}
. Hence,
\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\textit{db}}(H)(\overline{a})=k_{1}\odot\dots\odot k_{n}
since
Q
is full. Because
\mathcal{K}
is a semi-integral domain, the latter product is non-zero and hence
(\overline{a},k_{1}\odot\dots\odot k_{n})\in\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
.

•

If
(\overline{a},k)\in\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
then by definition
k\not=\mymathbb{0}
and, because
Q
is full,
k=k_{1}\odot\dots\odot k_{n}
where
k_{i}=R_{i}^{\textit{db}}({\overline{a}}|_{\overline{z}_{i}})
. As such
k_{i}\not=\mymathbb{0}
for
1\leq i\leq n
. Hence,
R_{i}^{\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})}({\overline{a}}|_{\overline{z}_{i}})=\texttt{t}
for every
i
and thus,
(\overline{a},\texttt{t})\in\texttt{Answer}(Q,\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db}))
. Therefore,
(\overline{a},k_{1}\odot\dots\odot k_{n})
will be enumerated by our algorithm above.

(2)

When the query plan
(T,N)
is such that
N
consists of a single node
N=\{r\}
we next show that may compute the entire result of
Q
in

\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
time. This implies that
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
: compute
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
and use this data structure (e.g., represented as a linked list) to enumerate its elements, which trivially supports constant delay. Essentially, the argument that we use here is the same as the standard argument in the theory of acyclic conjunctive queries that Boolean acyclic queries on Boolean databases can be evaluated in linear time by a series of projection operations and semijoin operations
[
Yan81
,
AHV95
]
. We repeat the argument in the
\mathcal{K}
-setting here for completeness only. For notational convenience, if
\psi
is an
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}
formula then we denote by
\texttt{Answer}(\psi,\textit{db})
the set
\texttt{Answer}(P_{\psi},\textit{db})
where
P_{\psi}:H(\overline{x})\leftarrow\psi
with
\overline{x}
a repetition-free list of the free variables of
\psi
. The algorithm exploits the fact that, since
(T,N)
is a query plan for
Q
we have the body of
Q
is equivalent to
\varphi[T,N]=\varphi[T,r]
. Hence, it suffices to show that
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,r],\textit{db})
may be computed in linear time.
3
3

3

The only difference between
Q
and
\varphi[T,r]
is that
Q
may repeat some variables in the head; the result of
Q
can hence be obtained by computing
\varphi[T,r]
and repeating values in each result tuple as desired.

We argue inductively on the height of
T
that
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,N],\textit{db})
may be computed in linear time. By Lemma
12
, we may assume without loss of generality that for every node
n
with children
c_{1},c_{2}
in
T
either
\var(c_{1})=\var(n)
and
\var(c_{2})\subseteq\var(n)
; or
\var(c_{2})=\var(n)
and
\var(c_{1})\subseteq\var(n)
.

(a)

When
T
has height 1 then the root
r
is a leaf and hence an atom, say
R(\overline{x})
. As such,
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,r],\textit{db})
can trivially be computed in
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
time by scanning
R^{\textit{db}}
.

(b)

When
T
has height greater than 1 we identify two cases.

•

Root
r
has one child
c
. By Lemma
15
we have
\varphi[T,r]\equiv\exists\overline{y}.\varphi[T,c]
with
\overline{y}=\var(c)\setminus\var(r)
. Here,
\varphi[T,c]
equals
\varphi[T_{c},c]
with
T_{c}
the subtree of
T
rooted at
c
. As such, we may compute
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,r],\textit{db})
by first computing
A:=\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T_{c},c],\textit{db})
and then projecting on the variables in
\var(r)
. This is in linear time by induction hypothesis and the fact that projections on
\mathcal{K}
-relations can be done in linear time in the RAM model, even on general
\mathcal{K}
-databases.
4
4

4

Create an empty lookup table. Iterate over the tuples
(\overline{a},k)
of
A
. For every such tuple
(\overline{a},k)
, look up
{\overline{a}}|_{\var(r)}
in the lookup table. If this is present with
\mathcal{K}
-value
l
, then set
l:=l\oplus k
; otherwise add
({\overline{a}}|_{\var(r)},k)
to the table. After the iteration on
A
, enumerate the entries
(\overline{b},k)
in the lookup table and add each entry to the output when
k\not=\mymathbb{0}
. This is exactly the projected result.

•

Root
r
has two children
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
. We may assume w.l.o.g. that
\var(r)=\var(c_{1})
and
\var(c_{2})\subseteq\var(r)=\var(c_{1})
. By Lemma
16
we have
\varphi[T,r]\equiv\varphi[T,c_{1}]\wedge\varphi[T,c_{2}]
. Note that because
\var(c_{1})\subseteq\var(c_{2})
and
\free(\varphi[T,c_{i}])=\var(c_{i})
, the formula
\varphi[T,r]
is hence expressing a semijoin between
\varphi[T,c_{1}]
and
\varphi[T,c_{2}]
. Hence, we may compute
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,r],
) by first computing
A_{1}:=\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T_{1},c_{1}],\textit{db})
and
A_{2}:=\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T_{2},c_{2}],\textit{db})
(where
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are the subtrees of
T
rooted at
c_{1}
, resp.
c_{2}
) and then computing the semijoin between
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
. This is in linear time by induction hypothesis and the fact that semijoins on
\mathcal{K}
-relations can be done in linear time in the RAM model, even on general
\mathcal{K}
-databases.
5
5

5

Create a lookup table on
A_{2}
, allowing to retrieve the
\mathcal{K}
-annotation given a
\var(c_{2})
-tuple. Then iterate over the entries
(\overline{a},k)
of
A_{1}
. For every such tuple
(\overline{a},k)
, look up
{\overline{a}}|_{\var(c_{2})}
in the lookup table. If this is present with
\mathcal{K}
-value
l
, then output
(\overline{a},k\odot l)
provided
k\odot l\not=\mymathbb{0}
.

(3)

When
Q
is not full and
N
has more than one node we reduce to the previous two cases as follows. By Lemma
14
we have that
\varphi[T,N]\equiv\varphi[T_{1},n_{1}]\wedge\dots\wedge\varphi[T_{l},n_{l}]
where
F=\{n_{1},\ldots,n_{l}\}
is the frontier of
N
, i.e, the nodes without children in
N
, and
T_{1},\dots,T_{l}
are the subtrees of
T
rooted at
n_{1},\dots,n_{l}
respectively. Hence, we may compute
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,N],\textit{db})
by computing
A_{i}:=\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T_{i},n_{i}],\textit{db})
for
1\leq i\leq n
and then taking the join of the resulting
\mathcal{K}
-relations. By item (2) above, each
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T_{i},n_{i}],\textit{db})
can be computed in linear time, and is hence of linear size. The preprocessing and enumeration of the final join can be done as in item (1) above, since this is a full join.

From
\text{CQ}^{\not\leq}
to CQ

An inequality atom
x\leq c
is
covered
in a CQ
Q
if there is a relational atom in
Q
that mentions
x
; it is
non-covered
otherwise. So, in the query
H(y,z)\leftarrow R(x,y)\wedge y\leq c\wedge z\leq d
the inequality
y\leq c
is covered but
z\leq d
is not.
For a CQ
Q
define the
split
of
Q
to be the pair
\texttt{split}(Q)=(Q_{\text{rel}},Q_{\text{ineq}})
where:

\displaystyle Q_{\text{rel}}(\overline{x})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\exists\overline{y}.\bigwedge_{R(\overline{z})\in\atoms(\varphi)}R(\overline{z})

\displaystyle Q_{\text{ineq}}(\overline{u})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\exists\overline{v}.\bigwedge_{w\leq c\in\atoms(\varphi),\text{non-covered}}w\leq c

with
\overline{x}
and
\overline{y}
the set of all free resp. bound variables in
Q
that occur in a relational atom in
Q
, and
\overline{u}
and
\overline{v}
the set of free resp. bound variables in
Q
that occur in a non-covered inequality in
Q
.
In what follows, we call
Q_{\text{rel}}
the relational part of
Q
.

We note that if
Q
does not have any non-covered inequality then
Q_{\text{ineq}}
is in principle the empty query. For convenience, we then set it equal to
Q_{\text{ineq}}()\leftarrow\exists x.x\leq 1
in that case, which is equivalent to
t
.

The next proposition relates free-connexness of a CQ with inequalities to the free-connexness of its relational part.

Proposition 50
.

Let
Q
be a CQ and assume
\texttt{split}(Q)=(Q_{\text{rel}},Q_{\text{ineq}})
.

Q
is free-connex if, and only if,
Q_{\text{rel}}
is free-connex.

Proof 10.2
.

This is essentially because unary atoms (like inequality atoms) can never affect the (a)cyclicity of a CQ: if a CQ is acyclic it remains so if we add or remove unary atoms from its body.

The following property relates the evaluation of a CQ (with inequalities) to the evaluation of its relational part.
Let
Q
be a CQ over vocabulary
\Voc
. To each relational atom
\alpha=R(\overline{x})
we associate the query

Q[\alpha]:H(\overline{x})\leftarrow R(\overline{x})\wedge\iota_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\iota_{\ell}

where
\iota_{1},\dots,\iota_{\ell}
are all inequality atoms in
Q
covered by
\alpha
. We say that a database
db
is
atomically consistent
w.r.t
Q
if for every relation symbol
R\in\Voc
of arity
n
, every atom
\alpha=R(\overline{x})
in
Q
over relation symbol
R
, and every
n
-tuple
\overline{a}
we have

\text{if }R^{\textit{db}}(\overline{a})\not=\mymathbb{0}\text{ and }\overline{a}\models\overline{x}\qquad\text{then}\qquad\llbracket Q[\alpha]\rrbracket_{\textit{db}}(H)(\overline{a})\not=\mymathbb{0}.

Intuitively, if
db
is atomically consistent, then every tuple in a relation
R
that satisfies the atom
\alpha=R(\overline{x})
6
6

6

Note that
\overline{x}
may repeat variables, so an atom like
S(x,y,x)
requires the first and third components of
\overline{a}
to be equal.
also satisfies all inequalities covered by
\alpha
. Note that we may always make a given
db
atomically consistent with
Q
in
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
time: simply scan each relation
R
of
db
and for each tuple
\overline{a}
loop over the
R
-atoms
\alpha=R(\overline{x})
in
Q
. If
\overline{a}\models\overline{x}
then check that the constraints imposed by
Q[\alpha]
are satisfied. Since
Q[\alpha]
is a full query, this can be done in constant time per tuple in data complexity. We call this process
atomically reducing
db
, that is, of making
db
atomically-consistent with
Q
.

The following claims are straighforward to verify.

{clm}

Let
Q
be a CQ over vocabulary
\Voc
, let
db
be a database and let
\textit{db}^{\prime}
be its atomic reduction. Then
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})=\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db}^{\prime})
.

{clm}

Let
Q(\overline{x})
be a CQ with
\texttt{split}(Q)=(Q_{\text{rel}}(\overline{x}_{1}),Q_{\text{ineq}}(\overline{x}_{2}))
. If
db
is atomically consistent with
Q
then
\llceil{Q}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)=\llceil{Q_{\text{rel}}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}({\nu}|_{\overline{x}_{1}})\odot\llceil{Q_{\text{ineq}}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}({\nu}|_{\overline{x}_{2}})
for every valuation
\nu\colon\overline{x}
.

Lemma 51
.

Let
Q
be a CQ over vocabulary
\Voc
with
\texttt{split}(Q)=(Q_{\text{rel}},Q_{\text{ineq}})
and let
\mathcal{K}
be a semiring (not necessarily a semi-integral domain). Then
\texttt{Eval}(Q_{\text{ineq}},\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
.

Proof 10.3
.

Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
be the free variables of
Q_{\text{ineq}}
and
y_{1},\dots,y_{m}
its quantified variables. Let
\#z
denote the number of inequalities mentioning variable
z
(free or quantified). Assume w.l.o.g. that
Q_{\text{ineq}}
is of the form

H(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\leftarrow\exists y_{1},\dots,y_{m}.\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}\bigwedge_{j\leq\#x_{i}}(x_{i}\leq c^{i}_{j})\wedge\bigwedge_{1\leq p\leq m}\bigwedge_{q\leq\#y_{j}}(y_{p}\leq d^{p}_{q}).

Let
db
be a
\mathcal{K}
-database over
\Voc
. It is straightforward to verify that
((a_{1},\dots,a_{n}),k)\in\texttt{Answer}(Q_{\text{ineq}},\textit{db})
if, and only if,

•

For every
1\leq i\leq n
we have that
a_{i}\leq c_{i}
where
c_{i}:=\min_{1\leq j\leq\#x_{i}}\textit{db}(c^{i}_{j})
;

•

k\not=\mymathbb{0}
; and

•

k=(\underbrace{\mymathbb{1}\odot\dots\odot\mymathbb{1}}_{\#y_{1}\text{ times}})\oplus\dots\oplus(\underbrace{\mymathbb{1}\odot\dots\odot\mymathbb{1}}_{\#y_{m}\text{ times}})=\underbrace{\mymathbb{1}\oplus\dots\oplus\mymathbb{1}}_{m\text{ times}}

In particular, the annotation
k
is the same for each output tuple. Because
m
depends only on the query, we may compute
k
in constant time. Furthermore, we may compute the numbers
c_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
in
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
time. Computing
c_{i}
and
k
constitutes the preprocessing step of our enumeration algorithm. Having these numbers computed, it is then straightforward to enumerate the tuples: indicate end of enumeration immediately if
k=\mymathbb{0}
; otherwise enumerate all tuples
((a_{1},\dots,a_{n}),k)\in\texttt{Answer}(Q_{\text{ineq}},\textit{db})
with constant delay by means of a nested loop.

Proposition 52
.

Let
Q
be a CQ over
\Voc
with
\texttt{split}(Q)=(Q_{\text{rel}},Q_{\text{ineq}})
. If it holds that

\texttt{Eval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
and
\mathcal{K}
is a semi-integral domain then

\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
.

Proof 10.4
.

Let
db
be the input database. First make
db
atomically consistent with
Q
, which takes time
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
. Let
\textit{db}^{\prime}
be the resulting database. Note that, by Claim
10.1
, we have
\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db})=\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db}^{\prime})
. Because
\parallel\!\textit{db}^{\prime}\!\parallel=\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
it hence suffices to show that we may enumerate
\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db}^{\prime})
with constant delay after linear time preprocessing.

Thereto, we reason as follows. Because
\texttt{Eval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
we may compute a data structure in
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}^{\prime}\!\parallel)
time
that allows us to enumerate
\texttt{Vals}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db}^{\prime})
with constant delay. Furthermore, by Lemma
51
we may compute in
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}^{\prime}\!\parallel)
time another data structure that allows us to enumerate
\texttt{Vals}(Q_{\text{ineq}},\textit{db}^{\prime})
with constant delay. Computing both data structures constitutes our preprocessing step. By Claim
10.1
we can then use both data structures to enumerate
\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db}^{\prime})
with constant delay as follows:

•

For each
(\nu_{1},k_{1})\in\texttt{Vals}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}^{Q})

–

For each
(\nu_{2},k_{2})\in\texttt{Vals}(Q_{\text{ineq}},\textit{db})
, output
(\nu_{1}\cup\nu_{2},k_{1}\odot k_{2})
.

Note that
k_{1}\odot k_{2}\not=\mymathbb{0}
as required since
k_{1}\not=\mymathbb{0}
,
k_{2}\not=\mymathbb{0}
and
\mathcal{K}
is a semi-integral domain.

We can finally prove Theorem
48
.

Proof 10.5

(Proof of Theorem
48
)
.

If
Q=(Q_{\text{rel}},Q_{\text{ineq}})
is free-connex then by Proposition
50
we have that
Q_{\text{rel}}
is free-connex. By Proposition
49
and because
\mathcal{K}
is a semi-integral domain,
\texttt{Eval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
. And finally due to Proposition
52
, the latter implies
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
.

Now we prove the second part of the result, i.e., achieve the same processing complexity for the matrix evaluation problem
\texttt{Eval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
. Fix a matrix-to-relational schema encoding
\Rel
on
\Sch(\mathbf{Q})
such that the head atom of
Q
is binary. The former requirement arises purely due to
\texttt{Answer}(\mathbf{Q},\mathcal{I})
being defined as
\{(i,j,\llbracket\mathbf{Q}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})(\mathbf{H})_{i,j})\mid\llbracket\mathbf{Q}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})(\mathbf{H})_{i,j}\neq\mymathbb{0},i\leq\mathcal{I}(\alpha),j\leq\mathcal{I}(\beta)\}
where
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{H}:=e
and
\mathbf{H}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
.

By Corollary
22
there exists a free-connex CQ
Q
over vocabulary
\Voc(Q)=\Rel(\Sch(\mathbf{Q}))
that simulates
\mathbf{Q}
under
\Rel
. As such, we may reduce
\texttt{Eval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
to
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
: given a matrix instance
\mathcal{I}
over
\Sch
, compute
Rel(\mathcal{I})
and call the enumeration algorithm for
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
. By definition of simulation, the set
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\Rel(\mathcal{I}))
is exactly the same as
\texttt{Answer}(\mathbf{Q},\mathcal{I})
. Hence, because
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
and because
\parallel\!\Rel(\mathcal{I})\!\parallel=\bigo(\parallel\!\mathcal{I}\!\parallel)
it follows that
\texttt{Eval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\mathcal{I}\!\parallel,1)
.

10.2.
Lower bounds for free-connex queries

Next, we show how to extend the lower bounds of
[
BDG07
]

and
[
BGS20
]
to our setting. As in those works,
our lower bounds are for CQ
without self-joins
. Further, we need some additional
restrictions for inequalities. Recall that an inequality
x\leq c
in
Q
is

covered
, if there exists a relational atom in
Q
that mentions
x
. We
say that
Q
is
constant-disjoint
if (i) for all covered inequalities

x\leq c
we have
c\not=1
, and (ii) for all pairs
(x\leq c,y\leq d)
in

Q
of covered inequality
x\leq c
and non-covered inequality
y\leq d
if

c=d
then
y\not\in\free(Q)
. In other words, if a constant symbol other
than
1
occurs in both a covered and non-covered inequality in
Q
, then it
occurs with a bound variable in the non-covered inequality.

A semiring
\mathcal{K}=(K,\oplus,\odot,\mymathbb{0},\mymathbb{1})

is
zero-sum free

[
HW96
,
HW98
]
if for all
a,b\in K
it holds that
a\oplus b=\mymathbb{0}
implies
a=b=\mymathbb{0}
.
The
subsemiring of
\mathcal{K}
generated
by
\mymathbb{0}
and
\mymathbb{1}
is the semiring
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=(K^{\prime},\oplus,\odot,\mymathbb{0},\mymathbb{1})
where
K^{\prime}
is the smallest set closed that (i) contains
\mymathbb{0}
and
\mymathbb{1}
and (ii) is closed under
\oplus
and
\odot
.

We will extend the lower bound from
[
BDG07
,
BGS20
]
as follows.

Theorem 53
.

Let
Q
be a CQ over
\Voc
without self-joins and constant-disjoint. Let

\mathcal{K}
be a semiring such that the subsemiring generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and

\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is zero-sum free. If
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
can be evaluated with linear-time preprocessing and constant-delay, then
Q

is free-connex, unless either the Sparse Boolean Matrix Multiplication, the
Triangle Detection, or the
(k,k+1)
-Hyperclique conjecture is false.

It is important to note that most semirings used in practice, like
\mathbb{B}
,
\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathbb{R}
, are such that the subsemiring generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is zero-sum free.

To see why the constant-disjointness condition is necessary, consider the CQ:

Q:H(x,z)\leftarrow\exists y.R(x,y)\wedge S(y,z)\wedge y\leq 1.

This query violates condition (i) of constant-disjointness, and is not free-connex. Nevertheless, its evaluation is in
\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
: because
y
can take only a single value, we can evaluate
Q
by doing preprocessing and enumeration for
Q^{\prime}\colon H(x,y,z)\leftarrow R(x,y)\wedge S(y,z)\wedge y\leq 1
instead, which is free-connex, hence in
\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
. Whenever a tuple
(x,y,z)
is enumerated for
Q^{\prime}
we yield the tuple
(x,z)
for the enumeration of
Q
. This is also constant-delay and without duplicates due to the inequality
y\leq 1
.

A similar argument holds for condition (ii) of
constant-disjointness. Consider

P\colon H(x,z,u)\leftarrow\exists y.R(x,y)\wedge S(y,z)\wedge y\leq c\wedge u\leq c,

which violates condition (ii) of constant-disjointess. Again, this
query is not free-connex but its evaluation is in
\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
. To see why,
consider the query

P^{\prime}\colon H(x,y,z)\leftarrow R(x,y)\wedge S(y,z)\wedge y\leq c
, which is
free-connex and therefore in
\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
. To evaluate
P
we simply do all the
(linear-time) preprocessing for
P^{\prime}
and use
P^{\prime}
enumeration procedure to
enumerate
P
with constant delay. This works as follows. During the
enumeration of
P
maintain a lookup table
L
mapping
(x,z)
tuples to the
smallest natural number
u
such that all tuples
(x,z,u)
that have already
been output for
P
satisfy
u\leq L(x,z)
. If
L(x,z)=0
then no entry
occurs in the lookup table. To enumerate
P
we invoke
P^{\prime}
enumeration
procedure. When a tuple
(x,y,z)
is enumerated by
P^{\prime}
, we output

(x,z,L(x,z)+1)
for the enumeration of
P
. Additionally, we update

L(x,z):=L(x,z)+1
. Once
P^{\prime}
’s enumeration is exhausted, we iterate over the entries of the lookup table, and for each such entry
(x,z)
output all remaining tuples
(x,z,u)
with
L(x,z)<u^{\prime}\leq c
. This last step is not necessarily constant-delay (there may be an unbounded number of entries with
L(x,z)=c
, yielding no output), but this can be fixed by removing
(x,z)
from
L
during the enumeration of
P^{\prime}
whenever
L(x,z)
is updated to become
c
. The constraint that
y\leq c\wedge u\leq c
ensures that the enumeration is still correct.

Theorem
53
allows to also derive lower bounds for
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
.
Unfortunately, given the asymmetry between the relational and matrix settings,
the lower bounds do not immediately transfer from the relational to the matrix
setting. Specifically, we need a syntactical restriction for
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
that
implies the constant-disjointedness restriction in the translation
of Theorem
10
. In order to achieve
this, the prenex normal form of
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
expressions (defined in
Section
4
) proves useful. Let

e=\Sigma\overline{\mathbf{v}}.\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbf{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbf{s}_{n}\times\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{T}
be a
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
sentence in prenex normal form. A vector
variable
\mathbf{v}\in\overline{\mathbf{v}}
is
covered
in
e
if (i) there exists a scalar
subexpression
\mathbf{s}_{i}
of the form
\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{u}
or

\mathbf{u}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{v}
; or (ii) if there exists scalar subexpression
\mathbf{s}_{i}

doing a vector multiplication
\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{u}
or
\mathbf{u}^{T}\mathbf{v}
with
\mathbf{u}
covered in
e
. (Note that the latter notion is recursive.) The sentence
e
is
constant-disjoint
if for every pair

\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in\overline{\mathbf{v}}
of quantified vector variables of the same type,

\mathbf{v}\colon(\gamma,1)
and
\mathbf{w}\colon(\gamma,1)
, if
\mathbf{v}
is covered and
\mathbf{w}

is not then
\mathbf{w}\notin\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}
. A
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
sentence
e\colon(\alpha,\beta)
is
constant-disjoint
if its conversion into prenex normal form is constant-disjoint.

The former is straightforwardly extended to a
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\mathbf{Q}
. The following lower bound is now attainable.

Corollary 54
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}
be a
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query over
\Sch
such that
\mathbf{Q}
does not repeat
matrix symbols and
\mathbf{Q}
is constant-disjoint. Let
\mathcal{K}
be a semiring such
that the subsemiring generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}

is zero-sum free. If
\texttt{Eval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
can be evaluated with linear-time
preprocessing and constant-delay, then
\mathbf{Q}
is equivalent to a
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query,
unless either the Sparse Boolean Matrix Multiplication, the Triangle
Detection, or the
(k,k+1)
-Hyperclique conjecture is false.

The remainder of this section is devoted to proving Theorem
53
and Corollary
54
.

Lower bound for free-connex CQs

We begin by proving Theorem
53
, which is done in two steps. First we show that constant-disjointness is a sufficient condition for us to be able to reduce the evaluation problem of
\text{CQs}^{\not\leq}
over
\mathbb{B}
-databases to the evaluation problem of CQs over
\mathbb{B}
-databases. Second, we show that the evaluation problem of CQs over
\mathbb{B}
-databases can be reduced to the evaluation problem of CQs over
\mathcal{K}
-databases, assuming that the subsemiring generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is zero-sum free.

Proposition 55
.

Let
Q
be a constant-disjoint CQ over
\Voc
with
\texttt{split}(Q)=(Q_{\text{rel}},Q_{\text{ineq}})
. If
Q
is
constant-disjoint and
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
then

\texttt{Eval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathbb{B})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
.

Proof 10.6
.

We reduce
\texttt{Eval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc_{\text{rel}},\mathbb{B})
to
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})
. Denote the free variables of
Q
,
Q_{\text{rel}}
and
Q_{\text{ineq}}
by
\overline{x}
,
\overline{x}_{1}
and
\overline{x}_{2}
, respectively. Note that
\overline{x}_{1}
and
\overline{x}_{2}
are disjoint and
\var(\overline{x})=\var(\overline{x}_{1})\cup\var(\overline{x}_{2})
. Consider an arbitrary database
\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}
over
\Voc
, input to
\texttt{Eval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathbb{B})
. Construct the database
db
, input to
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})
as follows.

Recall that all data values occurring in tuples in
\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}
are non-zero natural numbers. Scan
\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}
and compute the largest natural number
M
occurring in
\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}
. Then construct the database
db
by setting

•

R^{\textit{db}}=R^{\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}}
for each relation symbol
R\in\Voc
;

•

db(c):=M
for all constant symbols
c\in\Voc
that occur in a covered inequality in
Q
. Note that
c\not=1
because
Q
is constant-disjoint, and hence we are allowed to map
c\mapsto M
;

•

db(c):=1
for all other constant symbols
c\in\Voc
.

The computation of
db
is clearly in
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}\!\parallel)
and
\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel=\parallel\!\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}\!\parallel
.

Note that, because
Q_{\text{rel}}
consists of relational atoms only, its evaluation is hence independent of the values assigned to the constant symbols by

\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}
. Therefore,
\texttt{Answer}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db}_{\text{rel}})=\texttt{Answer}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db})
. To obtain the proposition, it hence suffices to show that
\texttt{Answer}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db})
may be enumerated with constant delay after further linear time preprocessing. We do so by showing that
\texttt{Vals}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db},
) may be enumerated with constant delay after linear time preprocessing as follows. Observe that
db
is atomically consistent with
Q
. This is by construction since for every covered inequality
x\leq c
we have
\textit{db}(c)
equal to
M
, with
M
larger than any value in
db
. Hence, by Claim
10.1
we have

\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db})=\{(\nu_{1}\cup\nu_{2},\texttt{t})\mid(\nu_{1},\texttt{t})\in\texttt{Vals}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db}),(\nu_{2},\texttt{t})\in\texttt{Vals}(Q_{\text{ineq}},\textit{db})\}.

As such,
\texttt{Vals}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db})=\{({\nu}|_{\overline{x}_{1}},\texttt{t})\mid(\nu,\texttt{t})\in\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db})\}
and we may hence enumerate
\texttt{Vals}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db})
by enumerating the elements of
\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db})
and for each such element output
({\nu}|_{\overline{x}_{1}},\texttt{t})
. Note that the resulting enumeration is necessarily duplicate-free as required: by definition of
db
the set
\texttt{Vals}(Q_{\text{ineq}},\textit{db})
consists of a single
element: the pair
(\overline{x}_{2}\mapsto 1,\texttt{t})
. To see why this is neccesarily the case observe that, by definition, all free variables
y\in\overline{x}_{2}
of
Q_{\text{ineq}}
must occur in an non-covered inequality
y\leq c
. Because
Q
is constant-disjoint this constant
c
is such that there is no covered inequality
z\leq d
in
Q
with
c=d
. (If there were,
y
would be non-free.) This means that we have set
c^{\textit{db}}=1
. Therefore, for every free variable
y
of
Q_{\text{ineq}}
, we neccesarily have that
\nu(y)=1
in a resulting valuation. As such, all valuations in
\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db})
are constant on the variables in
\overline{x}_{2}
and duplicates can hence not occur when projecting on
\overline{x}_{1}
. The proposition then follows because we may enumerate
\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db})
with
\bigo(1)
delay after linear time preprocessing by assumption.

Proposition 56
.

Let
Q
be a CQ over
\Voc
and
\mathcal{K}
a semiring such that the subsemiring
generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is non-trivial and zero-sum free. If

\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
then
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
.

Proof 10.7
.

Let
\mathcal{K}=(K,\oplus,\odot,\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}},\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}})
be a a semiring such that the subsemiring generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is non-trivial and zero-sum free. We reduce
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})
to
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
as follows. Let
\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}
be a
\mathbb{B}
-database over

\Voc
, input to
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})
. Construct the
\mathcal{K}
-database
\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}
, input to
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
by setting

•

for every relation symbol
R\in\Voc

\llceil{R}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}\colon\overline{d}\mapsto\begin{cases}\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}\text{ if }R^{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}(\overline{d})=\texttt{t}\\
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

•

\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}(c):=\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}(c)
for every constant symbol
c
.

Note that this takes time
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}\!\parallel)
.

By assumption,
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
thus the set
\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}})
can be enumerated with constant delay after a linear time preprocessing. Now, it suffices to do the following: for every outputed tuple
(\nu,\llceil{Q}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}(\nu))
we output
(\nu,\texttt{t})
. This forms a constant delay enumeration for the set
\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}})
, since we claim that for every valuation
\nu
we have
(\nu,\texttt{t})\in\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}})
if and only if
(\nu,k)\in\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}})
for some
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}\neq k\in K
. We next argue why this claim holds.

Let
\varphi=\exists\overline{y}.R_{1}(\overline{z}_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge R_{n}(\overline{z}_{m})\wedge w_{1}\leq c_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\overline{w}_{m}\leq c_{m}
be the body of
Q(\overline{x})
and assume
\nu\colon\overline{x}
.

First, if
(\nu,\llceil{Q}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}(\nu))\in\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}})
then in particular
\llceil{Q}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}(\nu)\neq\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
. Because

\displaystyle\llceil{Q}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\mu\colon\var(\varphi)\text{ s.t.}{\mu}|_{\overline{x}}=\nu}\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\mu\colon\var(\varphi)\text{ s.t.}{\mu}|_{\overline{x}}=\nu}R_{1}^{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{\overline{z}_{1}})\odot\cdots\odot R_{n}^{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{\overline{z}_{n}})\odot

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\llceil{w_{1}\leq c_{1}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{w_{1}})\odot\cdots\odot\llceil{w_{m}\leq c_{m}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{w_{m}})

we know in particular that there exists some
\mu
such that

R_{1}^{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{\overline{z}_{1}})\odot\cdots\odot R_{n}^{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{\overline{z}_{n}})\odot\llceil{w_{1}\leq c_{1}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{w_{1}})\odot\cdots\odot\llceil{w_{m}\leq c_{m}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{w_{m}})\neq\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}.

This is because in any semiring, a sum of terms can be non-
\mymathbb{0}
only if one term is non-
\mymathbb{0}
. Moreover, a product is non-
\mymathbb{0}
only if all of
its factors are non-
\mymathbb{0}
. By construction,
R_{i}^{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{\overline{z}_{i}})\not=\mymathbb{0}
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
and
\llceil{w_{j}\leq c_{j}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{w})\not=\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
for all
j=1,\ldots,m
only if
R_{i}^{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}({\mu}|_{\overline{z}_{i}})=\texttt{t}
and
\llceil{w_{j}\leq c_{j}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}({\mu}|_{w})=\texttt{t}
for all
i
and
j
. Thus
(\nu,\texttt{t})\in\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}})
.

Second, if
(\nu,\texttt{t})\in\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}})
then in particular
\llceil{Q}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}(\nu)=\texttt{t}
. Because

\displaystyle\llceil{Q}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\bigvee_{\mu\colon\var(\varphi)\text{ s.t.}{\mu}|_{\overline{x}}=\nu}R_{1}^{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}({\mu}|_{\overline{z}_{1}})\wedge\cdots\wedge R_{n}^{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}({\mu}|_{\overline{z}_{n}})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\wedge\llceil{w_{1}\leq c_{1}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}({\mu}|_{w_{1}})\wedge\cdots\wedge\llceil{w_{m}\leq c_{m}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}({\mu}|_{w_{m}})

this implies there is some valuation
\mu
such that

R_{1}^{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}({\mu}|_{\overline{z}_{1}})\wedge\cdots\wedge R_{n}^{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}({\mu}|_{\overline{z}_{n}})\wedge\llceil{w_{1}\leq c_{1}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}({\mu}|_{w_{1}})\wedge\cdots\wedge\llceil{w_{m}\leq c_{m}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}({\mu}|_{w_{m}})=\texttt{t}.

In particular,
R_{i}^{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}({\mu}|_{\overline{z}_{i}})=\texttt{t}
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
and
\llceil{w_{j}\leq c_{j}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}({\mu}|_{w_{j}})=\texttt{t}
for
j=1,\ldots,m
. Then, by construction of
\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}
we have
R_{i}^{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{\overline{z}_{i}})=\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
and
\llceil{w_{j}\leq c_{j}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{w_{j}})=\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
for
j=1,\ldots,m
. Hence,

R_{1}^{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{\overline{z}_{1}})\odot\cdots\odot R_{n}^{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{\overline{z}_{n}})\odot\llceil{w_{1}\leq c_{1}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{w_{1}})\odot\cdots\odot\llceil{w_{m}\leq c_{m}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}({\mu}|_{w_{m}})=\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}.

Thus, since the subsemiring generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is non-trivial and zero-sum free,

\displaystyle\llceil{Q}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\mu\colon\var(\varphi)\text{ s.t.}{\mu}|_{\overline{x}}=\nu}\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}(\mu)\not=\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}

Recall that by convention we only consider commutative and non-trivial semirings throughout the paper. As such when we consider an arbitrary semiring
\mathcal{K}
, then we assume that this semiring is commutative and non-trivial. For such semirings, it is straightforward to check that the sub-semiring generated by
\mymathbb{0}
and
\mymathbb{1}
is also commutative and non-trivial. (We note that we have only explicitly add the “non-trivial” requirement in the previous proposition because the proof uses this property.) Given this discussion, we can now prove Theorem
53
.

Proof 10.8

(Proof of Theorem
53
)
.

Let
\texttt{split}(Q)=(Q_{\text{rel}},Q_{\text{ineq}})
and assume
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
. Because the subsemiring of
\mathcal{K}
generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is non-trivial and zero-sum free,
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
holds by Proposition
56
. This implies
\texttt{Eval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathbb{B})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
, due to Proposition
55
. Theorem
9
yields that
Q_{\text{rel}}
is free-connex, unless either the Sparse Boolean Matrix Multiplication, the Triangle Detection, or the
(k,k+1)
-Hyperclique conjecture is false. Finally, since
Q_{\text{rel}}
is free-connex if and only if
Q
is free-connex (Proposition
50
), this immediately implies that
Q
is free-connex, unless one of these hypothesis is false.

Lower bound for
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
queries

We now proceed to prove Corollary
54
. A necessary ingredient to prove the lower bound is to have
enumeration-suited
matrix-to-relational encodings.
Let
\mathbf{Q}\coloneqq\mathbf{H}=e
be a
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query. A matrix-to-relational encoding
\Rel
is
enumeration-suited
for
\mathbf{Q}
if

•

\Rel(\mathbf{A})
is a nullary relation if
\mathbf{A}
is of scalar type and a unary relation if
\mathbf{A}
is of vector type (but not scalar), for every
\mathbf{A}\in\Sch\!\left(\mathbf{Q}\right)\setminus\mathbf{H}
.

•

\Rel(\mathbf{H})
is a binary relation.

One can easily see that for every
\mathbf{Q}
there exists some matrix-to-relational encoding
\Rel
that is enumeration-suited for
\mathbf{Q}
(i.e., choose the natural encoding that maps scalars to nullary relations, vectors to unary relations, and matrices to binary relations). Therefore, to prove Corollary
54
we may always choose a
\Rel
that is enumeration-suited, and apply the following proposition to move from the matrix setting to the relational setting.

Proposition 57
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{H}\coloneqq e
be a
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query and let
\Rel
be an enumeration-suited matrix-to-relational encoding for
\mathbf{Q}
. If
\mathbf{Q}
is constant-disjoint then there exists a constant-disjoint CQ that simulates
\mathbf{Q}
w.r.t.
\Rel
.

Proof 10.9
.

Assume that
e
, when converted into prenex normal form, is of the form
e=\Sigma\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{k}.\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbf{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbf{s}_{n}\times\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{T}
and that this prenex normal form is constant-disjoint. Let
\overline{\mathbf{v}}=\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{k}
.

The proof proceeds in two steps. (i) We first show that because
\Rel
is enumeration-suited, the CQ
Q

with equality atoms
simulating
\mathbf{Q}
that is obtained through the translation given in the proof Proposition
4
, is constant-disjoint. (ii) Then we show that eliminating equality atoms from
Q
yields a CQ
Q^{\prime}
that is still constant disjoint.

Formally for point (i) to make sense, we say that a CQ with equality atoms is constant-disjoint if satisfies the same conditions as for normal CQs, but with the modification that now an inequality atom
z\leq c
is covered if variable
z
is covered: it appears in a relational atom or it appears in an equality atom
z=w
and
w
is covered. Note that this definition is recursive. For example, in the formula
\exists u,v,w.A(x,u)\wedge u=v\wedge v=w
we have that
u,v,w
are covered.

We first prove (i). The
Q
CQ simulating
\mathbf{Q}
obtained by Proposition
4
is of the form
Q\colon H(x,y)\leftarrow\exists v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}.a_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge a_{n}\wedge x\leq\alpha\wedge y\leq\beta
where each
a_{i}
is determined as follows, for
1\leq i\leq n
. Let non-bold symbol
A
denote
\Rel(\mathbf{A})
, and let non-bold
w
be the FO variable selected respectively for
\mathbf{w}\in\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{k}\}
.

•

If
\mathbf{s}_{i}=\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{w}
then:

–

a_{i}\coloneqq A(v,w)
if
A
is binary. Note that since
\Rel
is enumeration suited, this case can only happen if
\mathbf{A}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
with
\alpha\neq 1
and
\beta\neq 1
. Due to well-typedness,
\mathbf{v}\colon(\alpha,1)
and
\mathbf{w}\colon(\beta,1)
.

–

a_{i}\coloneqq A(v)\wedge w\leq 1
if
A
is
unary and
\mathbf{A}\colon(\alpha,1)
. Note that since
\Rel
is enumeration suited, necessarily
\alpha\not=1
. For later use we remark that since
\mathbf{s}_{i}
is well-typed,
\mathbf{v}\colon(\alpha,1)
and
\mathbf{w}\colon(1,1)
in this case.

–

a_{i}\coloneqq v\leq 1\wedge A(w)
if
A
is unary and
\mathbf{A}\colon(1,\alpha)
. Since
\Rel
is enumeration-suited, necessarily
\alpha\not=1
. For later use we remark that since
\mathbf{s}_{i}
is well-typed,
\mathbf{v}\colon(1,1)
and
\mathbf{w}\colon(\alpha,1)
.

–

a_{i}\coloneqq v\leq 1\wedge w\leq 1\wedge A()
if
A
is nullary and
\mathbf{A}\colon(1,1)
. Note that necessarily
\mathbf{v}\colon(1,1)
and
\mathbf{w}\colon(1,1)
in this case.

•

If
\mathbf{s}_{i}=\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{w}
with
\mathbf{v}\colon(\gamma,1)
and
\mathbf{w}\colon(\gamma,1)
then:

–

a_{i}\coloneqq v\leq\gamma
if
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{w}
.

–

a_{i}\coloneqq v\leq\gamma\wedge w\leq\gamma\wedge v=w
if
\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{w}
.

Recall that a CQ
Q
is constant-disjoint if (1) for all covered inequalities
z\leq c
we have
c\not=1
, and (2) for all pairs
(v\leq c,y\leq d)
in
Q
of covered inequality
w\leq c
and non-covered inequality
w\leq d
if
c=d
then
w\not\in\free(Q)
.

We start by showing that in
Q
condition (1) always holds. Consider an inequality
z_{1}\leq 1
in
Q
. This necessarily appears in some subformula
a_{i_{1}}
with
\mathbf{s}_{i_{1}}=\mathbf{v}_{i_{1}}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i_{1}}
and
\mathbf{z}_{1}\in\{\mathbf{v}_{i_{1}},\mathbf{w}_{i_{1}}\}
.
By inspection of the construction above we observe that necessarily
\mathbf{z}_{1}\colon(1,1)
. We further observe that
z_{1}
does not appear in a relational atom in the subformula
a_{i_{1}}
. Hence, if
z
is covered in
Q
, it must be because there are other subformulas
a_{i_{2}},\dots,a_{i_{\ell}}
that contain equality atoms
z_{j-1}=z_{j}
for
2\leq j\leq\ell
, as well as a subformula
a_{i_{\ell+1}}
where
z_{j}
occurs in a relational atom. The corresponding subexpressions
\mathbf{s}_{i_{j}}
of
e
with
1\leq j\leq\ell
are necessarily of the form
\mathbf{v}_{i_{j}}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i_{j}}
with
\mathbf{z}_{i_{j}}\in\{\mathbf{v}_{i_{j}},\mathbf{w}_{i_{j}}\}
. Moreover,
\mathbf{s}_{\ell}
must be of the form
\mathbf{v}_{\ell+1}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{\ell+1}
with
\mathbf{z}_{i_{\ell}}\in\{\mathbf{v}_{i_{\ell}},\mathbf{w}_{i_{\ell}}\}
. Because each
\mathbf{s}_{i_{j}}
is well-typed and all
\mathbf{v}_{i_{j}},\mathbf{w}_{i_{j}}
are vector variables, it follows that each
\mathbf{z}_{i_{j}}
has type
(1,1)
for
1\leq j\leq\ell
. This yields a contradiction: it is readily verified that when the construction generates a relational atom containing a variable
z_{\ell}
, that variable cannot have type
(1,1)
.

We prove that if
\mathbf{Q}
is constant-disjoint, then property (2) holds in
Q
. We also do this by contradiction. Suppose it does not hold, i.e., suppose there is a pair
(v\leq c,w\leq c)
in
Q
of covered inequality
v\leq c
and non-covered inequality
w\leq c
and assume that
w
is free in
Q
. Then
w\in\{x,y\}
and since
v\leq c
is covered it is the case that there is some
a_{i}\in\{A(v,z),A(z,v),A(v)\wedge z\leq 1,z\leq 1\wedge A(v)\}
. Because
\Rel
is enumeration-suited, we have that
\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\{\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{v}\}
with
\mathbf{v}\colon(\gamma,1)
with
\gamma\neq 1
. Hence we have a pair
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
where
\mathbf{v}\colon(\gamma,1)
is covered,
\mathbf{w}\colon(\gamma,1)
is not covered and
\mathbf{w}\in\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}
, which means that
\mathbf{Q}
is not constant-disjoint, a contradiction.

For step (ii), let
Q^{\prime}
be the equivalent CQ after unification and removal of equality atoms on
Q
. First note that since
Q
is well-typed w.r.t.
\Rel^{-1}
also
Q^{\prime}
is well-typed w.r.t
\Rel^{-1}
since we only unify variables, and since all unified variables must have had the same type in
Q
by definition of the
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
type system.
Further observe that because our definition of constant-disjointness in
Q
takes equality into account, and because
Q^{\prime}
is obtained by unifying variables that must be equal, also
Q^{\prime}
must necessarily be constant-disjoint.

We are ready to prove Corollary
54
.

Proof 10.10

(Proof of Corollary
54
)
.

Let
\Rel
be a matrix-to-relational encoding scheme over
\Sch
that is enumeration-suited for
\mathbf{Q}
. Let
\Mat=\Rel^{-1}
be its inverse relational-to-matrix encoding.

We apply Proposition
57
on
\mathbf{Q}
to obtain binary CQ
Q
over
\Voc=\Rel(\Sch)
that simulates
\mathbf{Q}
w.r.t.
\Rel
. We know that
Q
is binary, self-join free and constant-disjoint. We next modify
Q
into a query
Q^{\prime}
over
\Voc
such that the following properties hold:

(1)

Q^{\prime}
continues to simulate
\mathbf{Q}
w.r.t.
\Rel
;

(2)

Q^{\prime}
continues to be constant-disjoint;

(3)

\texttt{Eval}(Q^{\prime},\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
.

This suffices to prove the proposition. Indeed, by Theorem
53

Q^{\prime}
is necessarily free-connex unless unless either the Sparse Boolean Matrix Multiplication, the Triangle Detection, or the
(k,k+1)
-Hyperclique conjecture is false. If
Q^{\prime}
is free-connex, then by Corollary
22
there exists a
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
that simulates
Q^{\prime}
w.r.t
\Mat=\Rel^{-1}
. Consequently,
\mathbf{Q}
and
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
are then equivalent.

Intuitively, the modification of
Q
into
Q^{\prime}
is necessary to obtain property (P3): the fact that
\texttt{Eval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\mathcal{I}\!\parallel,1)
only implies that we may evaluate
Q
with linear time preprocessing and constant delay
on databases that encode some matrix instance
. To apply Theorem
53
, by contrast we need to show that we can evaluate
Q
on
arbitrary databases
. The crux will be that we obtain
Q^{\prime}
by adding extra inequalities to
Q
, such that evaluating
Q^{\prime}
on an arbitrary database yields the same result as evaluating it (and
Q
,
\mathbf{Q}
) on some matrix instance.

The definition of
Q^{\prime}
is as follows. Assume
Q
is of the form
Q:H(\overline{y})\leftarrow\exists\overline{z}.\psi
with
\psi
quantifier free. For every relational atom
\alpha=R(\overline{x})
of
\psi
we define a formula
\varphi_{\alpha}
as follows. This formula is a conjunction of inequalities:

•

If
\alpha=R(x_{1},x_{2})
with
R
binary and
\Mat(R)\colon(\beta,\gamma)
then
\varphi_{\alpha}:=x_{1}\leq\Rel(\beta)\wedge x_{2}\leq\Rel(\gamma)
. Note that, by definition of
\Rel
,
\Rel(\alpha)\not=1\not=\Rel(\beta)
.

•

If
\alpha=R(x_{1})
with
R
unary then by definition of
\Rel
either
\Mat(R)\colon(\beta,1)
or
\Mat(R)\colon(1,\beta)
for some size symbol
\beta\not=1
. Then take
\varphi_{\alpha}:=x_{1}\leq\Rel(\beta)
. Note that by definition of matrix-to-relational encoding schemes,
\Rel(\beta)\not=1
since
\beta\not=1
.

•

If
\alpha=R()
with
R
nullary then by definition of
\Rel
we have
\Mat(R)\colon(1,1)
and we take
\varphi_{\alpha}
the empty formula (which is equivalent to true).

Then we define
Q^{\prime}
to be the CQ

Q^{\prime}\colon H(\overline{y})\leftarrow\exists z.(\psi\wedge\bigwedge_{\alpha\text{ rel. atom in }\psi}\varphi_{\alpha}).

Note that
Q^{\prime}
continues to simulate
\mathbf{Q}
over
\Rel
(property P1). Indeed, for any matrix instance
\mathcal{I}
over
\Sch
we know that
\texttt{Answer}(\mathbf{Q},\mathcal{I})=\texttt{Answer}(Q,\Rel(\mathcal{I}))
. By definition,
\Rel(\mathcal{I})
is consistent with
\Rel^{-1}=\Mat
, and therefore the tuples in
\Rel(\mathcal{I})
vacuously satisfy all the extra inequalities that we have added to
Q
to obtain
Q^{\prime}
. As such,
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\Rel(\mathcal{I}))=\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\Rel(\mathcal{I}))
. Thus,
Q^{\prime}
continues to simulate
\mathbf{Q}
.

Also note that
Q^{\prime}
is constant-disjoint (property P2). Indeed:

•

We have only added covered inequalities to
Q
to obtain
Q^{\prime}
. Any such covered inequality that we have added was of the form
x\leq c
with
c\not=1
. Therefore, since
Q
does not have covered inequalities of the form
x\leq 1
, neither does
Q^{\prime}
.

•

We did not add any non-covered inequalities to
Q
to obtain
Q^{\prime}
. Moreover,
Q^{\prime}
has the same free variables as
Q
. Therefore, all non-covered inequalities
y\leq c
in
Q^{\prime}
continue to be such that
y
is not free in
Q^{\prime}
, unless
c=1
. Hence for all pairs
(x\leq c,y\leq d)
of covered inequality
x\leq c
and non-covered inequality
y\leq d
with
d\neq 1
in
Q^{\prime}
we have that
y
is not free in
Q^{\prime}
as required for constant-disjointness.

Finally, we show that
\texttt{Eval}(Q^{\prime},\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
(property P3), as claimed. We do this by reducing
\texttt{Eval}(Q^{\prime},\Voc,\mathcal{K})
to
\texttt{Eval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
as follows. Let
db
be a
\mathcal{K}
-database over
\Voc
, input to
\texttt{Eval}(Q^{\prime},\Voc,\mathcal{K})
. First, create the database
\textit{db}^{\prime}
that is equal to
db
except that for every relation symbol
R\in\Voc
that is not mentioned in
Q
we set
R^{\textit{db}^{\prime}}
to empty. This can clearly be done in linear time. Note that, if
R
is not mentioned in
Q
, then the answer of
Q
on
db
is independent of the contents of
R^{\textit{db}}
. Therefore,
\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db})=\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db}^{\prime})
. Subsequently, compute
\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}
, the atomic reduction of
\textit{db}^{\prime}
w.r.t.
Q
. Computing
\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}
can be done in time
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}^{\prime}\!\parallel)=\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
. By Claim
10.1
we have
\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db})=\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db}^{\prime})=\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db}^{\prime\prime})
. Now verify the following claim:
\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}
is consistent w.r.t.
\Mat=\Rel^{-1}
. This is because we have added, for each matrix symbol
\mathbf{A}
with
\Rel(\mathbf{A})
occurring in
Q
, the inequalities that are required by consistency as atomic inequalities to
Q^{\prime}
. (The matrix symbols
\mathbf{A}
with
\Rel(\mathbf{A})
not occurring in
Q
are empty in
\textit{db}^{\prime}
and hence vacuously consistent w.r.t.
\Mat
.) Since
\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}
is consistent with
\Mat
we have that
\Mat(\textit{db}^{\prime\prime})
is a matrix instance over
\Sch
and hence a valid input to
\mathbf{Q}
. Then, because
Q^{\prime}
simulates
\mathbf{Q}
, we have
\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db})=\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db}^{\prime\prime})=\texttt{Answer}(\mathbf{Q},\Mat(\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}))
. The last equality holds because the head of
Q^{\prime}
is a binary relation since
\Rel
is enumeration-suited for
\mathbf{Q}
.

Because
\texttt{Eval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
is in
\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\mathcal{I}\!\parallel,1)
it hence follows that with additional preprocessing of time
\bigo(\parallel\!\Mat(\textit{db}^{\prime\prime})\!\parallel)=\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
we may enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db}^{\prime\prime})=\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db})
with constant delay, as desired.

11.
Efficient evaluation of q-hierarhical queries

This last section derives our algorithmic results for qh-CQ and
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
. qh-CQ is the subclass of CQ that allows efficient evaluation in a dynamic setting, where insertion and deletion of tuples are admitted. Then, similar to the previous section, we aim to lift these algorithmic results to
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
.

We start by introducing the dynamic setting for query evaluation, and then study the upper and lower bounds.

11.1.
The dynamic evaluation setting

We move now to the dynamic query evaluation both in the relational and matrix scenarios. Specifically, we consider the following set of updates. Recall that
\mathcal{K}=(K,\oplus,\odot,\mymathbb{0},\mymathbb{1})
is a semiring and
\Voc
a vocabulary.

•

A single-tuple
insertion
(over
\mathcal{K}
and
\Voc
) is an operation

u=\texttt{insert}(R,\overline{d},k)
with
R\in\Voc
,
\overline{d}
a
tuple of arity
\arity(R)
, and
k\in K
. When applied to a database

db
it induces the database
\textit{db}+u
that is
identical to
db
, but
R^{\textit{db}+u}(\overline{d})=R^{\textit{db}}(\overline{d})\oplus k
.

•

A single-tuple
deletion
(over
\mathcal{K}
and
\Voc
) is an expression

u=\texttt{delete}(R,\overline{d})
with
R\in\Voc
and
\overline{d}
a
tuple of arity
\arity(R)
. When applied to a database
db
it induces the database
\textit{db}+u
that is identical
to
db
, but
R^{\textit{db}+u}(\overline{d})=\mymathbb{0}
.

Notice that if every element in
\mathcal{K}
has an additive inverse (i.e.,
\mathcal{K}
is a ring), one can simulate a deletion with an insertion. However, if this is not the case (e.g.,
\mathbb{B}
or
\mathbb{N}
), then a single-tuple deletion is a necessary operation.

As the reader may have noticed, updates allow to modify the contents of relations, but not of constant symbols. This is because an update to a constant translates as an update to the dimension value of a matrix within the equivalent linear algebra setting. And updates in the linear algebra setting affect only entry values, not dimensions. An interesting line of future work is to consider dimension updates.

Dynamic enumeration problems

A
dynamic enumeration problem
is an enumeration problem
P
together with a set
U
of
updates
on
P
’s inputs: a set of operations such that applying an update
u\in U
to an input
I
of
P
yields a new input to
P
, denoted
I+u
. An algorithm
A

solves
a dynamic enumeration problem
D=(P,U)
if it solves the enumeration problem
P
(computing, for each input
I
a data structure in a preprocessing phase from which
P(I)
may be enumerated) and, moreover, it is possible, for every input
I
and update
u
to update the data structure that
A
computed on
I
during the update phase to a data structure for
I+u
. By definition, the latter updated data structure hence allows to enumerate
P(I+u)
. The preprocessing time and enumeration delay of
A
are defined as for normal (static) enumeration problems. The
update time
of
A
is an upper bound on the time needed to update the data structure for
I
into one for
I+u
.

In this document, we will consider two versions of the dynamic query evaluation problem for conjunctive query
Q
over
\Voc
under semiring
\mathcal{K}
:

•

The
unbounded
version,
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
which is the dynamic enumeration problem
(\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K}),U)
with
U
the set of all single-tuple insertions and deletions over
\mathcal{K}
and
\Voc
.

•

The
bounded
version, denoted
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
, where
M\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
is a constant positive natural number, and which is defined as follows.

Define
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
to be the bounded version of the
static
query evaluation problem: this is the collection of all pairs
(I,O)
with

I=\textit{db}
with
db
a
\mathcal{K}
-database over
\Voc

such that any data value
d
occurring in
db
is at most
M
, i.e.,
d\leq M
7
7

7

Recall that the set of data values that may appear in database tuples equals
\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
.
, and
O=\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
. Hence,
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
is the evaluation problem of
Q
on
\mathcal{K}
-databases whose values in the active domain are bounded by
M
.

Then the bounded dynamic evaluation problem
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
is the dynamic enumeration problem
(\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M),U)
with
U
the set of all single-tuple updates
\texttt{insert}(R,\overline{d},k)
or
\texttt{delete}(R,\overline{d})
where every value
d\in\overline{d}
satisfies
d\leq M
.

Clearly, one can check that
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})=\bigcup_{M=1}^{\infty}\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
.

In an analogous manner, in the matrix setting for a query
\mathbf{Q}
over
\Sch
we define the dynamic query evaluation problem
\texttt{DynEval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
and
\texttt{DynEval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K},M)
of
\texttt{Eval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
and
\texttt{Eval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K},M)
, respectively. The allowed updates in these problems are of the form
\texttt{insert}(\mathbf{A},i,j,k)
, which sets
\mathbf{A}_{i,j}:=\mathbf{A}_{i,j}+k
, and
\texttt{delete}(\mathbf{A},i,j)
which sets
\mathbf{A}_{i,j}:=\mymathbb{0}
.

Dynamic complexity classes

For functions
f,g
, and
h
from the natural numbers to the positive reals we define
\texttt{DynEnum}(f,g,h)
to be the class of dynamic enumeration problems
(P,U)
for which there exists an enumeration algorithm
A
such that for every input
I
it holds that the preprocessing time is
\bigo\left(f(\parallel\!I\!\parallel)\right)
, the delay is
\bigo\left(g(\parallel\!I\!\parallel)\right)
, where
\parallel\!I\!\parallel
denotes the size of
I
. Moreover, processing update
u
on current input
I
takes time
\bigo(\left(h(\parallel\!I\!\parallel,\parallel\!u\!\parallel)\right)
. Note that, since the inputs in a dynamic evaluation problem
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
or
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
consists only of the database and not the query, we hence measure complexity in
data complexity
as is standard in the literature
[
BDG07
,
IUV+20
,
BGS20
,
BKS17
]
.
In what follows we set the size
\parallel\!u\!\parallel
of a single-tuple update
u
to
1
: each update is of constant size, given the fixed vocabulary
\Voc
.

Note that, since
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})=\bigcup_{M=1}^{\infty}\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
if
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
then also
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
, for every
M
. Conversely, if
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)\not\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
for some
M
, then also
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\not\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
. Specifically, we will use the bounded evaluation problem
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
to obtain our lower bounds in the relational setting. Analogously, we focus on the bounded evaluation problem
\texttt{DynEval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K},M)
to obtain our lower bounds in the matrix setting.

Complexity hypothesis and known results.

Like the authors of
[
HKNS15
,
BKS17
]
we use the following hypothesis concerning the hardness of dynamic problems as hypothesis for obtaining conditional lower bounds:

•

The
Online Boolean Matrix-Vector Multiplication (OMv) problem
:
given an
n\times n
boolean matrix
A
, compute
A\cdot v_{1},\ldots,A\cdot v_{n}
sequentally
for
n\times 1
vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
. It is required that the result of
A\cdot v_{i-1}
must already be computed in order to access
v_{i}
.
The
OMv conjecture
states that this problem cannot be solved in time
\bigo(n^{3-\varepsilon})
for any
\varepsilon>0
.

{thmC}

[
[
BKS17
]
]
Let
Q
be a
\text{CQ}^{\not\leq}
over
\Voc
.

•

If
Q
is q-hierarchical, then
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
.

•

If
Q
is a query without self joins and

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
for every
M\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
,
then
Q
is q-hierarchical unless the
OMv
conjecture is false.

11.2.
Upper bounds for q-hierarchical queries

Similar than for free-connex queries, we can provide dynamic evaluation algorithms for qh-CQ and
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
queries. However, for this dynamic setting, we require some additional algorithmic assumptions over the semiring.
Let
\mathcal{K}=(K,\oplus,\odot,\mymathbb{0},\mymathbb{1})
be a semiring and
M
be the set of all multisets of
K
.
For any
k\in K
and
m\in M
, define
\texttt{ins}(k,m)
and
\texttt{del}(k,m)
to be the multisets resulting from inserting or deleting
k
from
m
, respectively.
Then we say that
\mathcal{K}
is
sum-maintainable
if there exists a data structure
\mathcal{D}
to represent multisets of
K
such that the empty set
\emptyset
can be built in constant time, and if
\mathcal{D}
represents
m\in M
then:
(1) the value
\bigoplus_{k\in m}k
can always be computed from
\mathcal{D}
in constant time;
(2) a data structure that represents
\texttt{ins}(k,m)

can be obtained from
\mathcal{D}
in constant time; and
(3) a data structure that represents
\texttt{del}(k,m)

can be obtained from
\mathcal{D}
in constant time.
One can easily notice that if each element of
\mathcal{K}
has an additive inverse (i.e.,
\mathcal{K}
is a ring), then
\mathcal{K}
is sum-maintainable, like
\mathbb{R}
. Other examples of sum-maintainable semirings (without additive inverses) are
\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{N}
.

The main result of this subsection is the following.

Theorem 58
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a sum-maintainable semi-integral domain.
For every q-hierarchical CQ
Q
,
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
can be evaluated
dynamically with constant-time update and constant-delay. In particular,

\texttt{DynEval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
can also be evaluated dynamically with constant-time
update and constant-delay for every
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}

\mathbf{Q}
over
\Sch
.

We now prove Theorem
58
in two steps. We first transfer the upper bound of
\text{CQ}^{\not\leq}
queries over
\mathbb{B}
-databases to
\text{CQ}^{\not\leq}
queries over
\mathcal{K}
-databases. We then generalize the latter to CQ queries over
\mathcal{K}
-databases.

Proposition 59
.

Let
Q
be a q-hierarchical
\text{CQ}^{\not\leq}
over
\Voc
and
\mathcal{K}
a sum-maintainable semi-integral domain.
Then
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
.

Proof 11.1
.

Note that since
Q
is q-hierarchical it has a guarded query plan
(T,N)
by
Proposition
30
. This implies in
particular that
Q
is also free-connex. By
Proposition
49
we hence know
that
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
. As such, there is an algorithm
A

that, given
db
builds a data structure in linear time from which

\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
can be enumerated with constant delay. We will show that
for any update
u
to
db
this data structure can also be updated in

\bigo(1)
into a data structure for
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db}+u)
. Because, in the
proof of Proposition
49
the
algorithm (and data structure) depends on the shape of
Q
and
(T,N)
we make
a corresponding case analysis here.

(1)

If
Q
is a full-join, i.e., no variable is quantified, we reduce to the Boolean case as follows. Assume that
Q=H(\overline{z})\leftarrow R_{1}(\overline{z}_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge R_{n}(\overline{z}_{n})
. In
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
time construct
\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})
, the
\mathbb{B}
-database obtained from
db
defined as:

R^{\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})}\colon\overline{d}\mapsto\begin{cases}\texttt{t}\text{ if }R^{\textit{db}}(\overline{d})\neq\mymathbb{0}\\
\texttt{f}\text{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

for every
R\in\Voc
. Furthermore, preprocess
db
by creating lookup tables such that for every relation
R
and tuple
\overline{a}
of the correct arity we can retrieve the annotation
R^{\textit{db}}(\overline{a})
in time
\bigo(|\overline{a}|)
time. Note that since
\Voc
is fixed,
|\overline{a}|
is constant. This retrieval is hence
\bigo(1)
in data complexity. It is well-known that such lookup tables can be created in
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
time in the RAM model.

Finally, invoke the algorithm for
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})
on
\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})
. By Theorem
11.1
, this algorithm has
\bigo(\parallel\!\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})\!\parallel)=\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
preprocessing time, after which we may enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db}))
with
\bigo(1)
delay and maintain this property under updates to
\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})
in
\bigo(1)
time. We have shown in Proposition
49
that using the enumeration procedure for
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db}))
and the lookup tables, we may also enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
with constant delay. We do not repeat this argument here.

Given a single-tuple update
u
we update the lookup tables and the data structure maintained by
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})
as follows.

•

If
u=\texttt{insert}(R,\overline{d},k)
then lookup
\overline{d}
in the lookup table for
R
and retrieve its old annotation
\ell
(if the lookup table does not contain
\ell
, set
\ell=\mymathbb{0}
). There are two cases to consider:

–

If
k+\ell=\mymathbb{0}
then the tuple
\overline{d}
is now deleted from
R^{\textit{db}+u}
and we similarly remove it from
\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db}+u)
by issuing the update
u^{\prime}=\texttt{delete}(R,\overline{d})
to
\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})
(and
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})
).

–

If
k+\ell\not=\mymathbb{0}
then the tuple is certainly present in
R^{\textit{db}+u}
inserted and similarly make sure it is in
\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db}+u)
by issuing
u^{\prime}=\texttt{insert}(R,\overline{d},\texttt{t})
to
\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})
(and
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})
). Note that if the tuple was already in
\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})
then
u^{\prime}
has no-effect since we are working in the boolean semiring there.

•

If
u=\texttt{delete}(R,\overline{d})
then delete
\overline{d}
from the lookup table and issue the update
u^{\prime}=\texttt{delete}(R,\overline{d})
to
\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db})
and
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B})
.

Note that in all cases, after issuing
u^{\prime}
we obtain
\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db}+u)
as desired, so that after this update using the enumeration procedure for
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\texttt{Bool}(\textit{db}+u))
and the lookup tables, we will enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db}+u)
with constant delay.

(2)

If the guarded query plan
(T,N)
is such that
N
consists of a single node
N=\{r\}
, then by Lemma
31
we may assume without loss of generality that for every node
n
with two children
c_{1},c_{2}
in
T
we have
\var(n)=\var(c_{1})=\var(c_{2})
. Intuitively, the algorithm will work as follows. We show that for any database
db
we may compute
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
in
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
