time. Because we can simply store this result, we may certainly enumerate it with constant delay. Then, by also storing the subresults computed during the computation of
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
we show that we can also maintain
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
and all subresults in
\bigo(1)
time under updates.

Formally the data structure used by
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
will be the query result itself, plus some extra lookup tables. In particular, given input database
db
we:

•

compute, for every node
n\in T
the
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n],\textit{db})
. We have shown in Proposition
49
that we may compute
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n],\textit{db})
in linear time, for every node
n
. We do not repeat the argument here. Since
Q
(and hence
T
) is fixed there are a constant number of nodes in
T
. So this step takes linear time overall.

•

convene that we store
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n],\textit{db})
for every node
n
by creating a lookup table such that for any tuple
(\overline{a},k)\in\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n],\textit{db})
we can retrieve
k
in
\bigo(1)
time given
\overline{a}
. It is well-known that the lookup tables can be made such that may we enumerate the entries
(\overline{a},k)
of this lookup table with constant delay. It is well-known that such lookup tables can be created in time linear in
\parallel\!\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n],\textit{db})\!\parallel=\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
. So also this step takes linear time.

Note that by definition
Q\equiv\varphi[T,r]
; therefore the root represents
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
as desired, and can be used to enumerate the result with constant delay.

It remains to show that given an update
u
to
db
we can update
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n]{\textit{db}},
) into
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n]{\textit{db}+u},
) in
\bigo(1)
time, for every node
n
. In order to be able to show this, we store one auxiliary lookup table
L_{n}
for all nodes
n
that have a single child
c
. This auxiliary table is defined as follows. By Lemma
15
if
n
has a single child
c
then
\varphi[T,n]\equiv\exists\overline{y}.\varphi[T,c]
with
\overline{y}=\var(c)\setminus\var(n)
. The lookup table
L_{n}
stores, for each
(\overline{d},k)
in
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n],\textit{db})
a pointer to a data structure
\mathcal{D}_{\overline{d}}
that represents the multiset

\{\!\{k\mid(\overline{a},k)\in\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T_{c},c],\textit{db}),{\overline{a}}|_{\var(r)}=\overline{d}\}\!\}.

Because
\mathcal{K}
is
sum-maintainable, and because
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T_{c},c],\textit{db})
is linear in
db
, this extra lookup table can be computed in linear time.
8
8

8

Initially, the lookup table is empty. Loop over the entries
(\overline{a},k)
of
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T_{c},c],\textit{db})
one by one. For each such tuple, compute
\overline{d}={\overline{a}}|_{\var(r)}
and lookup
\overline{d}
in
L
. If it is present with data structure
\mathcal{D}_{\overline{d}}
then apply
\texttt{ins}(k,\mathcal{D}_{\overline{d}})
, hence updating
\mathcal{D}_{\overline{d}}
. If it is not present, initialize
\mathcal{D}_{\overline{d}}
to empty; add
k
to it, and insert
(\overline{d},\mathcal{D}_{\overline{d}})
to the lookup table.

We now verify that for every single-tuple update
u
to
db
and every node
n
we can update
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n]{\textit{db}},
) into
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n]{\textit{db}+u},
) in
\bigo(1)
time. The proof is by induction on the height of
n
in
T
. (I.e., the length of longest path
from
n
to a leaf.)

•

When
n
is a leaf, it is an atom, say
R(\overline{x})
. Upon update
u
, if
u
is of the form
\texttt{insert}(R,\overline{a},k)
or
\texttt{delete}(R,\overline{a})
then we update the lookup table that represents
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n]{\textit{db}},
) in the obvious way. Such updates take
\bigo(1)
in the RAM model. If
u
pertains to a relation other then
R
, we simply ignore it.

•

When
n
is an interior node with a single child
c
then by Lemma
15
we have
\varphi[T,n]\equiv\exists\overline{y}.\varphi[T,c]
with
\overline{y}=\var(c)\setminus\var(r)
. When we receive update
u
to
db
, by inductive hypothesis this yields a bounded number of single-tuple updates
u_{c}
to
A=\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,c],\textit{db})
which we may compute in
\bigo(1)
time. We translate these updates
u_{c}
into updates
u^{\prime}
to apply to
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n],\textit{db})
:

–

If
u_{c}=\texttt{insert}(\overline{a},k)
. Then apply
u_{c}
to
A
and update the auxiliary lookup table
L_{n}
by applying
\texttt{ins}(k,\mathcal{D}_{\overline{d}})
with
\overline{d}={\overline{a}}|_{\var(r)}
. Furthermore, update
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n],\textit{db})
by applying
u^{\prime}=\texttt{insert}(\overline{d},k)
to the lookup table representing it.

–

If
u_{c}=\texttt{delete}(\overline{a})
. Before applying
u_{c}
to
A=\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,c],\textit{db})
, look up the old annotation
k
of
\overline{a}
in
A
. Then apply
u_{c}
to
A
and update
L_{n}
by applying
\texttt{del}(k,\mathcal{D}_{\overline{d}})
with
\overline{d}={\overline{a}}|_{\var(r)}
. If
\texttt{query}(\mathcal{D}_{\overline{d}})=\mymathbb{0}
, then
u
effectively causes
\overline{d}
to be removed from
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n],\textit{db})
. Hence, the update
u^{\prime}
to apply to (the lookup table representing)
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n],\textit{db})
is
u^{\prime}=\texttt{delete}(\overline{d})
. Otherwise,
\texttt{query}(\mathcal{D}_{\overline{d}})\not=\mymathbb{0}
and the update
u^{\prime}
to apply is
u^{\prime}=\texttt{insert}(\overline{d},k)
.

•

Node
n
has two children
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
. By Lemma
16
we have
\varphi[T,n]\equiv\varphi[T,c_{1}]\wedge\varphi[T,c_{2}]
. Note that this denotes an intersection since
\var(n)=\var(c_{1})=\var(c_{2})
. When we receive update
u
to
db
, by inductive hypothesis this yields a bounded number of single-tuple updates to
A_{1}=\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,c_{1}],\textit{db})
and
A_{2}=\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,c_{1}],\textit{db})
which we may compute in
\bigo(1)
time. We translate these updates to
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
into updates to
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n]{\textit{db}},
) as follows. We only show the reasoning for updates to
A_{1}
. The reasoning for updates to
A_{2}
is similar. Let
u_{1}
be update to
A_{1}
. We translate this into update
u^{\prime}
to apply to
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n],\textit{db})
as follows:

–

if
u_{1}=\texttt{insert}(\overline{d},k_{1})
then use the lookup table on
A_{2}
to find the annotation
\ell
of
\overline{d}
in
A_{2}
. (If
\overline{d}
does not appear in
A_{2}
then take
\ell=\mymathbb{0}
). If
k_{1}\odot\ell\not=\mymathbb{0}
then apply
u^{\prime}=\texttt{insert}(d,k_{1}\odot\ell)
to
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n],\textit{db})
; otherwise there is no update
u^{\prime}
to apply.

–

if
u_{1}=\texttt{delete}(\overline{d})
then apply
u^{\prime}=\texttt{delete}(d)
to
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,n],\textit{db})
.

(3)

When
Q
is not full and
N
has more than one node we reduce to the previous two cases because
\varphi[T,N]\equiv\varphi[T_{1},n_{1}]\wedge\dots\wedge\varphi[T_{l},n_{l}]
where
F=\{n_{1},\ldots,n_{l}\}
is the frontier of
N
, i.e, the nodes without children in
N
, and
T_{1},\dots,T_{l}
are the subtrees of
T
rooted at
n_{1},\dots,n_{l}
respectively. Hence, we may enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,N],\textit{db})
with constant delay by first fully computing the full join
\varphi[T_{1},n_{1}]\wedge\dots\wedge\varphi[T_{l},n_{l}]
on these subresults. By item (2) above, each
A_{i}:=\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T_{i},n_{i}],\textit{db})
can be computed in linear time from
db
. Furthermore, each
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T_{i},n_{i}],\textit{db})
can be maintained under updates in
\bigo(1)
time by item (2) above. By item (1) the result of the final full join can be enumerated with constant delay after linear time processing on
A_{i}
. In addition, we can maintain this property under updates in
\bigo(1)
time by item (1) above, since this is a full join and
\varphi[T,N]
is q-hierarchical. Therefore, the entire result
\texttt{Answer}(\varphi[T,N],\textit{db})
can be enumerated with constant delay after linear time processing, and be maintained under updates in
\bigo(1)
time.

Next, we show how to extend Proposition
59
when queries have inequalities.
Towards this goal, note that the conditions for a CQ to be q-hierarchical are imposed over relational atoms. Hence, the following result is straightforward (recall the definition of
\texttt{split}(Q)
given in Section
10
).

Corollary 60
.

Let
Q
be a CQ and
\texttt{split}(Q)=(Q_{\text{rel}},Q_{\text{ineq}})
.
Then
Q
is q-hierarchical if and only if
Q_{\text{rel}}
is q-hierarchical.

Then, we can exploit the split of
Q
between
Q_{\text{rel}}
and
Q_{\text{ineq}}
to evaluate efficiently any
q
-hierarchical query
Q
as follows.

Proposition 61
.

Let
Q
be a CQ over
\Voc
and
\texttt{split}(Q)=(Q_{\text{rel}},Q_{\text{ineq}})
and
\mathcal{K}
a
semi-integral domain. If
\texttt{DynEval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
then

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
.

Proof 11.2
.

Let
\Voc
be the vocabulary of
Q
and
db
be an arbitrary
\mathcal{K}
-database over
\Voc
. Since
\texttt{DynEval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
we have
\texttt{Eval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
and consequently
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
, by Proposition
52
.

Note that Proposition
52
shows that
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
by first making input database
db
atomically consistent with
Q
and then invoking
\texttt{Eval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathcal{K})
on the atomically reduced database
\textit{db}^{\prime}
. It is then possible to enumerate
\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db}^{\prime})=\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db})
with constant delay, by enumerating
\texttt{Vals}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db}^{\prime})=\texttt{Vals}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db})
with constant delay. In other words, the data structure of
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
is simply that of
\texttt{Eval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathcal{K})
.

Hence, to obtain the proposition, it suffices to translate a given update
u
to
db
into an update
u^{\prime}
to
\textit{db}^{\prime}
such that
\textit{db}^{\prime}+u^{\prime}
is the atomic reduction of
\textit{db}+u
. This allows us to update the data structure
\texttt{Eval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathcal{K})
in
\bigo(1)
time by assumption, and still be able to enumerate
\texttt{Vals}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db}^{\prime}+u^{\prime})=\texttt{Vals}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db}+u)
. We translate the update as follows

•

If
u=\texttt{insert}(R,\overline{d},k)
then for each atom
R(\overline{x})
in
Q
with
\overline{d}\models\overline{x}
check that the inequality constraints imposed by
Q[R(\overline{x})]
are satisfied. If this is the case, apply
u^{\prime}=u
to
\textit{db}^{\prime}
. Otherwise, no update needs to be done to
\textit{db}^{\prime}
.

•

If
u=\texttt{delete}(R,\overline{d})
then apply
u^{\prime}=u
to
\textit{db}^{\prime}
.

We finally have all the machinery to prove Theorem
58
.

Proof 11.3

(Proof of Theorem
58
)
.

Let
\texttt{split}(Q)=(Q_{\text{rel}},Q_{\text{ineq}})
. Since
Q
is q-hierarchical, we have that
Q_{\text{rel}}
is q-hierarchical by Corollary
60
. Then, by Proposition
59
,
\texttt{DynEval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
since
\mathcal{K}
is a sum-maintainable semi-integral domain. Hence we get that

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
because of Proposition
61
.

We proceed to prove the second part of the result, i.e., achieve the same processing complexity for the matrix evaluation problem
\texttt{DynEval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
. Let
\mathbf{Q}
be a
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query over
\Sch
and
\mathcal{I}
and instance over
\Sch
. Fix a matrix-to-relational schema encoding
\Rel
over
\Sch
such that the head atom of
Q
is binary. Since in particular
\mathbf{Q}
is also a
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query, by Theorem
48

\llbracket\mathbf{Q}\rrbracket(\mathcal{I})
can be enumerated with constant delay after a preprocessing that runs in time
\bigo(\parallel\!\mathcal{I}\!\parallel)
.
Now, use Theorem
37
to compute the q-hierarchical relational query
Q
over
\Voc
that is equivalent to
\mathbf{Q}
under
\Rel
. This is in constant time in data complexity. Next, let
u
be a update
u
to
\mathcal{I}
. We define a series of single tuple updates
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}
to
\Rel(\mathcal{I})
such that
\Rel(\mathcal{I})+u_{1}+\ldots+u_{n}=\Rel(\mathcal{I})+u=\Rel(\mathcal{I}_{u})
. This can be done in
\bigo(\parallel\!u\!\parallel)
time.
Because
Q
is q-hierarchical,
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
can be evaluated dynamically with constant-time update and constant-delay, and hence we can enumerate each
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\Rel(\mathcal{I}))
,
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\Rel(\mathcal{I})+u_{1})
up to
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\Rel(\mathcal{I})+u_{1}+\ldots+u_{n})
with constant delay. This holds because processing each update
u_{i}
one at a time takes time
\bigo(1)
. After
\bigo(\parallel\!u\!\parallel)
time, the former yields constant delay enumeration for
\texttt{Answer}(\mathbf{Q},\mathcal{I}_{u})
.

11.3.
Lower bounds for q-hierarchical queries

Similar than for free-connex CQ, we can extend the lower bound in
[
BKS17
]

when the subsemiring generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is zero-sum free,
by assuming the Online Boolean Matrix-Vector Multiplication (OMv) conjecture
[
HKNS15
]
. For this, recall the definition of constant-disjoint introduced in Section
10
.

Theorem 62
.

Let
Q
be a CQ over
\Voc
without self-joins and constant-disjoint. Let

\mathcal{K}
be a semiring such that the subsemiring generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and

\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is zero-sum free.
If
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})
can be evaluated dynamically with constant-time update and constant-delay, then
Q
is q-hierarchical, unless the OMv conjecture is false.

To prove Theorem
62
it suffices to show that the statement holds in the bounded problem
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)
, for every
M\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
. Towards that goal, we first reduce the
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)
problem into the
\texttt{DynEval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)
problem assuming that
Q
is constant-disjoint. Subsequently, we reduce
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
to
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)
.

Proposition 63
.

Let
M\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
and let
Q
be a CQ over
\Voc
with

\texttt{split}(Q)=(Q_{\text{rel}},Q_{\text{ineq}})
. If
Q
is constant-disjoint and

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
then

\texttt{DynEval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\allowbreak\mathbb{B},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
.

Proof 11.4
.

The proof uses the same ideas as the proof of
Proposition
55
, but specialized to the dynamic setting, and utilizing that
M
is a bound on the active domain of the databases that we will evaluate
Q_{\text{rel}}
on.

Specifically, we reduce
\texttt{DynEval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc_{\text{rel}},\mathbb{B},M)
to

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)
. Denote the free variables of
Q
,
Q_{\text{rel}}
and

Q_{\text{ineq}}
by
\overline{x}
,
\overline{x}_{1}
and
\overline{x}_{2}
, respectively. Note that
\overline{x}_{1}
and
\overline{x}_{2}

are disjoint and
\var(\overline{x})=\var(\overline{x}_{1})\cup\var(\overline{x}_{2})
. Consider an
arbitrary database
\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}
over
\Voc
, input to

\texttt{DynEval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)
. In particular, all values in the active domain of
db
are bounded by
M
. Construct the database
db
, input to

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)
as follows.

•

R^{\textit{db}}=R^{\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}}
for each relation symbol
R\in\Voc
;

•

db(c):=M
for all constant symbols
c\in\Voc
that occur in a
covered inequality in
Q
. Note that
c\not=1
because
Q
is
constant-disjoint;

•

db(c):=1
for all other constant symbols
c\in\Voc
.

This is well-defined because
Q
is constant-disjoint. The computation of

db
is clearly in
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}\!\parallel)
and
\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel=\parallel\!\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}\!\parallel
.
We call
db
the
extension
of
\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}
.

Because
M
is an upper bound on the domain values occurring in
\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}
we have shown in Proposition
55
that
\texttt{Answer}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db}_{\text{rel}})=\texttt{Answer}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db})
and, that moreover, we may enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q_{\text{rel}},\textit{db})
with constant delay using the constant-delay enumeration for
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db})
, which exists because
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
.

Hence, to obtain the proposition, it suffices to show that we can translate
any given update
u
to
\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}
into an update
u^{\prime}
to
db
such that

\textit{db}+u^{\prime}
is the extension of
\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}+u
. Because

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
this allows us to update the
data structure of
\texttt{Eval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)
in
\bigo(1)
, and still be
able to enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db}+u^{\prime})
, which yields the enumeration of

\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}+u)
with constant delay. We reason as follows. Let
u
be
a single-tuple update to
\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}
such that all data values occurring in
u

are bounded by
M
. Then simply apply
u
to
db
. Clearly,
\textit{db}+u
is the
extension of
\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}+u
.

Proposition 64
.

Let
Q
be a CQ over
\Voc
and
\mathcal{K}
a semiring such that the subsemiring
generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is non-trivial and zero-sum
free. Let
M\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
. If
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
then

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
.

Proof 11.5
.

Let
\mathcal{K}=(K,\oplus,\odot,\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}},\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}})
be a a semiring such
that the subsemiring generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is
non-trivial and zero-sum free. The proof uses the same ideas as the proof
Proposition
56
, but
specialized to the dynamic setting, with bounded evaluation.

Specifically, we reduce
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)
to

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
as follows. Let
\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}
be a
\mathbb{B}
-database
over
\Voc
, input to
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)
. Construct the

\mathcal{K}
-database
\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}
, input to
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
by setting

•

for every relation symbol
R\in\Voc

\llceil{R}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}\colon\overline{d}\mapsto\begin{cases}\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}\text{ if }R^{\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}}(\overline{d})=\texttt{t}\\
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

•

\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}(c):=\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}(c)
for every constant symbol
c
.

We call
\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}
the
\mathcal{K}
version of
\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}

Note that this takes time
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}\!\parallel)
. Then run

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
on
\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}
, which takes linear time. We have
shown in the proof of
Proposition
56
that we may
then enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}})
with constant delay using the
constant-delay enumeration procedure for
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}})
provided by

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
.
By assumption,
\texttt{Eval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1)
thus the set

\texttt{Vals}(Q,\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}})
can be enumerated with constant delay after a linear
time preprocessing.

Hence, to obtain the proposition, it suffices to show that we can translate
any given update
u
to
\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}
into an update
u^{\prime}
to
\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}
such
that
\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}+u^{\prime}
yields the
\mathcal{K}
-version of
\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}+u
. Because

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
this allows us to update the
data structure of
\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)
in
\bigo(1)
, and still be
able to enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}+u^{\prime})
, which yields the enumeration of

\texttt{Answer}(Q,\textit{db}_{\text{rel}}+u)
with constant delay.

We reason as follows. Let
u
be a single-tuple update to
\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}
such
that all data values occurring in
u
are bounded by
M
.

•

If
u=\texttt{insert}(R,\overline{d},\texttt{t})
and
\overline{d}
does not occur in
R^{\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}}
(meaning that
\overline{d}
did not occur in
\textit{db}_{\mathbb{B}}
before), then set
u^{\prime}=\texttt{insert}(R,\overline{d},\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}})
.

•

If
u=\texttt{insert}(R,\overline{d},\texttt{f})
then
\textit{db}+u
is the same as
db
, and we hence issue no update
u^{\prime}
to
\textit{db}_{\mathcal{K}}
.

•

If
u=\texttt{delete}(R,\overline{d})
then the update
u^{\prime}=\texttt{delete}(R,\overline{d})
.

As argued, the next theorem clearly implies Theorem
62
.

Theorem 65
.

Let
Q
be a CQ over
\Voc
without self-joins and constant-disjoint and

\mathcal{K}
a semiring such that the subsemiring generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and

\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is non-trivial and zero-sum free.

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
for every
M\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, then
Q

is q-hierarchical, unless the
OMv
conjecture is false.

Proof 11.6
.

Let
\texttt{split}(Q)=(Q_{\text{rel}},Q_{\text{ineq}})
. Since

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
for every
M
, by
Proposition
64
we have that

\texttt{DynEval}(Q,\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
for every
M
, because the
subsemiring generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is non-trivial
zero-sum free.
Proposition
63
states then
that
\texttt{DynEval}(Q_{\text{rel}},\Voc,\mathbb{B},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
for every
M
, since
Q

is constant disjoint. Because
Q_{\text{rel}}\in\text{CQ}^{\not\leq}
, by
Theorem
11.1
,
Q_{\text{rel}}
is q-hierarchical
unless the
OMv
conjecture is false. Given that
Q_{\text{rel}}
is q-hierarchical if
and only if
Q
is q-hierarchical, the former directly implies that
Q
is
q-hierarchical unless the
OMv
conjecture is false.

11.4.
Lower bounds for
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
queries

Similarly to the lower bound in the free-connex case, we show that Theorem
65
transfers to
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
.

Corollary 66
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}
be a
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query over
\Sch
such that
\mathbf{Q}
does not repeat matrix symbols and
\mathbf{Q}
is constant-disjoint. Let
\mathcal{K}
be a semiring such that the subsemiring generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is zero-sum free. If
\texttt{DynEval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
can be evaluated dynamically with constant-time update and constant-delay, then
\mathbf{Q}
is equivalent to a
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query, unless the OMv conjecture is false.

Similarly as in the relational case, we rely on the fact that the following theorem clearly implies Corollary
66
: indeed, if
\texttt{DynEval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\mathcal{I}\!\parallel,1)
then trivially
\texttt{DynEval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K},M)\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\mathcal{I}\!\parallel,1)
for every
M
, in which case the following proposition yields the claim of Corollary
66
.

Theorem 67
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}
be a
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query over
\Sch
such that
\mathbf{Q}
does not repeat matrix symbols and
\mathbf{Q}
is constant-disjoint. Let
\mathcal{K}
be a semiring such that the subsemiring generated by
\mymathbb{0}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mymathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
is non-trivial and zero-sum free. If
\texttt{DynEval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K},M)\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\mathcal{I}\!\parallel,1)
for every
M\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, then
\mathbf{Q}
is equivalent to a
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query, unless the OMv conjecture is false.

Proof 11.7
.

Let
\Rel
be a matrix-to-relational encoding scheme over
\Sch
such that it is enumeration-suited for
\mathbf{Q}
(see Section
10.2
). Let
\Mat=\Rel^{-1}
be its inverse relational-to-matrix encoding.

We apply Proposition
57
on
\mathbf{Q}
to obtain binary CQ
Q
over
\Voc=\Rel(\Sch)
that simulates
\mathbf{Q}
w.r.t.
\Rel
. We know that
Q
is binary, self-join free and constant-disjoint. We next modify
Q
into a query
Q^{\prime}
over
\Voc
such that the following properties hold:

(1)

Q^{\prime}
continues to simulate
\mathbf{Q}
w.r.t.
\Rel
;

(2)

Q^{\prime}
continues to be constant-disjoint;

(3)

\texttt{DynEval}(Q^{\prime},\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
, for every
M\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
.

This suffices to prove the proposition. Indeed, by Theorem
65

Q^{\prime}
is necessarily q-hierarchical unless the OMv conjecture fails. If
Q^{\prime}
is q-hierarhical, then by Corollary
22
there exists a
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
query
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
that simulates
Q^{\prime}
w.r.t
\Mat=\Rel^{-1}
. Consequently,
\mathbf{Q}
and
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
are then equivalent.

Intuitively, the modification of
Q
into
Q^{\prime}
is necessary to obtain property (P3): the fact that
\texttt{DynEval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K},M)\in\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\mathcal{I}\!\parallel,1)
only implies that we may dynamically evaluate
Q
in
\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)

on databases that encode some matrix instance
. To apply Theorem
65
, by contrast we need to show that we can dynamically evaluate
Q
on
arbitrary databases
. The crux will be that we obtain
Q^{\prime}
by adding extra inequalities to
Q
, such that evaluating
Q^{\prime}
on an arbitrary database yields the same result as evaluating it (and
Q
,
\mathbf{Q}
) on some matrix instance.

The definition of
Q^{\prime}
is as follows. Assume
Q
is of the form
Q:H(\overline{y})\leftarrow\exists\overline{z}.\psi
with
\psi
quantifier free. For every relational atom
\alpha=R(\overline{x})
of
\psi
we define a formula
\varphi_{\alpha}
as follows. This formula is a conjunction of inequalities:

•

If
\alpha=R(x_{1},x_{2})
with
R
binary and
\Mat(R)\colon(\beta,\gamma)
then
\varphi_{\alpha}:=x_{1}\leq\Rel(\beta)\wedge x_{2}\leq\Rel(\gamma)
. Note that, by definition of
\Rel
,
\Rel(\alpha)\not=1\not=\Rel(\beta)
.

•

If
\alpha=R(x_{1})
with
R
unary then by definition of
\Rel
either
\Mat(R)\colon(\beta,1)
or
\Mat(R)\colon(1,\beta)
for some size symbol
\beta\not=1
. Then take
\varphi_{\alpha}:=x_{1}\leq\Rel(\beta)
. Note that by definition of matrix-to-relational encoding schemes,
\Rel(\beta)\not=1
since
\beta\not=1
.

•

If
\alpha=R()
with
R
nullary then by definition of
\Rel
we have
\Mat(R)\colon(1,1)
and we take
\varphi_{\alpha}
the empty formula (which is equivalent to
t
).

Then we define
Q^{\prime}
to be the CQ

Q^{\prime}\colon H(\overline{y})\leftarrow\exists z.(\psi\wedge\bigwedge_{\alpha\text{ rel. atom in }\psi}\varphi_{\alpha}).

Note that
Q^{\prime}
continues to simulate
\mathbf{Q}
over
\Rel
(property P1). Indeed, for any matrix instance
\mathcal{I}
over
\Sch
we know that
\texttt{Answer}(\mathbf{Q},\mathcal{I})=\texttt{Answer}(Q,\Rel(\mathcal{I}))
. By definition,
\Rel(\mathcal{I})
is consistent with
\Rel^{-1}=\Mat
, and therefore the tuples in
\Rel(\mathcal{I})
vacuously satisfy all the extra inequalities that we have dded to
Q
to obtain
Q^{\prime}
. As such,
\texttt{Answer}(Q,\Rel(\mathcal{I}))=\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\Rel(\mathcal{I}))
. Thus,
Q^{\prime}
continues to simulate
\mathbf{Q}
.

Also note that
Q^{\prime}
is constant-disjoint (property P2). Indeed:

•

We have only added covered inequalities to
Q
to obtain
Q^{\prime}
. Any such covered inequality that we have added was of the form
x\leq c
with
c\not=1
. Therefore, since
Q
does not have covered inequalities of the form
x\leq 1
, neither does
Q^{\prime}
.

•

We did not add any uncovered inequalities to
Q
to obtain
Q^{\prime}
. Moreover,
Q^{\prime}
has the same free variables as
Q
. Therefore, all uncovered inequalities
y\leq c
in
Q^{\prime}
continue to be such that
y
is not free in
Q^{\prime}
, unless
c=1
. Hence for all pairs
(x\leq c,y\leq d)
of covered inequality
x\leq c
and non-covered inequality
y\leq d
with
d\neq 1
in
Q^{\prime}
we have that
y
is not free in
Q^{\prime}
as required for constant-disjointness.

Finally, we show that
\texttt{DynEval}(Q^{\prime},\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)\in\texttt{DynEnum}(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel,1,1)
for every
M\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
(property P3), as claimed. Fix
M\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
arbitrarily. We reduce
\texttt{DynEval}(Q^{\prime},\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
to
\texttt{DynEval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
(which is an
unbounded
evaluation problem) as follows. Let
db
be a
\mathcal{K}
-database over
\Voc
, input to
\texttt{DynEval}(Q^{\prime},\Voc,\mathcal{K},M)
. First, create the database
\textit{db}^{\prime}
that is equal to
db
except that for every relation symbol
R\in\Voc
that is not mentioned in
Q
we set
R^{\textit{db}^{\prime}}
to empty. This can clearly be done in linear time. Note that, if
R
is not mentioned in
Q
, then it is also not mentioned in
Q^{\prime}
, and hence of
Q^{\prime}
on
db
is independent of the contents of
R^{\textit{db}}
. Therefore,
\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db})=\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db}^{\prime})
. Subsequently, compute
\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}
, the atomic reduction of
\textit{db}^{\prime}
w.r.t.
Q
. Computing
\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}
can be done in time
\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}^{\prime}\!\parallel)=\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
. By Claim
10.1
we have
\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db})=\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db}^{\prime})=\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db}^{\prime\prime})
. Now verify the following claim:
\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}
is consistent w.r.t.
\Mat=\Rel^{-1}
. This is because we have added, for each matrix symbol
\mathbf{A}
with
\Rel(\mathbf{A})
occurring in
Q
, the inequalities that are required by consistency as atomic inequalities to
Q^{\prime}
. (The matrix symbols
\mathbf{A}
with
\Rel(\mathbf{A})
not occurring in
Q
are empty in
\textit{db}^{\prime}
and hence vacuously consistent w.r.t.
\Mat
.) Since
\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}
is consistent with
\Mat
we have that
\Mat(\textit{db}^{\prime\prime})
is a matrix instance over
\Sch
and hence a valid input to
\mathbf{Q}
. Then, because
Q^{\prime}
simulates
\mathbf{Q}
, we have
\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db})=\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db}^{\prime\prime})=\texttt{Answer}(\mathbf{Q},\Mat(\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}))
. Because
\texttt{DynEval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
is in
\texttt{Enum}(\parallel\!\mathcal{I}\!\parallel,1)
it hence follows that with additional preprocessing of time
\bigo(\parallel\!\Mat(\textit{db}^{\prime\prime})\!\parallel)=\bigo(\parallel\!\textit{db}\!\parallel)
we may enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db}^{\prime\prime})=\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db})
with constant delay, as desired.

In essence, therefore, the data structure needed to enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db})
with constant delay is the data structure computed by
\texttt{DynEval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
when run on
\Mat(\textit{db}^{\prime\prime})
. We next show how to maintain this data structure under updates
u
to
db
.

Specifically, we next show how we may translate in
\bigo(1)
time an update
u
to
db
into an update
u^{\prime\prime}
to
\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}
and an update
u_{M}
to
\Mat(\textit{db}^{\prime\prime})
so that (1)
\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime\prime}
yields the same database as when we would do the above procedure starting from
\textit{db}+u
instead of
db
:
(\textit{db}+u)^{\prime\prime}=\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime\prime}
and (2) applying
u_{M}
to
\Mat(\textit{db}^{\prime\prime})
yields
\Mat(\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime\prime})
. Therefore, applying
u_{M}
to
\texttt{DynEval}(\mathbf{Q},\Sch,\mathcal{K})
will give us the same data structure as when it was run on
\Mat(\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime\prime})
. From this we can again enumerate
\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db}^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime\prime})=\texttt{Answer}(Q^{\prime},\textit{db}+u)
with constant delay. This is because the output relation is binary since
\Rel
is enumeration-suited for
\mathbf{Q}
. We reason as follows.

•

If
u=\texttt{insert}(R,\overline{d},k)
with
R
not occurring in
Q^{\prime}
then we ignore
u
, there are no updates
u^{\prime\prime}
and
u_{M}
to be done.

•

If
u=\texttt{insert}(R,\overline{d},k)
with
R
occurring in
Q^{\prime}
then we check whether
\overline{d}
is atomically consistent with
Q^{\prime}[R(\overline{x})]
for every atom
R(\overline{x})
in
Q^{\prime}
. This can be done in constant time, because
Q^{\prime}
(and hence the number of inequalities to check) is fixed. If it is atomically consistent then
u^{\prime}=u
and
u_{M}
inserts
k
in
\Mat(R)
at the coordinates specified by
\overline{d}
.

•

If
u=\texttt{delete}(R,\overline{d},k)
with
R
not occurring in
Q^{\prime}
then we ignore
u
, there are no updates
u^{\prime\prime}
and
u_{M}
to be done.

•

If
u=\texttt{delete}(R,\overline{d},k)
with
R
occurring in
Q^{\prime}
then we check whether
\overline{d}
is atomically consistent with
Q^{\prime}[R(\overline{x})]
for every atom
R(\overline{x})
in
Q^{\prime}
. This can be done in constant time, because
Q^{\prime}
is fixed. If it is atomically consistent then
u^{\prime}=u
and
u_{M}
sets the entry in
\Mat(R)
at the coordinates specified by
\overline{d}
to
\mymathbb{0}
.

12.
Conclusions and future work

In this work, we isolated the subfragments of
\mathsf{conj}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
that admit efficient evaluation in both static and dynamic scenarios. We found these algorithms by making the correspondence between CQ and
\mathsf{MATLANG}
, extending the evaluation algorithms for free-connex and q-hierarchical CQ, and then translating these algorithms to the corresponding subfragments, namely,
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work that characterizes subfragments of linear algebra query languages that admit efficient evaluation. Moreover, this correspondence improves our understanding of its expressibility.

Regarding future work, a relevant direction is to extend
\mathsf{fc}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
and
\mathsf{qh}\text{-}\mathsf{MATLANG}
with disjunction, namely, matrix summation. This direction is still an open problem even for CQ with union
[
CK21
]
. Another natural extension is to add point-wise functions and understand how they affect expressibility and efficient evaluation. Finally, improving the lower bounds to queries without self-join would be interesting, which is also an open problem for CQ
[
BGS20
,
CS23
]
.

Appendix A
Omitted proofs from Section
2

Proof of Proposition
1

Proof A.1
.

Let
\varphi
be a safe
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
formula. First observe the following: every subformula
\psi
of
\varphi
that is itself not an equality atom, is also safe. This is because safety is defined “universally” on
\varphi
.
Fix a database
db
. We prove that
\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}
has finite support
by induction on
\varphi
.

•

Both
R(\overline{x})
and
x\leq c
have finite support by definition.

•

x=y
by itself is not safe; this case hence cannot occur.

•

If
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
, we discern the following cases.

–

Neither
\varphi_{1}
nor
\varphi_{2}
are an equality atom
x=y
. Then
both
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
are safe, and the result follows by
induction hypothesis because an annotation computed by
\varphi
can only
be non-zero if is obtained by multiplying non-zero annotations originating
from
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
, which both have finite support.

–

One of
\varphi_{1}
or
\varphi_{2}
is of the form
x=y
. Assume for the
sake of presentation that
\varphi_{2}=(x=y)
. Then, by definition of
safety, at least one of
x,y
is in
\rr(\varphi_{1})
. In particular, from
the definition of
\rr(\cdot)
it follows that
\varphi_{1}
itself cannot
be an equality atom. Therefore,
\varphi_{1}
is safe and the induction
hypothesis applies to it. We discern two further cases.

*

Both
x,y\in\rr(\varphi_{1})
. Since

\rr(\varphi_{1})\subseteq\free(\varphi_{1})
, it follows that

\free(\varphi_{1})=\free(\varphi)
. The result follows directly from
the induction hypothesis, since it follows that the support of

\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}
is a subset of the support of

\llceil{\varphi_{1}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}
, which is finite by induction hypothesis.

*

Only one of
x,y\in\rr(\varphi_{1})
. Assume w.l.o.g. that

x\in\rr(\varphi_{1})
but
y\not\in\rr(\varphi_{1})
. By induction
hypothesis,
\llceil{\varphi_{1}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}
has finite support. Now consider a
valuation
\nu\colon\free(\varphi)
such that

\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)\not=\mymathbb{0}
and define

\nu_{1}={\nu}|_{\free(\varphi_{1})}
. By definition of the semantics
of
\wedge
we have also
\llceil{\varphi_{1}}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu_{1})\not=\mymathbb{0}
. Now note that
\nu_{1}
completely specifies
\nu
: the only
variable that is potentially in the domain of
\nu
but not in the
domain of
\nu_{1}
is
y
, but
\varphi
requires
x=y
. So, knowing

\nu(x)
, which is given by
\nu_{1}(x)
, we also know
\nu(y)
. We
conclude that if there were an infinite number of distinct

\nu\colon\free(\varphi)
such that

\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)\not=\mymathbb{0}
then there are also an
infinite number of
\nu_{1}\colon\free(\varphi_{1})
such that

\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)\not=\mymathbb{0}
, which cannot happen by
induction hypothesis.

•

If
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
, then neither
\varphi_{1}
nor
\varphi_{2}
can themselves be equality atoms. (If they were it would violate condition (1) of safety.). The result then straightforwardly follows from the fact that
\free(\varphi_{1})=\free(\varphi_{2})=\free(\varphi)
and from the induction hypothesis.

•

If
\varphi=\exists y.\varphi^{\prime}
then
\varphi^{\prime}
itself cannot be an equality atom: if it were it would violate condition (1) of safety. The result then straightforwardly follows from the induction hypothesis.

Appendix B
Omitted proofs from Section
4

Proof of Lemma
3

Proof B.1
.

We prove the lemma by induction on
e
.

•

If
e=\mathbf{A}\colon\left(\alpha,\beta\right)
then fix
\mathbf{x}\colon\left(\alpha,1\right)
and
\mathbf{y}\colon\left(\beta,1\right)
. Clearly,
e\equiv\Sigma\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}.\hskip 4.30554pt\left(\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{y}\right)\times\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{T}
.

•

If
e=\mathbf{w}\colon(\alpha,1)
with
\mathbf{w}
a vector variable, then fix
\mathbf{x}\colon\left(\alpha,1\right)
and
\mathbf{y}\colon\left(1,1\right)
. Clearly,
e\equiv\Sigma\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}.\left(\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{w}\right)\times\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{T}
.

•

If
e=(e_{1})^{T}\colon(\beta,\alpha)
with
e_{1}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
. By inductive hypothesis we can assume that
e_{1}\equiv\Sigma\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}.\,\mathbf{s}\times\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{T}
. Hence,
e\equiv\Sigma\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}.\,\mathbf{s}\times\mathbf{y}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{T}
.

•

If
e=e_{1}\odot e_{2}
with
e_{1}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
and
e_{2}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
, then:

e_{1}\odot e_{2}=\Sigma\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}.\left(\left(\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot e_{1}\cdot\mathbf{y}\right)\times\left(\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot e_{2}\cdot\mathbf{y}\right)\right)\times\left(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{T}\right)

with
\mathbf{x}\colon(\alpha,1)
and
\mathbf{y}\colon(\beta,1)
, and we can reduce
e_{1}\odot e_{2}
to the other cases.

•

If
e=e_{1}\times e_{2}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
. By inductive hypothesis we have

\displaystyle e_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv\Sigma\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}.\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbf{s}\times\mathbf{x}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{y}_{1}^{T}

\displaystyle e_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\Sigma\mathbf{x}_{2},\mathbf{y}_{2},\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{l}.\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbf{t}\times\mathbf{x}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{y}_{2}^{T}.

Note that
\mathbf{x}_{1}\colon(1,1)
and
\mathbf{y}_{1}\colon(1,1)
. Therefore,

e\equiv\Sigma\mathbf{x}_{2},\mathbf{y}_{2},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k},\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{l},\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{1}.\hskip 4.30554pt\left(\mathbf{s}\times\mathbf{t}\right)\times\mathbf{x}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{y}_{2}^{T}.

•

If
e=e_{1}\cdot e_{2}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
. By inductive hypothesis we have

\displaystyle e_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv\Sigma\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}.\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbf{s}\times\mathbf{x}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{y}_{1}^{T}

\displaystyle e_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\Sigma\mathbf{x}_{2},\mathbf{y}_{2},\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{l}.\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbf{t}\times\mathbf{x}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{y}_{2}^{T}.

Note that in this case,
\mathbf{x}_{1}\colon(\alpha,1)
,
\mathbf{y}_{1}\colon(\gamma,1)
,
\mathbf{x}_{2}\colon(\gamma,1)
and
\mathbf{y}_{2}\colon(\beta,1)
. Therefore,

e\equiv\Sigma\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{2},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k},\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{l},\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}.\hskip 4.30554pt\left(\mathbf{s}\times\mathbf{t}\times\mathbf{y}_{1}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{2}\right)\times\mathbf{x}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{y}_{2}^{T}.

•

If
e=\Sigma\mathbf{v}.e_{1}\colon(\alpha,\beta)
. By inductive hypothesis we have

e_{1}\equiv\Sigma\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}.\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbf{s}\times\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{T}.

Therefore,
e\equiv\Sigma\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k},\mathbf{v}.\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbf{s}\times\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}^{T}
.

Appendix C
Omitted proofs from Section
6

Proof of Lemma
26

Proof C.1
.

We first observe:

(1)

If
\free(\varphi)=\{x\}
then essentially
\psi
expresses a renaming
of variable
x
into variable
y
, in the sense that for all
db
and all

\nu\colon\{y\}
we have

\llceil{\psi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)=\llceil{\varphi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(x\mapsto\nu(y))
. In such a
case, it hence suffices to take
\psi^{\prime}:=\varphi[x\leftrightarrow y]
, the

\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula obtained from
\varphi
by simultaneously replacing all
occurrences (free and bound) of
x
in
\varphi
by
y
, and all occurrences
of
y
in
\varphi
by
x
.

(2)

If
\free(\varphi)=\{y\}
then observe that
\psi\equiv\varphi
. In this case it hence suffices to take
\psi^{\prime}:=\varphi
.

It hence remains to show the result when
\free(\varphi)=\{x,y\}
. The proof
for this case is by well-founded induction on the length of
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formulas.

•

When
\varphi
is an atom, it must be an atom of the form
A(\overline{z})
since

\free(\varphi)=\{x,y\}
. Then
\psi
expresses that
x
and
y
must be
bound to the same values in
A
, and
x
dropped from the resulting
valuation. It hence suffices to take
\psi^{\prime}:=\varphi[x\to y]
, which is
the atom obtained from
A(\overline{z})
where all occurrences of
x
are replaced by

y
.

•

The case where
\varphi=\exists z.\varphi^{\prime}
cannot happen, as this
necessarily has only one free variable.

•

When
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
we make a further case
analysis.

–

If
\free(\varphi_{1})=\{x\}
and
\free(\varphi_{2})=\{y\}
then
\psi\equiv\exists x.(\varphi_{1}[x\leftrightarrow y]\wedge\varphi_{2})\equiv\varphi_{1}[x\leftrightarrow y]\wedge\varphi_{2}
where
\varphi_{1}[x\leftrightarrow y]
is the formula obtained by simultaneously replacing all occurrences (free and bound) of
x
by
y
and all occurrences of
y
by
x
. Hence we take
\psi^{\prime}:=\varphi_{1}[x\leftrightarrow y]\wedge\varphi_{2}
.

–

If
\free(\varphi_{1})=\{x,y\}
and
\free(\varphi_{2})=\{x,y\}
then
\psi\equiv(\exists x.\varphi_{1}\wedge x=y)\wedge(\exists x.\varphi_{2}\wedge x=y)
. The formula
\psi^{\prime}
is then obtained by applying the induction hypothesis on
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
, and taking the conjunction of the resulting formulas.

–

If
\free(\varphi_{1})=\{x,y\}
and
\free(\varphi_{2})=\{y\}
then
\psi\equiv(\exists x.\varphi_{1}\wedge x=y)\wedge\varphi_{2}
. The formula
\psi^{\prime}
is obtained by applying the induction hypothesis on
\varphi_{1}
and taking the conjunction of the result with
\varphi_{2}
.

–

If
\free(\varphi_{1})=\{x,y\}
and
\free(\varphi_{2})=\{x\}
then we observe that

\psi\equiv\exists x.\left((\exists y.\varphi_{1}\wedge x=y)\wedge\varphi_{2}\wedge x=y\right).

The latter formula first selects from
\varphi_{1}
all valuations where
x=y
, projects the result on
x
and then multiplies this result with
\varphi_{2}
. By induction hypothesis there exists
\psi^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
equivalent to
\exists y.(\varphi_{1}\wedge x=y)
. Let
\psi^{\prime}_{2}:=\psi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\varphi
. Then
\psi\equiv\exists x.(\psi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge x=y)
. Note that
\psi^{\prime}_{1}
has only one free variable,
x
, and hence we can apply the reasoning of (1) above to obtain our desired formula
\psi
.

–

If
\free(\varphi_{1})=\{x,y\}
and
\free(\varphi_{2})=\emptyset

then
\psi\equiv(\exists x.\varphi_{1}\wedge x=y)\wedge\varphi_{2}
. The formula
\psi^{\prime}
is obtained by applying the induction hypothesis on
\varphi_{1}
and taking the conjunction of the result with
\varphi_{2}
.

–

All other cases are symmetrical variants of those already seen. Note that the cases where
\free(\varphi_{1})\cup\free(\varphi_{2})\not=\{x,y\}
cannot occur.

Proof of Lemma
27

Proof C.2
.

The proof is by induction on
\psi
. We assume w.l.o.g. that the only
variables that occur in
\psi
are
\{x,y\}
. The case where
\psi
is an atom
is immediate, and the case where
\psi
is a conjunction follows immediately
from the induction hypothesis. Hence, assume
\psi
is of the form

\psi=\exists x.\psi_{1}
. (The case
\exists y.\psi_{1}
is analogous.) By
induction hypothesis, there exists
\psi^{\prime}_{1}
equivalent to
\psi_{1}
in which
equality atoms only occur at the top level. If no equality atom occurs in

\psi^{\prime}_{1}
then it clearly suffices to take
\psi^{\prime}=\exists x.\psi^{\prime}_{1}
. Hence, assume that some equality atom occurs in
\psi^{\prime}_{1}
. We may
assume w.l.o.g. that only the atom
x=y
occurs; atoms of the form
x=x
or

y=y
are always valid and hence can be easily be removed. Also, because
x

and
y
are the only variables ever used, there are no other
possibilities. Then,
\psi^{\prime}_{1}
is of the form
\varphi\wedge x=y
with

\varphi
an
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
formula. The result then follows by
Lemma
26
.

Appendix D
Omitted proofs from Section
8

Proof of Lemma
45

Proof D.1
.

The case when
\varphi\in\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
is entirely analogous to the proof of Lemma
26
with fewer cases involved in the conjunction, since only conjunction between formulas with the same free variables is allowed.

Hence we focus when
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\varphi_{k}
with
\varphi_{i}\in\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
for every
i
. Consider the set
F=\{\free(\varphi_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
. Note that
F\not=\emptyset
. Furthermore, because
\phi
is a hierarchical conjunction,
\{\{x,y\},\{x\},\{y\}\}\not\subseteq F
. There are hence
2^{2^{2}}
-3 = 13 possibilities for
F
. We construct
e
by case analysis on
F
. We only illustrate three important cases; the other cases can be easily derived from the ones presented next. We adopt the following notation: for each
S\in F
the formula
\varphi_{S}
denotes the conjunction of all
\varphi_{i}
with
\free(\varphi_{i})=S
.

•

Assume
F=\{\{x\},\{y\},\emptyset\}
. Then
\varphi\equiv\varphi_{x}\wedge\varphi_{y}\wedge\varphi_{\emptyset}
. To express
\exists x.\varphi\wedge x=y
it suffices to take
\psi^{\prime}\coloneqq\varphi_{x}[x\leftrightarrow y]\wedge\varphi_{y}\wedge\varphi_{\emptyset}
, where
\varphi_{y}[x\leftrightarrow y]
is the formula obtained by simultaneously replacing all occurrences (free and bound) of
x
by
y
and all occurrences of
y
by
x
.

•

Assume
F=\{\{x,y\},\{y\},\emptyset\}
. Then
\varphi\equiv\varphi_{x,y}\wedge\varphi_{y}\wedge\varphi_{\emptyset}
. To express
\exists x.\varphi\wedge x=y
it suffices to take
\psi^{\prime}\coloneqq(\exists x.\varphi_{x,y}\wedge x=y)\wedge\varphi_{y}\wedge\varphi_{\emptyset}
, where
\exists x.\varphi_{x,y}\wedge x=y
can be expressed in
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
since
\varphi_{x,y}\in\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
.

•

Assume
F=\{\{x,y\},\{x\},\emptyset\}
. Then
\varphi\equiv\varphi_{x,y}\wedge\varphi_{x}\wedge\varphi_{\emptyset}
. To express
\exists x.\varphi\wedge x=y
it suffices to take
\psi^{\prime}\coloneqq\exists x.\left((\exists y.\varphi_{x,y}\wedge x=y)\wedge\varphi_{x}\wedge\varphi_{\emptyset}\right)
, where the latter formula first selects from
\varphi_{x,y}
all valuations where
x=y
, projects the result on
x
and then multiplies this result with
\varphi_{x}
(and
\varphi_{\emptyset}
). Note that here we also rely on the fact that
\exists y.\varphi_{x,y}\wedge x=y
can be expressed in
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
since
\varphi_{x,y}\in\mathsf{simple}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
.

Proof of Lemma
46

Proof D.2
.

For the proof, assume w.l.o.g. that
z=y
. Let
\psi=\exists x.\varphi\wedge x=y
. Then for all
\nu\colon\{z\}
and
db
we have
\llceil{\psi}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(\nu)=\llceil{\varphi\wedge x=y}\rrfloor_{\textit{db}}(x\mapsto\nu(z),y\mapsto\nu(z))
. It hence suffices to show that we can express
\psi(z)
in
\mathsf{h}\text{-}\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
. The result is then implied by Lemma
45
.

Appendix E
Related Work in Detail

E.1.
On treewidth and finite-variable logics

Geerts and Reutter
[
GR22
]
introduce a tensor logic
\mathsf{TL}
over binary relations and show that conjunctive expressions in this language that have treewidth
k
can be expressed in
\mathsf{TL}_{k+1}
, the
k
-variable fragment of
\mathsf{TL}
. In essence,
\mathsf{TL}
is a form of
\mathsf{FO}^{+}
evaluated over
K
-relations.

Geerts and Reutter
[
GR22
]
do take free variables into account when defining treewidth. Specifically, a formula with
\ell
free variables has treewidth at least
\ell
. Therefore, the treewidth of the following free-connex conjunctive query,

\varphi(x,y)=\exists z,u,v.R(x,y),R(x,z),R(z,u),R(z,v)

has treewidth at least
2
. For this query, therefore, the result by Geerts and Reutter only implies expressibility in
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{3}
, not
\mathsf{FO}^{\wedge}_{2}
as we show in this paper.
